01. Introduction

Source: 01 - Introduction(2).docx
Marketing is not only advertising
It is creating + telling + delivering value.
Marketing කියන්නේ ad එක විතරක් නෙමෙයි; value හදලා customerට දෙන whole process එක.
4 old-to-new ideas
Production → Product → Selling → Marketing.
make cheap → make best → push sell → understand customer first.
STP
Split → Pick → Place in mind.
Segment → Target → Position.
Value / Satisfaction / Retention
Worth it → expectation met → customer comes back.
වටිනවා → සතුටුයි → ආයෙ එනවා.
Decision model
Input → Process → Output.
info එනවා → හිතලා decide කරනවා → buy/use/result.

Learning Objectives

What you need to learn and understand by the end of this lecture.

Objective 1.1

Understand the evolution of marketing and what consumer behavior is

Understand the EVOLUTION of the marketing concept — how marketing ideas changed over time.

Understand WHAT consumer behavior is — the study of how people choose and buy products.

Understand the COMPONENTS of strategic marketing (segmentation, targeting, positioning, 4Ps).

Objective 1.2

Understand how technology has changed marketing

Understand how TECHNOLOGY has benefited both MARKETERS (to reach customers better) and CONSUMERS (to make smarter choices).

Objective 1.3

Understand value, satisfaction, and loyalty

Understand how providing VALUE and SATISFACTION helps retain customers long-term.

Understand how technology ENHANCES customer LOYALTY and RETENTION.

Objective 1.4

Understand ethics in marketing

Understand marketers' SOCIAL and ETHICAL RESPONSIBILITIES — doing business the right way.

Objective 1.6

Career application

Understand how knowledge of consumer behavior helps you FIND A JOB after graduation.

📌 EXAMPLE Think of these objectives as a checklist — by the end of the lecture, you should be able to explain each one clearly in an exam.

SLIDE 3 — Marketing — Definition
The big picture activity that connects businesses to customers and society.
සිංහල idea
Marketing කියන්නේ ad දාන එක විතරක් නෙමෙයි. Customerට වටිනා දෙයක් හදනවා, ඒ ගැන කියනවා, ඒක customerට ලැබෙන්න සලස්වන whole process එක.
•
Creating = customerට ඕන product/service එක හදන එක.
•
Communicating = ඒ ගැන customerට කියන එක.
•
Delivering = shop/online/delivery හරහා customerට දෙන එක.
•
Value = customer දෙන money/time/effort වලට වටින benefit එකක් ලැබෙනවාද කියන එක.
උදාහරණය: Dialog data package එකක් හදලා, ad කරලා, app එකෙන් customerට ලබා දෙන එක.
Exam මතකය: Marketing = Create + Communicate + Deliver Value.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Marketing

The big picture activity that connects businesses to customers and society.

DEFINITION Marketing = the activity, set of institutions, and processes for CREATING, COMMUNICATING, and DELIVERING offerings that have VALUE for customers, clients, partners, and society.

Breaking Down the Definition

Understanding each part of what marketing means

CREATING = making a product or service that people actually want or need.

COMMUNICATING = telling people about the product through ads, social media, word-of-mouth.

DELIVERING = getting the product to the customer through shops, online stores, or delivery.

VALUE = the product must be worth it — worth the money, time, and effort to the customer.

It benefits CUSTOMERS (get what they need), CLIENTS, PARTNERS, and SOCIETY as a whole.

KEY CONCEPT Marketing is NOT just advertising — it covers the full journey from creating to delivering value. A good marketer thinks about ALL parties: customers, partners, and society. Marketing that harms society is unethical — which is why social responsibility matters.
📌 EXAMPLE Dialog Axiata uses marketing to CREATE data packages, COMMUNICATE them via TV ads, and DELIVER them through their app — adding VALUE to millions of Sri Lankan users.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide answers Objective 1.1 — it gives the DEFINITION of marketing itself, the foundation for everything else in this course.
SLIDE 4 — Consumer Behavior — Definition
The study of HOW and WHY people search for, buy, and use products.
සිංහල idea
Consumer Behavior කියන්නේ මිනිස්සු product/service එකක් හොයන්නේ, compare කරන්නේ, buy කරන්නේ, use කරන්නේ කොහොමද සහ ඇයි කියලා බලන එක.
•
Searching = තොරතුරු හොයනවා.
•
Evaluating = options compare කරනවා.
•
Purchasing = buy කරනවා.
•
Using = use කරලා satisfaction බලනවා.
උදාහරණය: Laptop එකක් ගන්න YouTube reviews බලලා Dell/Asus compare කරලා එකක් buy කරන එක.
Exam මතකය: Search → Evaluate → Purchase → Use.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Consumer Behavior

The study of HOW and WHY people search for, buy, and use products.

DEFINITION Consumer Behavior = the study of consumers' CHOICES during searching, evaluating, purchasing, and using products and services that they believe would SATISFY THEIR NEEDS.

The Four Stages of Consumer Choice

Consumer behavior covers the full journey — not just the buying moment

SEARCHING — looking for information about products (Google, YouTube, asking friends).

EVALUATING — comparing options: which brand is better? Which price is fair?

PURCHASING — making the actual decision and paying for the product.

USING — actually using the product and deciding if it was worth it.

KEY CONCEPT Consumer behavior is a STUDY — it uses psychology, sociology, and economics together. Marketers study consumer behavior to design better products and more effective ads. The goal is to understand WHAT consumers need and HOW they decide.
📌 EXAMPLE When a student SEARCHES for the best laptop, COMPARES Asus vs Dell, BUYS an Asus on ikman.lk, and then USES it for lectures — all four stages = consumer behavior.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide defines the CORE SUBJECT of this course. Slide 3 defined marketing; now Slide 4 defines what we actually STUDY within marketing: consumer behavior.
SLIDE 5 — What Can a Car Express About Its Owner?
Products are not just functional — they communicate identity, status, and personality.
සිංහල idea
Car එක transportation එකක් විතරක් නෙමෙයි. Brand/model එකෙන් ownerගේ status, personality, lifestyle සහ self-image එක ගැන signal එකක් දෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Simple English idea: Products are not just functional — they communicate identity, status, and personality.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
What Can a Car Express About Its Owner?

Products are not just functional — they communicate identity, status, and personality.

Porsche Boxster Ad

'Unfulfilled dreams cost a lot more' — aspirational luxury positioning

Porsche markets the Boxster as an EMOTION — not just a car. It sells the FEELING of driving.

Tagline: 'Unfulfilled dreams cost a lot more' — buying this car = fulfilling a dream.

Target consumer: Someone who values LUXURY, PERFORMANCE, and SELF-EXPRESSION.

The car COMMUNICATES: 'I am successful, I have good taste, I appreciate excellence.'

Scion tC Ad

'Take On The Machine' — bold, youthful, rebellious positioning

Scion targets YOUNG consumers who want to feel POWERFUL and UNIQUE.

Tagline: 'Take On The Machine' — aggressive, confident, youthful energy.

The car COMMUNICATES: 'I am bold, I am different, I do not follow the crowd.'

KEY CONCEPT Products act as SYMBOLS — they tell others (and ourselves) something about who we are. Understanding this is KEY to consumer behavior — people buy meaning, not just function. Marketers use this insight to create emotional connections between consumers and products.
📌 EXAMPLE In Sri Lanka, driving a Toyota Prius communicates 'I am eco-conscious and smart.' Driving a Land Cruiser communicates 'I am wealthy and powerful.' Same road, different messages.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide is a REAL-LIFE illustration of consumer behavior (Slide 4) in action. It shows that consumer choices are driven by meaning and identity — not just practical needs.
SLIDE 6 — Marketing Concept
The modern business philosophy: start with what the consumer NEEDS, not what you want to sell.
සිංහල idea
මුලින් product එක හදලා පස්සේ sell කරන්න try කරන එක නෙමෙයි. මුලින් customerට මොනවද ඕනේ කියලා හොයලා, ඒ need එක satisfy වෙන product එක හදන එක.
•
Customer need first.
•
Value create කරන්න.
•
Customer satisfy කරන්න.
•
Long-term customer retention බලන්න.
උදාහරණය: Customerලා low-sugar drink එකක් ඕනේ කියලා research කරලා ඒකට product එකක් හදන එක.
Exam මතකය: Need first → product after.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Marketing Concept

The modern business philosophy: start with what the consumer NEEDS, not what you want to sell.

DEFINITION Marketing Concept = the premise that marketing consists of SATISFYING CONSUMERS' NEEDS, creating VALUE, and RETAINING CUSTOMERS. Companies must produce only goods they have ALREADY DETERMINED would satisfy consumer needs and meet organisational goals.

Key Idea

The marketing concept flips the traditional approach

OLD way: Make a product → then try to sell it to people.
NEW (Marketing Concept) way: Research what people NEED → then make that product.

The product only gets made AFTER confirming customers will want it.

It must satisfy BOTH the customer's needs AND the company's business goals.

Real Example: Classico Pasta Sauce

How a brand successfully applied the marketing concept

Classico's tagline: 'We made it like you'd make it' — they researched how home cooks make pasta sauce.

They recreated that EXACT homemade taste — giving consumers what they already wanted.

Result: Consumers felt the product was made FOR THEM — not pushed on them.

⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Marketing Concept = satisfy needs + create value + retain customers. Companies produce what consumers ALREADY want — not the other way around.
📌 EXAMPLE Keells Fresh supermarket uses the marketing concept — they research what Sri Lankan families need weekly (fresh produce, local brands) and stock EXACTLY that, not random items.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces the MARKETING CONCEPT — the most important philosophy in marketing. It directly addresses Objective 1.1: the evolution and meaning of marketing.
SLIDE 7 — Evolution of Marketing Concept
How business thinking changed over four historical stages.
සිංහල idea
Marketing කියන්නේ ad දාන එක විතරක් නෙමෙයි. Customerට වටිනා දෙයක් හදනවා, ඒ ගැන කියනවා, ඒක customerට ලැබෙන්න සලස්වන whole process එක.
•
Creating = customerට ඕන product/service එක හදන එක.
•
Communicating = ඒ ගැන customerට කියන එක.
•
Delivering = shop/online/delivery හරහා customerට දෙන එක.
•
Value = customer දෙන money/time/effort වලට වටින benefit එකක් ලැබෙනවාද කියන එක.
උදාහරණය: Dialog data package එකක් හදලා, ad කරලා, app එකෙන් customerට ලබා දෙන එක.
Exam මතකය: Marketing = Create + Communicate + Deliver Value.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Evolution of the Marketing Concept

How business thinking changed over four historical stages.

The Four Stages in Order

Marketing philosophy evolved step by step over time

Stage 1: PRODUCTION CONCEPT — oldest idea; focus on making products cheaply.

Stage 2: PRODUCT CONCEPT — focus on making the BEST product with most features.

Stage 3: SELLING CONCEPT — focus on aggressively SELLING what you have made.

Stage 4: MARKETING CONCEPT — modern idea; focus on finding and satisfying consumer NEEDS.

KEY CONCEPT Each stage was a response to the LIMITATIONS of the previous stage. The marketing concept is the most advanced — it puts the CONSUMER at the centre. In exams, you may be asked to LIST these four in ORDER — remember: Production → Product → Selling → Marketing.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Know all four concepts in ORDER and what each one focuses on. This is a very common exam question.
📌 EXAMPLE Think of it like cooking: Production = cook fast and cheap. Product = cook the most gourmet dish. Selling = convince people to eat what you already cooked. Marketing = ask what people want to eat FIRST.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide is an OVERVIEW of the four stages. Slide 6 introduced the Marketing Concept. Now we see WHERE it came from — and what came before it.
SLIDE 8 — Production Concept and Product Concept
The first two stages of marketing evolution — focused on production and product quality.
සිංහල idea
මිනිස්සු product එකක් function එකට විතරක් ගන්නේ නෑ. ඒකෙන් තමන් ගැන අනිත් අයට message එකක් දෙන්නත් ගන්නවා.
•
Luxury product = status/success කියන signal එකක් වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Sporty brand = active/bold personality එකක් පෙන්නන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Eco product = environmentally responsible image එකක් දෙන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Land Cruiser එකක් wealth/power signal එකක් වගේ පේන්න පුළුවන්.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Production Concept and Product Concept

The first two stages of marketing evolution — focused on production and product quality.

Production Concept

The oldest marketing idea — focus on availability and low price

Consumers are mostly interested in PRODUCT AVAILABILITY at LOW PRICES.

Implicit marketing objectives: CHEAP and EFFICIENT production + INTENSIVE distribution.

The company's goal = produce as much as possible, as cheaply as possible.

Works well when DEMAND exceeds SUPPLY — people just want the product to exist and be affordable.

Product Concept

The second stage — focus on making the best product

Assumes consumers will buy the product offering the HIGHEST QUALITY, BEST PERFORMANCE, and MOST FEATURES.

The company invests in engineering and innovation to make the 'perfect' product.

Danger: companies can become obsessed with the product and forget about CONSUMER NEEDS.

📌 EXAMPLE Production Concept: Manufactured goods in a Sri Lankan factory focus on producing maximum units cheaply for the local market. Product Concept: Samsung packs its latest Galaxy with every possible feature — assuming 'more features = more buyers.'
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide EXPANDS on two of the four stages introduced in Slide 7. It gives the details behind Production and Product concepts.
SLIDE 9 — Marketing Myopia
The dangerous mistake of focusing on your product instead of the customer's need.
සිංහල idea
Company එක තමන්ගේ product එකටම බලලා customerට ඇත්තටම ඕන need එක අමතක කරන එක.
•
Product එකට love වෙලා customer problem එක අමතක වෙනවා.
•
Technology change උනාම company එක adapt වෙන්න බැරි වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Business එක product name එකෙන් නෙමෙයි customer need එකෙන් define කරන්න.
උදාහරණය: DVD shop එක 'DVD rent කරන business' කියලා හිතුවොත් fail වෙන්න පුළුවන්; 'entertainment දෙන business' කියලා හිතුවොත් online streamingට adapt වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Product එක නෙමෙයි — need එක බලන්න.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Marketing Myopia

The dangerous mistake of focusing on your product instead of the customer's need.

DEFINITION Marketing Myopia = a SHORT-SIGHTED approach where companies focus on the PRODUCT rather than on the NEEDS the product is meant to satisfy. Companies 'look in the mirror instead of out the window.'

What Does 'Look in the Mirror' Mean?

A vivid way to explain the myopia mistake

'Looking in the mirror' = the company only sees ITSELF — its own product, its own factory, its own goals.

'Looking out the window' = seeing the CUSTOMER — their needs, their lives, their problems.

Managers who focus on the PRODUCT forget about the NEED it is designed to fulfil.

Why This Is Dangerous

Companies that suffer from marketing myopia eventually fail

If you define your business by the PRODUCT, a better product from a competitor will destroy you.

If you define your business by the NEED you fulfill, you can adapt as technology changes.

Famous case: Kodak made the BEST film cameras — but failed to see customers needed PHOTOS (not film). Digital cameras destroyed them.

KEY CONCEPT Define your business by the CUSTOMER'S NEED — not by your product. A railway company should think 'We are in the TRANSPORTATION business' — not 'We sell train tickets.' This mindset allows adaptation when technology or customer habits change.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Marketing Myopia = focusing on the PRODUCT rather than the NEED. Coined by Theodore Levitt. Classic example: Kodak (film) vs digital cameras.
📌 EXAMPLE A Sri Lankan video rental shop focused on 'renting DVDs' (product myopia) instead of 'providing entertainment' (customer need) — then Netflix arrived and the DVD shop closed.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide is a CRITICAL WARNING connected to the Product Concept (Slide 8). It shows what happens when companies love their product TOO MUCH and forget the consumer.
SLIDE 10 — Selling Concept
The third stage of marketing — push products hard even if customers did not ask for them.
සිංහල idea
මුලින් product එක හදලා පස්සේ sell කරන්න try කරන එක නෙමෙයි. මුලින් customerට මොනවද ඕනේ කියලා හොයලා, ඒ need එක satisfy වෙන product එක හදන එක.
•
Customer need first.
•
Value create කරන්න.
•
Customer satisfy කරන්න.
•
Long-term customer retention බලන්න.
උදාහරණය: Customerලා low-sugar drink එකක් ඕනේ කියලා research කරලා ඒකට product එකක් හදන එක.
Exam මතකය: Need first → product after.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Selling Concept

The third stage of marketing — push products hard even if customers did not ask for them.

DEFINITION Selling Concept = maintains that marketers' primary focus is SELLING the products that THEY HAVE DECIDED TO PRODUCE — not what the customer necessarily wants.

How It Works

The selling concept is product-first, consumer-second

The company first MAKES the product (no market research).

Then they spend heavily on ADVERTISING and SALESPEOPLE to convince people to buy it.

The attitude: 'We made it — now let's sell it aggressively.'

Works for UNSOUGHT GOODS — things people don't think to buy (insurance, funeral plans, encyclopedias).

Weakness: creates CUSTOMER DISSATISFACTION — customers feel pressured, not served.

KEY CONCEPT Selling Concept = push strategy. Marketing Concept = pull strategy. Selling concept can generate SHORT-TERM sales but fails at LONG-TERM loyalty. Modern marketing has largely moved away from the selling concept.
📌 EXAMPLE An insurance salesperson who calls repeatedly, uses pressure tactics, and convinces you to buy a policy you didn't originally want — that is the selling concept at work.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide covers the THIRD stage of evolution. After production (make cheap) and product (make best), the selling concept says: 'make whatever, then push it hard.' The marketing concept (Slide 6) was created to fix this.
SLIDE 11 — Marketing Concept Requirements
The tools and strategies needed to APPLY the marketing concept successfully.
සිංහල idea
Marketing Concept apply කරන්න STP + 4Ps දෙකම ඕන. STP = Segment → Target → Position. 4Ps = Product, Price, Place, Promotion. මුලින් කාට serve කරනවද decide කරලා, පස්සේ offer එක හදනවා.
Simple English idea: The tools and strategies needed to APPLY the marketing concept successfully.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Marketing Concept Requirements

The tools and strategies needed to APPLY the marketing concept successfully.

Three Strategic Requirements

Before marketing can begin, these three must be done

•
MARKET SEGMENTATION — divide the total market into smaller groups with similar needs.
•
TARGETING — choose WHICH group(s) to focus your marketing efforts on.
•
POSITIONING — create a clear, unique image of your brand in that target's mind.

The Marketing Mix — 4 Ps

The four tactical tools used to reach and serve the target market

PRODUCT or SERVICE — what you are selling; its features, quality, design.

PRICE — how much you charge; must reflect value and be competitive.

PLACE — where and how the customer can get the product (distribution channels).

PROMOTION — how you communicate with customers (ads, social media, PR).

KEY CONCEPT STP (Segmentation, Targeting, Positioning) + 4Ps = the complete marketing toolkit. Without segmentation, you try to please everyone — which pleases no one. The 4Ps must be aligned with the needs of the chosen target segment.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Know STP + 4Ps for any marketing question. STP = strategic decisions. 4Ps = tactical decisions. Both are required to successfully apply the marketing concept.
📌 EXAMPLE Apple: Segment = premium tech users. Target = young professionals and creatives. Position = 'the most elegant, intuitive technology.' Product = iPhone. Price = premium. Place = Apple Stores + online. Promotion = minimalist, aspirational ads.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide answers HOW to actually implement the Marketing Concept (Slide 6). STP tells you WHO to serve. The 4Ps tell you HOW to serve them.
SLIDE 12 — Segmentation, Targeting, Positioning — Definitions
Detailed definitions of the three core strategic marketing tools.
සිංහල idea
Marketing කියන්නේ ad දාන එක විතරක් නෙමෙයි. Customerට වටිනා දෙයක් හදනවා, ඒ ගැන කියනවා, ඒක customerට ලැබෙන්න සලස්වන whole process එක.
•
Creating = customerට ඕන product/service එක හදන එක.
•
Communicating = ඒ ගැන customerට කියන එක.
•
Delivering = shop/online/delivery හරහා customerට දෙන එක.
•
Value = customer දෙන money/time/effort වලට වටින benefit එකක් ලැබෙනවාද කියන එක.
උදාහරණය: Dialog data package එකක් හදලා, ad කරලා, app එකෙන් customerට ලබා දෙන එක.
Exam මතකය: Marketing = Create + Communicate + Deliver Value.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Market Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning

Detailed definitions of the three core strategic marketing tools.

DEFINITION Market Segmentation = the process of DIVIDING A MARKET into subsets of consumers with COMMON NEEDS or CHARACTERISTICS.

Market Segmentation

Splitting the total market into smaller, similar groups

Not all customers are the same — segmentation groups them by shared needs, age, income, lifestyle, location.

Segmentation bases: Geographic, Demographic, Psychographic, Behavioural.

Result: each segment can be targeted with a customised marketing approach.

DEFINITION Targeting = SELECTING the segments that the company views as PROSPECTIVE CUSTOMERS and PURSUING them with marketing efforts.

Targeting

Choosing which segment(s) to serve

After segmenting, the company evaluates each segment's SIZE, GROWTH, and PROFITABILITY.

It then selects the BEST-FIT segment(s) — those it can serve well and profitably.

Targeting can be: undifferentiated (everyone), differentiated (multiple segments), or concentrated (one niche).

DEFINITION Positioning = the process by which a company creates a DISTINCT IMAGE and IDENTITY for its products, services, and brands in CONSUMERS' MINDS.

Positioning

Making your brand stand out and mean something specific in the customer's mind

Positioning answers: 'Why should THIS customer choose THIS product over ALL alternatives?'

A clear position = a clear reason to buy. A vague position = ignored in the marketplace.

Positioning is communicated through the product's name, packaging, price, ads, and store placement.

📌 EXAMPLE SriLankan Airlines: Segment = business/premium travellers. Target = corporate clients + diaspora. Position = 'The airline of Sri Lanka — warmth, hospitality, and pride.' Every touchpoint reinforces this position.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide gives FULL DEFINITIONS of the three STP terms introduced in Slide 11. Slide 11 listed them; Slide 12 explains what each one means in detail.
SLIDE 13 — Consumers Have Embraced Technology
How different age groups use digital devices — and what this means for marketers.
සිංහල idea
Consumer Behavior කියන්නේ මිනිස්සු product/service එකක් හොයන්නේ, compare කරන්නේ, buy කරන්නේ, use කරන්නේ කොහොමද සහ ඇයි කියලා බලන එක.
•
Searching = තොරතුරු හොයනවා.
•
Evaluating = options compare කරනවා.
•
Purchasing = buy කරනවා.
•
Using = use කරලා satisfaction බලනවා.
උදාහරණය: Laptop එකක් ගන්න YouTube reviews බලලා Dell/Asus compare කරලා එකක් buy කරන එක.
Exam මතකය: Search → Evaluate → Purchase → Use.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Consumers Have Embraced Technology

How different age groups use digital devices — and what this means for marketers.

What the Chart Shows

Technology adoption rates across different generations

The chart compares technology ownership across 7 age groups — from Millennials (18–34) to Over 75 years old.

Devices measured: iPad-like Tablet, E-Book Reader, iPod/MP3 Players, Laptop, Desktop Computer, Cell Phone.

CELL PHONE is the most widely owned device across ALL age groups — even the elderly.

MILLENNIALS (18–34) have the highest technology adoption across nearly all devices.

Even OVER 75-year-olds own cell phones — showing that technology is no longer just for the young.

Marketing Implications

What this data means for how marketers must behave

Marketers MUST use digital channels to reach consumers — ignoring digital = missing most customers.

Different age groups prefer different devices — strategy must be TAILORED by segment.

The rise of smartphones means marketers can reach consumers ANYTIME, ANYWHERE.

Mobile marketing (SMS, app ads, social media) is now essential — not optional.

KEY CONCEPT Technology adoption = consumers are now MORE INFORMED and MORE CONNECTED than ever. Marketers benefit: cheaper, faster, more targeted advertising via digital platforms. Consumers benefit: easier price comparison, reviews, and access to products worldwide.
📌 EXAMPLE In Sri Lanka, even three-wheel (tuk-tuk) drivers use smartphones to accept PickMe bookings — proving that technology has reached ALL socioeconomic levels.

SLIDE 14 — Customer Value
The first element of building lasting customer relationships: delivering real value.
සිංහල idea
Customerට ලැබෙන benefits සහ ඒකට දෙන money/time/effort/risk compare කරලා 'මේක වටිනවද?' කියන feeling එක.
•
Benefits වැඩි, cost අඩු නම් value වැඩි.
•
Value subjective — දෙන්නෙක්ට same product එකේ value වෙනස් වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Value relative — competitor එක්ක compare වෙනවා.
උදාහරණය: Free delivery + good service තියෙන online shop එකක් ටිකක් price වැඩි උනත් value වැඩි කියලා customer හිතන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Value = Perceived Benefits ÷ Resources Used.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Successful Relationships — Customer Value

The first element of building lasting customer relationships: delivering real value.

DEFINITION Customer Value = the RATIO between the customer's PERCEIVED BENEFITS and the RESOURCES used to obtain those benefits. Value = Benefits ÷ Cost (money, time, effort, risk).

Key Characteristics of Customer Value

Understanding how value is measured in the customer's mind

Perceived value is RELATIVE — compared to alternatives in the market.

Perceived value is SUBJECTIVE — different customers perceive the same product's value differently.

Benefits include: functional (what it does), social (status it gives), emotional (how it makes you feel).

Resources include: money, time, effort, and psychological risk.

DEVELOPING A VALUE PROPOSITION is critical — a clear statement of why your product is worth it.

KEY CONCEPT Value Proposition = a promise of value to be delivered. It answers 'Why should I choose you?' High perceived value = customer feels they got MORE than they paid for. If perceived benefits < cost → customer feels cheated → no loyalty.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Value = Perceived Benefits ÷ Resources Used. It is PERCEIVED (in the customer's mind) and RELATIVE (compared to alternatives). These two properties are exam favourites.
📌 EXAMPLE Amazon Prime: Sri Lankans living abroad pay a subscription fee (resource). Benefits received: free delivery, Prime Video, exclusive deals. If benefits outweigh the cost → high perceived value → customer renews.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide begins a new section: Successful Relationships (Slides 14–19). After defining marketing tools, we now focus on HOW to keep customers happy and loyal. Customer Value is the foundation.
SLIDE 15 — Customer Satisfaction
The second element: how well the product meets the customer's expectations.
සිංහල idea
Product performance එක customer expect කරපු level එකට compare කරන එක.
•
Performance > Expectation = delighted.
•
Performance = Expectation = satisfied.
•
Performance < Expectation = dissatisfied.
උදාහරණය: 45 min delivery කියලා කියලා 20 min වල food එක හොඳට ආවොත් delight.
Exam මතකය: Performance vs Expectation.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Successful Relationships — Customer Satisfaction

The second element: how well the product meets the customer's expectations.

DEFINITION Customer Satisfaction = the individual's perception of the PERFORMANCE of the product or service in relation to his or her EXPECTATIONS. Satisfaction = Performance vs. Expectations.

The Satisfaction Formula

Simple but powerful: what you get vs. what you expected

Performance > Expectations → DELIGHT (very happy customer — potential loyalist or apostle).
Performance = Expectations → SATISFACTION (happy enough — but not emotionally engaged).
Performance < Expectations → DISSATISFACTION (unhappy — potential defector or terrorist).

Customer Loyalty Groups

Six types of customers based on their level of satisfaction and loyalty

LOYALISTS — very satisfied; keep buying; recommend to others.

APOSTLES — extremely delighted; actively PROMOTE the brand to everyone (best customers!).

DEFECTORS — satisfied enough but not loyal; switch when a better option appears.

TERRORISTS — very dissatisfied; actively DAMAGE the brand by complaining publicly.

HOSTAGES — unhappy but cannot leave (e.g., stuck with one internet provider in their area).

MERCENARIES — satisfied but disloyal; switch constantly based on price and deals.

⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Know all 6 customer loyalty groups and what defines each one. Loyalists and Apostles = best customers. Terrorists = most dangerous. This is a very common exam question.
📌 EXAMPLE A customer orders from a Colombo restaurant and the food arrives 20 min early and tastes amazing. Expectation: 45 min wait, average taste. Performance exceeded → DELIGHT → they become an Apostle and post on Instagram.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide builds on Slide 14 (Customer Value). Value is about benefits vs. cost. Satisfaction is about performance vs. expectations. Together they determine whether customers stay or leave.
SLIDE 16 — Customer Retention
The third element: keeping satisfied customers coming back again and again.
සිංහල idea
Satisfied customer එක ආයෙත් ආයෙත් brand එකෙන් buy කරන තත්වයට ගෙන යන එක.
•
Loyal customer buy more.
•
Price ගැන අඩු sensitivity.
•
Service cost අඩු වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Positive word-of-mouth දෙන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Loyalty points දීලා customer ආයෙත් shop එකට එන්න motivate කරන එක.
Exam මතකය: Value → Satisfaction → Retention.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Successful Relationships — Customer Retention

The third element: keeping satisfied customers coming back again and again.

DEFINITION Customer Retention = the strategy of keeping HIGHLY SATISFIED CUSTOMERS coming back. Loyal customers are the most valuable long-term asset a company has.

Why Loyal Customers Are So Valuable

Four powerful reasons to invest in retaining existing customers

•
They BUY MORE PRODUCTS — loyal customers expand their purchases over time.
•
They are LESS PRICE SENSITIVE — they care more about the brand than the price.
•
SERVICING THEM IS CHEAPER — they know the product, need less help, fewer complaints.
•
They SPREAD POSITIVE WORD OF MOUTH — they recommend the brand to friends and family for free.
KEY CONCEPT Acquiring a NEW customer costs 5–7x more than RETAINING an existing one. A 5% increase in retention can increase profits by 25–95% (according to Harvard research). Customer retention is the ultimate goal of providing value and satisfaction.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Know the FOUR benefits of loyal customers: buy more, less price sensitive, cheaper to service, positive word of mouth. These four are frequently tested.
📌 EXAMPLE Keells 1 Rewards Card loyalty program gives points for every purchase — encouraging customers to RETURN. Loyal Keells customers buy more products, ignore competitor prices, and recommend Keells to family.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide completes the VALUE → SATISFACTION → RETENTION chain. Value (Slide 14) creates satisfaction (Slide 15) which leads to retention (this slide). All three are interconnected.
SLIDE 17 — Forms of Engagement — Emotional and Transactional Bonds
The two types of bonds that connect consumers to brands and keep them coming back.
සිංහල idea
Customer brand එකට බැඳෙන්න දෙවිදිහක් තියෙනවා.
•
Emotional bond = brand එකට personal feeling/attachment.
•
Transactional bond = points, easy checkout, subscription වගේ practical benefits.
•
Strong loyalty එකකට දෙකම useful.
උදාහරණය: Apple brand feeling + ecosystem convenience දෙකම customer hold කරගන්නවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Forms of Engagement

The two types of bonds that connect consumers to brands and keep them coming back.

Emotional Bonds

Deep connections based on personal feelings and attachment

Emotional bonds = PERSONAL COMMITMENT and emotional ATTACHMENT to a brand.

Social media is a key tool used to get consumers to EMOTIONALLY ENGAGE with products and brands.

Emotional bonds are the STRONGEST type — they make switching to a competitor feel like a personal loss.

Brands build emotional bonds through: storytelling, community, shared values, and personal experiences.

Transactional Bonds

Practical structures that make it easy and rewarding to keep buying

Transactional bonds = the MECHANICS and STRUCTURES that facilitate exchanges between consumers and sellers.

Factors like ASSORTMENT (wide product variety) and TRANSACTION EASE (simple to buy) shape the relationship.

Examples: loyalty points, subscription models, fast checkout, easy returns.

Transactional bonds are WEAKER than emotional bonds — a better deal elsewhere can break them.

KEY CONCEPT The strongest customer relationships combine BOTH emotional AND transactional bonds. Emotional = they love the brand. Transactional = it is easy and rewarding to keep buying. Social media has become the primary tool for building emotional bonds at scale.
📌 EXAMPLE Apple creates EMOTIONAL bonds through design aesthetics, brand community, and identity. They also create TRANSACTIONAL bonds through the App Store, Apple Pay, and iCloud subscriptions — making it costly to switch.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide expands on HOW retention is built (from Slide 16). Two mechanisms: Emotional Bonds (deep loyalty) and Transactional Bonds (practical loyalty). Together they build lasting relationships.
SLIDE 18 — Determinants of Site Satisfaction
The key factors that determine whether a customer is satisfied with a website or online platform.
සිංහල idea
Product performance එක customer expect කරපු level එකට compare කරන එක.
•
Performance > Expectation = delighted.
•
Performance = Expectation = satisfied.
•
Performance < Expectation = dissatisfied.
උදාහරණය: 45 min delivery කියලා කියලා 20 min වල food එක හොඳට ආවොත් delight.
Exam මතකය: Performance vs Expectation.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Determinants of Site Satisfaction

The key factors that determine whether a customer is satisfied with a website or online platform.

The 11 Determinants

What makes an online customer feel satisfied and keep returning

ADAPTATION — the site personalises content to the individual user's needs and preferences.

INTERACTIVITY — users can interact with the site (search, filter, chat, customise).

NURTURING — the site builds a relationship (follow-ups, personalised emails, recommendations).

COMMITMENT — the site shows dedication to the customer's long-term satisfaction.

NETWORK — the site connects customers to a community of other users.

ASSORTMENT — wide variety of products/options available to choose from.

TRANSACTION EASE — simple, fast, and secure buying process.

ENGAGEMENT — the site is interesting and keeps the user involved (reviews, content, videos).

LOYALTY — mechanisms that reward repeat visits and purchases (points, exclusive deals).

INERTIA — user keeps coming back simply because switching is too much effort.

TRUST — the user believes the site is honest, secure, and will deliver as promised.

⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Know all 11 determinants. They can appear as a list question or be tested individually with examples. TRUST and TRANSACTION EASE are the most commonly tested.
📌 EXAMPLE Amazon satisfies all 11: it adapts recommendations (Adaptation), lets you filter and review (Interactivity), emails follow-ups (Nurturing), offers Prime loyalty (Loyalty), and is trusted worldwide (Trust).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide applies the engagement and retention concepts (Slides 16–17) specifically to ONLINE/DIGITAL PLATFORMS. In the digital age, these 11 factors determine online customer loyalty.
SLIDE 19 — Measures of Customer Retention
How companies MEASURE whether they are successfully keeping their customers.
සිංහල idea
Satisfied customer එක ආයෙත් ආයෙත් brand එකෙන් buy කරන තත්වයට ගෙන යන එක.
•
Loyal customer buy more.
•
Price ගැන අඩු sensitivity.
•
Service cost අඩු වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Positive word-of-mouth දෙන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Loyalty points දීලා customer ආයෙත් shop එකට එන්න motivate කරන එක.
Exam මතකය: Value → Satisfaction → Retention.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Measures of Customer Retention

How companies MEASURE whether they are successfully keeping their customers.

Three Key Measurement Methods

Tools to track and analyse customer retention performance

•
CUSTOMER VALUATION — value customers and categorise them according to their FINANCIAL and STRATEGIC WORTH.

→ Identifies which customers are most profitable and which are at risk of leaving.

•
RETENTION RATES — the PERCENTAGE of customers at the beginning of the year who are STILL CUSTOMERS by the end of the year.

→ Formula: Customers at end of year ÷ Customers at start of year × 100.

→ A high retention rate = strong customer loyalty and satisfaction.

•
ANALYSING DEFECTIONS — probing for details when talking to FORMER CUSTOMERS; analysing customer COMPLAINTS; benchmarking against COMPETITORS' defection rates.

→ Understanding WHY customers leave helps fix problems and prevent future defections.

KEY CONCEPT What gets measured gets managed — you cannot improve retention if you do not measure it. Retention Rate is the most commonly used metric to track customer loyalty over time. Analysing defections is the MOST INSIGHTFUL measure — former customers tell you the truth.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Know all 3 measures: Customer Valuation, Retention Rates, and Analysing Defections. Be able to define each one and explain what it measures.
📌 EXAMPLE Dialog Axiata calculates its monthly retention rate and sends a special offer to customers who have not recharged in 30 days — this is both measuring retention AND acting to prevent defection.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide provides the measurement tools for the retention strategy discussed in Slides 16–18. Theory without measurement is incomplete — this slide closes the loop.
SLIDE 20 — Consumer Decision Making Model
The complete framework showing how consumers make buying decisions — from inputs to outputs.
සිංහල idea
Consumer decision එක main stages 3කින් බලන්න: INPUT → PROCESS → OUTPUT. Input = marketing + social info. Process = need recognition, search, compare, psychological factors. Output = purchase, post-purchase feeling, repurchase/loyalty හෝ no purchase.
Simple English idea: The complete framework showing how consumers make buying decisions — from inputs to outputs.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Consumer Decision Making Model

The complete framework showing how consumers make buying decisions — from inputs to outputs.

The Three Stages: Input → Process → Output

A structured model to understand the complete consumer journey

INPUT — the information and influences that ENTER the consumer's world before a decision.

PROCESS — what happens INSIDE the consumer's mind as they think through the decision.

OUTPUT — the RESULT of the decision process: purchase or no purchase, and what happens after.

INPUT Stage

Two types of inputs that influence consumers

Firm Marketing Efforts: Product, Price, Place (distribution), Promotion — the 4Ps.

Sociocultural Influences: Reference groups, family, social class, culture and subculture.

Communication Sources: Advertising, buzz agents, social media (owned/paid) AND word-of-mouth, recommendations, self-generated social media content.

PROCESS Stage

The psychological journey inside the consumer's mind

Psychological Factors: Needs and motivation, personality traits, perception, attitudes.

Decision Steps: Need Recognition → Type of Decision → Pre-Purchase Information Search → Evaluation of Alternatives.

Learning: knowledge and experience from PAST decisions feed back into future choices.

OUTPUT Stage

What happens after the decision is made

PURCHASE → Post-Purchase Evaluation → Re-Purchase → Trust and Loyalty (positive cycle).
NO PURCHASE or NO RE-PURCHASE → consumer leaves without buying or does not return.
StageFirm / Marketer SideConsumer / Social Side
INPUTMarketing Mix (4Ps): Product, Promotion, Price, Distribution Communication: Advertising, Social Media, Buzz AgentsSociocultural: Reference Groups, Family, Social Class, Culture Word-of-Mouth: Advice, Recommendations, Self-Generated Content
PROCESSSteps: Need Recognition → Type of Decision → Pre-Purchase Search → Evaluation of AlternativesPsychological: Needs, Motivation, Personality, Perception, Attitudes + Learning from Knowledge & Experience
OUTPUTPURCHASE → Post-Purchase Evaluation → Re-Purchase → Trust & LoyaltyNO PURCHASE or NO RE-PURCHASE if experience is negative
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: The Consumer Decision Making Model has 3 stages: INPUT → PROCESS → OUTPUT. Know what belongs in each stage. Drawing or labelling this diagram is a very common exam task.
📌 EXAMPLE A student wants a new phone (Need Recognition = INPUT). Researches online, asks friends (Pre-Purchase Search = PROCESS). Buys Samsung (PURCHASE = OUTPUT). Loves it → buys Samsung laptop next year (Re-Purchase → Loyalty).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This is the GRAND SUMMARY SLIDE of the entire course introduction. It ties together everything: marketing inputs (Slides 3–12), value/retention (Slides 14–19), and consumer psychology — all in one unified model.
SLIDE 21 — Consumer Decision Making — Diagram
The complete visual flowchart showing all three stages and how they connect.
සිංහල idea
Consumer decision එක main stages 3කින් බලන්න: INPUT → PROCESS → OUTPUT. Input = marketing + social info. Process = need recognition, search, compare, psychological factors. Output = purchase, post-purchase feeling, repurchase/loyalty හෝ no purchase.
Simple English idea: The complete visual flowchart showing all three stages and how they connect.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Consumer Decision Making Model — Visual Diagram

The complete visual flowchart showing all three stages and how they connect.

Reading the Diagram

How to interpret the flowchart from top to bottom

TOP SECTION (INPUT): Two blue boxes = Marketing Mix + Sociocultural Influences. Two green boxes = Communication Sources (company-generated AND consumer-generated).

MIDDLE SECTION (PROCESS): Left box = decision steps (Need Recognition through Evaluation). Right boxes = Psychological Influences and Learning feeding into the process.

BOTTOM SECTION (OUTPUT): PURCHASE leads to Post-Purchase Evaluation → Re-Purchase → Trust and Loyalty. NO PURCHASE is also shown as a possible outcome.

LEARNING feeds BACKWARDS — past experience and knowledge loop back to influence future decisions.

The diagram shows that consumer decision making is NOT a straight line — it is an INTERCONNECTED SYSTEM.

KEY CONCEPT This diagram is the central framework of the entire Consumer Behavior course. Every future topic (motivation, perception, attitudes, social influence) fits into this model. Learn to draw and label this diagram — it will appear in exams.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Be able to reproduce this diagram from memory, label all boxes correctly, and explain what belongs in each section. This model is the foundation of the entire subject.
📌 EXAMPLE A person sees a McDonald's ad (INPUT: Marketing Mix). Feels hungry (Need Recognition). Checks if it's nearby (Pre-Purchase Search). Decides to go (Evaluation). Buys a McSpicy (Purchase). Loves it (Post-Purchase Satisfaction). Returns next week (Re-Purchase → Loyalty).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide is the VISUAL VERSION of Slide 20. Slide 20 listed the components in text. Slide 21 shows the full interconnected diagram. Together they give you a complete understanding of the model.

02. Consumer Perception

Perception
Same thing can mean different things to different people.
එකම product එක දෙන්නෙක්ට දෙවිදිහට පේන්න පුළුවන්.
5 senses
Sight, sound, smell, taste, touch can all sell.
ඇස කන නාසය දිව සම marketing tools.
Threshold trio
Absolute = notice exists; JND = notice a change; Subliminal = below conscious awareness.
මේ තුන mix කරන්න එපා.
Selective trio
Exposure = choose/skip; Attention = it is there but you focus only on useful; Defense = mind blocks uncomfortable info.
skip / focus / brain block.
Gestalt
Figure-ground + grouping + closure.
main/background + group + missing part fill.
Halo
One strong feature affects the whole judgement.

SLIDE 2 — What is Perception?
A fun question — do two people always see the same thing?

Perception කියන්නේ environment එකෙන් ලැබෙන information brain එක select කරලා, arrange කරලා, meaning එකක් දෙන process එක.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
What is Perception?

A fun question — do two people always see the same thing?

Optical Illusions

Images that trick your brain — different people see different things

Left image: Some people see a vase. Other people see two faces looking at each other.

Right image: Some people see an old woman. Other people see a young woman looking away.

This shows us — two people can look at the SAME thing and see something DIFFERENT.

That is the big idea of perception: your brain decides what something means.

KEY CONCEPT Perception is NOT just about your eyes — it is about your MIND. Two customers can see the same product and feel very differently about it. This is WHY marketing is hard — you cannot control how a customer THINKS.
📌 EXAMPLE At Keells, one person sees a product as 'cheap and good.' Another person sees the same product as 'low quality.' Same product — different perception!
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide INTRODUCES the topic with a visual question. It prepares your mind before giving the real definition.
SLIDE 3 — Perception — Definition
The process of gathering, understanding, and making meaning from information around us.
සිංහල idea

එකම thing එක දෙන්නෙක්ට වෙනස් විදිහට පේන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Actual product එකට වඩා customerගේ perception එක buying decision එකට වැදගත් වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Keells product එක එක්කෙනෙක්ට 'cheap & good', තව කෙනෙක්ට 'low quality' වගේ පේන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Perception = how the mind sees/understands the world.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Perception

The process of gathering, understanding, and making meaning from information around us.

DEFINITION Perception = the process through which a person gathers, processes, and interprets information from the environment. Simply: 'how we see the world around us.'

Why Perception Matters for Marketers

What the customer perceives about a product directly affects whether they buy it or not

How a consumer PERCEIVES a product = affects their buying DECISION.

If a customer thinks a product looks cheap, they will NOT buy it — even if it is actually high quality.

Consumers perceive brands through digital touchpoints (websites, apps), influencers, and AI recommendations.

📌 EXAMPLE If a Dialog ad feels exciting and modern, you may choose Dialog over Hutch — even if both networks have similar coverage. Perception changed your choice.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide gives the DEFINITION of perception. It answers the question posed by Slide 2 ('What is perception?'). Now we move from question to answer.
SLIDE 4 — Perception at Keells — Real Life Example
A real-life example of how perception begins the moment a customer enters a store.
සිංහල idea
Perception කියන්නේ environment එකෙන් ලැබෙන information brain එක select කරලා, arrange කරලා, meaning එකක් දෙන process එක.

උදාහරණය: Keells product එක එක්කෙනෙක්ට 'cheap & good', තව කෙනෙක්ට 'low quality' වගේ පේන්න පුළුවන්.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Perception Starts Before Buying

A real-life example of how perception begins the moment a customer enters a store.

When a Customer Enters Keells

The brain starts processing information IMMEDIATELY

Bright red discount boards → eyes catch them first → brain says 'SALE! Save money!'
Attractive biscuit packaging → colourful and shiny → brain says 'This looks tasty and premium!'
Smell from bakery section → fresh bread aroma → brain says 'This place is good and fresh!'

All three are SENSORY INPUTS — information entering the mind through the senses.

Perception begins EVEN BEFORE the customer buys anything — just from walking in.

KEY CONCEPT Sensory inputs = information your five senses collect from the environment. Marketers DESIGN stores to create positive sensory inputs on purpose. Every colour, smell, and sound in a store is PLANNED to affect your perception.
📌 EXAMPLE McDonald's uses yellow and red colours + the smell of fries to create instant positive feelings the moment you walk near the store.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide takes the DEFINITION from Slide 3 and shows it with a real example. It makes the theory come alive with the Keells supermarket story.
SLIDE 5 — Elements of Perception — Sensory Inputs
The first building block of perception: information coming in through your senses.
සිංහල idea
Perception කියන්නේ environment එකෙන් ලැබෙන information brain එක select කරලා, arrange කරලා, meaning එකක් දෙන process එක.
•
එකම thing එක දෙන්නෙක්ට වෙනස් විදිහට පේන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Actual product එකට වඩා customerගේ perception එක buying decision එකට වැදගත් වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Keells product එක එක්කෙනෙක්ට 'cheap & good', තව කෙනෙක්ට 'low quality' වගේ පේන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Perception = how the mind sees/understands the world.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Elements of Perception — Sensory Inputs

The first building block of perception: information coming in through your senses.

DEFINITION Sensation = the IMMEDIATE and DIRECT response of the sensory organs to stimuli. It is the first step of perception.

Stimulus

Any piece of information that enters through one of your five senses

A stimulus = any unit of input to any of the senses.

Stimuli include: products, packages, brand names, advertisements, commercials.

When you see a red Coca-Cola can → that is a VISUAL stimulus.
When you hear the McDonald's jingle → that is a SOUND stimulus.
KEY CONCEPT Stimulus → enters your senses → your brain processes it → you PERCEIVE something. Marketers create stimuli ON PURPOSE to guide your perception. Every colour, sound, and smell in an ad is a planned stimulus.
📌 EXAMPLE When you see SriLankan Airlines' blue-and-gold logo on a billboard, that image is a VISUAL stimulus that triggers thoughts of travel and prestige.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide begins the formal list of Elements of Perception. It is the FIRST element: sensory inputs. Slides before showed us WHY perception matters; now we learn HOW it works.
SLIDE 6 — Sensory Receptors
The body parts that receive information from the outside world.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් customerට information එක notice, select, organize, interpret වෙන විදිහ සහ marketer ඒ perception එකට බලපාන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: The body parts that receive information from the outside world.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Sensory Receptors

The body parts that receive information from the outside world.

DEFINITION Sensory receptors = the human organs (eyes, ears, nose, mouth, and skin) that receive sensory inputs and send signals to the brain.

The Five Sensory Organs

Each organ collects a different type of information

Eyes → receive LIGHT → allow us to SEE colours, shapes, and images.
Ears → receive SOUND WAVES → allow us to HEAR music, voices, and noise.
Nose → receive MOLECULES in air → allow us to SMELL fragrances and odours.
Mouth/Tongue → receives chemicals in food → allow us to TASTE sweet, sour, salty, bitter.
Skin → receives pressure and temperature → allow us to TOUCH and feel textures.
Example: Bad Smell Response

How a stimulus creates an immediate sensory response

Stimulus = bad smell from a dustbin.

Sensory organ = nose detects the odour molecules.

Immediate response = person covers their nose automatically.

This is SENSATION — a direct, automatic body response to a stimulus.

📌 EXAMPLE When you walk past a Maliban biscuit factory and smell fresh biscuits baking, your nose (sensory receptor) triggers instant pleasure — a positive sensation.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide continues Slide 5 by explaining the body parts that RECEIVE the stimuli. Now we know: Stimulus → Sensory Receptor → Sensation.
SLIDE 7 — Sensation — Energy Change and Sensitivity
Sensation depends on changes in the environment and individual sensitivity levels.
සිංහල idea
Sensation කියන්නේ senses වලට stimulus එකක් ආපු ගමන් ඇතිවන direct response එක. Stimulus කියන්නේ senses එකකට ඇතුල් වෙන input එක.
•
Sight, sound, smell, taste, touch = sensory inputs.
•
Package, logo, music, smell, product texture වගේ දේ stimulus වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Sensation = first raw feeling; Perception = brain gives meaning.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
How Sensation Works

Sensation depends on changes in the environment and individual sensitivity levels.

Energy Change

Sensation happens when something CHANGES in the environment

Sensation depends on ENERGY CHANGE in the environment — not just the stimulus alone.

If the room is always bright, you stop noticing the brightness. But when a light suddenly turns ON in a dark room — you notice immediately.

This is why CHANGE attracts attention — your brain is wired to notice what is DIFFERENT.

Individual Sensitivity

Different people notice different things — senses vary from person to person

Sensitivity to stimuli varies with the QUALITY of a person's sensory receptors.

Example: A person with excellent eyesight notices small text on a label; someone with poor eyesight cannot.
Example: A person with hearing loss may miss a soft audio advertisement.

Marketing Implications

Which senses do marketers focus on most?

Most marketing communications appeal to SIGHT and SOUND — TV ads, billboards, online videos.

However, SMELL and TOUCH also offer big opportunities — bakery smells, product feel in stores.

📌 EXAMPLE A Dialog TV ad with loud, energetic music grabs MORE attention than a soft, quiet version — because the energy change and sound intensity crosses your sensory threshold.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide DEEPENS Slide 6. Now we understand that sensation doesn't just need a stimulus — it needs a CHANGE in energy and enough INTENSITY to be noticed.

Perception and the Use of Senses

How each of the five senses is used as a marketing tool.

Sense-by-Sense Marketing Table

Every sense can be used to shape how a customer perceives a brand

SenseIllustration / How it is Used in Marketing
SmellSweet scents like deodorants or coffee aroma attract customers — smell triggers positive feelings before buying.
SoundMusic in TV ads affects how we interpret the message — pleasant music creates positive emotions and brand memory.
TasteBlind tests show that brand image changes how we perceive taste — consumers rate Coca-Cola differently when they see the brand.
TouchVery important in physical stores but tricky online — marketers now insert fabric samples in magazines so customers can feel before buying.

SLIDE 9 — Sight — The Dominant Sense
Why vision dominates over all other senses in consumer perception.
සිංහල idea

Sight — The Most Powerful Marketing Sense

Why vision dominates over all other senses in consumer perception.

"Almost our entire understanding of the world is experienced through our senses." — Martin Lindstrom

Why Sight is Number One

The brain processes visual information faster and more efficiently than any other sense

Almost 75% of ALL information processed by the human brain is VISUAL.

Sight is the FIRST sense we use to identify a brand (e.g., seeing a logo immediately).

Sight DOMINATES over the other four senses — it is called the most 'seductive' sense.

Visual advertisements are MORE prominent and MORE reliable — the brain gets more information while using FEWER resources.

This means: a good IMAGE can communicate more than 100 words of text.

KEY CONCEPT Marketers INVEST most in visual elements: logo design, packaging colour, billboard layout. A well-designed logo can build brand recognition in milliseconds. Online marketing is SO powerful partly because it reaches consumers VISUALLY.
📌 EXAMPLE The Apple logo (a bitten apple in silver/white) is recognised in MILLISECONDS by consumers worldwide — before they even read a single word about the product.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide ZOOMS IN on just one sense: SIGHT. The previous slide gave the full table. Now we go deep on the most important sense for marketers.

Sight in Action — Real Brand Examples

How top brands use visual cues to trigger instant perception.

Apple — Clean and Premium Visual Cues

Minimalist white packaging creates an instant perception of luxury

The consumer SEES the minimal white packaging of Apple products.

IMMEDIATE sensation: Clean. Premium. Sophisticated.

This happens BEFORE any technical evaluation — just from SEEING the box.

Apple uses WHITE (purity), SIMPLICITY (no clutter), and MINIMALISM to create a 'premium' perception.

Nike — Colour to Attract Attention

Bright colours inside a store direct customer attention intentionally

Bright yellow 'SALE' signs in Nike stores CATCH EYES instantly.

Yellow is a high-visibility colour — the brain is drawn to it automatically.

Marketers place sale signs at eye level and in bold colours for maximum visual impact.

📌 EXAMPLE When you walk into a Singer showroom in Colombo and see a big RED 'SALE' banner, your eyes go there FIRST — before you look at any product. That is visual perception at work.

Sound — Creating Emotional Connections Through Audio

How brands use sound to stay in your memory and build a relationship with you.

Why Sound is Powerful

Sound triggers emotions and memories more directly than most senses

Sound can be incredibly powerful in creating EMOTIONAL CONNECTIONS.

Sound TRIGGERS MEMORIES — a song can instantly bring back feelings from the past.

Companies create a SOUND IDENTITY — a unique sound linked to their brand.

The goal: the customer hears the sound → they INSTANTLY think of the brand.

Netflix Example

A global example of sound identity done perfectly

The Netflix intro sound ('ta-dum') creates IMMEDIATE RECOGNITION worldwide.

Even if you close your eyes and cannot see the screen, you know Netflix has started.

This sound has become part of Netflix's brand identity — as powerful as their red logo.

KEY CONCEPT Sound identity = a unique audio signature that represents a brand. The best sound identities are SHORT (under 3 seconds) but very MEMORABLE. Other examples: Intel's 4-note jingle, McDonald's 'ba da ba ba baa', Nokia ringtone.
📌 EXAMPLE The Dialog ringtone or jingle used in their TV ads — when Sri Lankan customers HEAR it, they immediately associate it with mobile connectivity and trust.

SLIDE 12 — Smell

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Smell — The Memory Sense

How smell creates the strongest and longest-lasting memories connected to products and brands.

Why Smell is Scientifically Special

Smell is directly connected to the memory and emotion centres of the brain

Two American researchers developed a SCIENTIFIC EXPLANATION of how people associate MEMORIES with SMELLS.

Smell is the only sense that is DIRECTLY connected to the brain's memory and emotion centres.

This is why a smell can instantly bring back a memory from 10 years ago.

Product Scent vs. Ambient Scent

It matters WHERE the smell comes from

PRODUCT SCENT = smell that comes from the product itself (e.g., a scented shampoo).

AMBIENT SCENT = smell in the environment where the product is sold (e.g., a perfume sprayed in the mall corridor).

Research found: PRODUCT SCENT creates STRONGER memory than ambient scent.

BONUS: When a product is scented, customers also remember OTHER features of the product better (e.g., packaging, price, brand name).

📌 EXAMPLE Lux soap has a signature floral smell — when a Sri Lankan customer smells it, they immediately remember the brand, the white packaging, and the 'beauty' association. Product scent at work!
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide continues the senses series — SMELL after SIGHT and SOUND. It introduces new research (product scent vs ambient scent) which is an EXAM-WORTHY detail.

Why physically touching a product makes you more likely to buy it.
සිංහල idea

Touch — The Power of Holding a Product

Why physically touching a product makes you more likely to buy it.

Touch Influences Buying Decisions

Studies show that holding a product changes how a customer feels about it

Multiple studies show: TOUCHING a product influences PERSUASION — it makes customers more likely to buy.

Touch can be used as a PERSUASIVE TOOL by smart retailers.

Physically holding products creates a sense of OWNERSHIP in the customer's mind.

This feeling of 'it is already mine' TRIGGERS 'must-have' purchase decisions.

The Golden Rule of Retail

The most important rule every sales person knows

'Get the customer to HOLD the product.' — This is the FIRST RULE of retail sales.

This is why Apple Stores let you pick up and USE every iPhone on display.

This is why Odel and clothing stores let you TRY on clothes before buying.

⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Touch is problematic for ONLINE retailers — customers CANNOT feel the product before buying. This is why online stores offer easy returns and fabric samples by mail.
📌 EXAMPLE At a Damro furniture store, the salesperson asks you to SIT on the sofa. The moment you sit and feel the comfort, you begin to feel it is 'yours' — and you are more likely to buy it.
