SLIDE 14 — Absolute Threshold
The minimum amount of a stimulus needed for a person to even notice it.
සිංහල idea
Stimulus එක තියෙනවා කියලා personට පළවෙනි වතාවට notice කරන්න පුළුවන් minimum level එක.
•
Below threshold = notice වෙන්නේ නෑ.
•
Above threshold = stimulus එක තියෙනවා කියලා දැනෙනවා.
උදාහරණය: Shop board එක දුරින් තියෙනකොට අකුරු ටික පේන්න පටන් ගන්න minimum distance/size.
Exam මතකය: Minimum to notice something exists.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Absolute Threshold

The minimum amount of a stimulus needed for a person to even notice it.

DEFINITION Absolute Threshold = the LOWEST level at which an individual can experience a sensation. It is the dividing line between 'something' and 'nothing.'

What It Means Simply

Think of it as the minimum signal needed to turn on your brain's awareness

Below the absolute threshold = person detects NOTHING — stimulus is too weak.

At or above the absolute threshold = person NOTICES the stimulus for the first time.

The absolute threshold is DIFFERENT for different people — some have sharper senses.

Sensory Adaptation

What happens when you are exposed to the same stimulus for a long time

Sensory adaptation = 'getting USED TO' certain sensations over time.

When you wear a perfume for hours, you stop smelling it — your nose adapts.

When a billboard has been in the same spot for months, drivers stop noticing it.

This forces marketers to CHANGE their ads regularly — to stay above the threshold of noticeability.

⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Absolute Threshold — minimum level to notice a stimulus. Sensory Adaptation — getting so used to a stimulus that you stop noticing it. Both are important exam concepts!
📌 EXAMPLE A very quiet radio ad that plays in a shop might fall BELOW your absolute threshold — you hear noise but do not process it. The advertiser must make it LOUDER to cross your threshold.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces the SECOND element of perception: thresholds. We have learned about stimuli and sensory receptors. Now we learn: how STRONG must a stimulus be to be noticed?
SLIDE 15 — Absolute Threshold — Advertising Examples
Practical examples of how the absolute threshold concept applies to marketing.
සිංහල idea
Stimulus එක තියෙනවා කියලා personට පළවෙනි වතාවට notice කරන්න පුළුවන් minimum level එක.
•
Below threshold = notice වෙන්නේ නෑ.
•
Above threshold = stimulus එක තියෙනවා කියලා දැනෙනවා.
උදාහරණය: Shop board එක දුරින් තියෙනකොට අකුරු ටික පේන්න පටන් ගන්න minimum distance/size.
Exam මතකය: Minimum to notice something exists.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Absolute Threshold in Real Advertising Situations

Practical examples of how the absolute threshold concept applies to marketing.

Billboard Example

How brightness affects whether a roadside ad is noticed

A roadside billboard with DIM LIGHTING may NOT be noticed by drivers — below the visual absolute threshold.

When brightness INCREASES enough to capture attention — it CROSSES the visual absolute threshold.

Advertisers ensure: lights, FONT SIZE, and CONTRAST are ABOVE the threshold of noticeability.

Perfume Section in a Mall

How scent levels determine whether a customer notices and investigates

A VERY LIGHT scent spray near the perfume section may or may NOT be noticed.

If fragrance is TOO FAINT → customer does not notice ANYTHING — below absolute threshold.
Once scent REACHES the detectable level → consumer turns head and becomes CURIOUS.

That minimum detectable scent level = the consumer's ABSOLUTE THRESHOLD for that smell.

KEY CONCEPT Marketers MUST design stimuli (lights, sounds, smells) to be ABOVE the absolute threshold. Too weak = ignored. Too strong = uncomfortable. The RIGHT level = noticed and appreciated. This is the 'Goldilocks Zone' of sensory marketing.
📌 EXAMPLE Odel's air conditioning and lighting system is carefully designed — bright enough to highlight products, cool enough to feel comfortable, scented enough to feel premium — all above threshold!
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide ILLUSTRATES Slide 14's definition with two very concrete examples. Definition → Examples. This pattern helps you remember concepts for exams.
SLIDE 16 — Differential Threshold (JND)
The minimum change in a stimulus that a person can actually notice.
සිංහල idea
දැනට තියෙන stimulus එකේ change එකක් වෙනවා කියලා notice කරන්න පුළුවන් minimum difference එක.
•
Price, size, packaging, logo change වලට apply වෙනවා.
•
Bad change එක customerට නොපෙනෙන්න below JND කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Good improvement එක පේන්න above JND කරන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Logo එක එකපාර ලොකු change එකක් නොකර පොඩි පොඩි changes කරන එක.
Exam මතකය: JND = minimum change you can notice.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Differential Threshold (JND)

The minimum change in a stimulus that a person can actually notice.

DEFINITION Differential Threshold = the MINIMAL DIFFERENCE that can be detected between two similar stimuli. Also called the Just Noticeable Difference (JND). Based on Weber's Law: the bigger the original stimulus, the bigger the change must be to be noticed.

Weber's Law

The rule that explains how we detect changes in stimuli

Weber's Law states: the noticeable difference is PROPORTIONAL to the strength of the original stimulus.

Big product → BIG change needed to be noticed. Small product → SMALL change enough.
Example: A 10g weight increase on 1kg feels nothing. The same 10g on a pencil feels huge.

JND — Two Marketing Applications

Marketers use JND in both directions depending on their goal

•
PRODUCT IMPROVEMENTS — want to EXCEED the JND: customers should CLEARLY notice the improvement.
Example: A new formula must taste noticeably better — otherwise customers think nothing changed.
•
PREVENT NEGATIVE CHANGES — want to STAY BELOW the JND: customers should NOT notice.
Example: Quietly reducing the quantity in a packet by 10g — below JND so customers do not notice.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: JND = Just Noticeable Difference. When IMPROVING a product — exceed JND so customers notice. When REDUCING quantity/quality (to save cost) — stay BELOW JND so customers do NOT notice.
📌 EXAMPLE Elephant House reduces their ice cream tub from 1L to 900ml quietly — below the JND. Customers do not notice the 10% reduction but the company saves millions in costs!
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces the SECOND type of threshold — Differential Threshold (JND). Slide 14 taught Absolute Threshold (notice/no notice). Now JND teaches: how big must a CHANGE be to be noticed?
SLIDE 17 — JND — Logos and Packaging
How the Just Noticeable Difference explains how companies update their logos gradually over time.
සිංහල idea
Logo/packaging change කරනකොට customerට එකපාර ලොකු වෙනසක් නොදැනෙන්න small changes කරන්න JND idea එක use කරනවා. Bad change hide කරන්න below JND; good improvement notice කරවන්න above JND.
Simple English idea: How the Just Noticeable Difference explains how companies update their logos gradually over time.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
JND Applied to Logos and Packaging

How the Just Noticeable Difference explains how companies update their logos gradually over time.

Gradual Logo Evolution

Big brands update their logos slowly so customers barely notice the change

Brands update their logo over years using SMALL INCREMENTAL CHANGES — staying below the JND each time.

The slide shows 8 portrait images — representing how a brand identity evolves step by step over decades.

Each individual update is small enough (BELOW JND) that customers do not feel disoriented.

But after many years, the logo looks VERY DIFFERENT from the original — a total transformation.

If the change was SUDDEN and LARGE (above JND), customers would feel confused or upset.

KEY CONCEPT Apple, Google, McDonald's, Pepsi — all have updated their logos gradually over decades. Staying below JND = brand loyalty is preserved. Customers feel 'same brand, just fresher.' Crossing JND suddenly = customers feel the brand changed too much = backlash.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: For logo/packaging redesign, companies STAY BELOW the JND to maintain brand recognition while modernising their look.
📌 EXAMPLE The Milo tin in Sri Lanka has changed its design several times over the years — but always SLOWLY. Each update was small enough that loyal Milo customers barely noticed the change.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide is a SPECIFIC APPLICATION of JND from Slide 16. It shows that JND is not just about product quantity — it applies to VISUAL IDENTITY (logos, packaging) too.
SLIDE 18 — Subliminal Perception
When your senses receive information without your conscious mind being aware of it.
සිංහල idea
Conscious mind එකට clear awareness නැති level එකක information brain එක process කරන එක.
•
Absolute threshold එක්ක mix කරන්න එපා.
•
Background music/lighting mood එකට subtle effect එකක් දෙන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Supermarket එකේ soft music එක customer mood එක calm කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Subliminal = below conscious awareness.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Subliminal Perception

When your senses receive information without your conscious mind being aware of it.

DEFINITION Subliminal Perception = receiving and processing information from your senses WITHOUT consciously being aware that it is happening. You perceive without knowing you perceive.

How Subliminal Perception Works

Your brain processes far more than your conscious mind is aware of

You CAN perceive stimuli without being consciously AWARE that you are doing so.

Your brain is always absorbing background information — colours, words, sounds — below your awareness level.

This is called 'below the threshold of awareness' — different from the absolute threshold (which is about noticing something EXISTS).

KEY CONCEPT Subliminal = below the level of conscious awareness. Subliminal messages in ads are CONTROVERSIAL — most research shows their effect is weak. But background music, lighting, and store layout work subliminally — shaping mood without you knowing.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Subliminal perception ≠ absolute threshold. Absolute threshold = minimum to notice. Subliminal = below conscious awareness. These are two different concepts!
📌 EXAMPLE The soft, relaxing background music playing inside a Cargills Food City store affects your MOOD subliminally — making you feel calm and take more time browsing (and buying more!).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces SUBLIMINAL perception — a special type that works BELOW conscious awareness. It contrasts with the absolute and differential thresholds we just studied.
SLIDE 19 — Perceptual Selection — The Stimulus
Physical features of a stimulus that attract consumer attention and shape perception.
සිංහල idea
අපිට එන හැම stimulus එකම brain එක process කරන්නේ නෑ. අපි select කරනවා.
•
Stimulus features — colour, size, contrast.
•
Expectation — අපි බලාපොරොත්තු වෙන දේ.
•
Motives — අපිට දැන් ඕන දේ.
උදාහරණය: Camera එකක් ගන්න ඉන්න කෙනා camera ad එක ඉක්මනින් notice කරනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Perceptual Selection — The Stimulus

Physical features of a stimulus that attract consumer attention and shape perception.

DEFINITION Perceptual Selection = consumers do NOT pay attention to ALL stimuli around them. They SELECT which stimuli to notice, based on the stimulus itself, their expectations, and their needs.

The Stimulus — Physical Characteristics That Attract Attention

What makes a stimulus stand out and get noticed?

Product and its attributes — shape, size, colour, quality.

Package design — attractive packaging draws the eye on a crowded shelf.

Brand name — a familiar or unusual name catches attention.

Advertisements and commercials — including copy claims, model choice, model positioning, ad size, typography.

Placement of promotional messages — WHERE in the ad space a message is placed matters.

KEY CONCEPT Contrast is the MOST POWERFUL attention-grabbing attribute of any stimulus. Something that is DIFFERENT from its surroundings gets noticed first. A black-and-white world with ONE red object — your eyes go to the red object instantly.
📌 EXAMPLE On a Sri Lankan TV channel, most ads are colourful — so if a brand uses a PURE BLACK AND WHITE ad, it stands out dramatically. The contrast makes viewers stop and watch.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces PERCEPTUAL SELECTION — the next stage after sensation. We now move from 'receiving stimuli' to 'choosing which stimuli to pay attention to.'
SLIDE 20 — Contrast — Attention-Compelling Stimulus
How contrast in advertising design makes a stimulus impossible to ignore.
සිංහල idea
Sensation කියන්නේ senses වලට stimulus එකක් ආපු ගමන් ඇතිවන direct response එක. Stimulus කියන්නේ senses එකකට ඇතුල් වෙන input එක.
•
Sight, sound, smell, taste, touch = sensory inputs.
•
Package, logo, music, smell, product texture වගේ දේ stimulus වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Sensation = first raw feeling; Perception = brain gives meaning.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Contrast — The Most Powerful Attention Tool

How contrast in advertising design makes a stimulus impossible to ignore.

Crest 3D Whitestrips — Contrast Advertising Example

A global brand uses all-white imagery to make a dramatic statement about tooth whitening

Both Crest ads use an ALL-WHITE visual world — white cherries, white coffee, white cup.

Everything in the background is white — making the PRODUCT LOGO the only contrast point.

The taglines: 'Removes the cherry from the cherries' and 'Removes the coffee from the coffee.'

This means: Crest whitens so well, it can remove even NATURAL stains from white things.

The contrast technique here = white background + small coloured logo = your eye goes STRAIGHT to the brand.

KEY CONCEPT Contrast = placing a stimulus AGAINST a very different background to make it stand out. Your brain is biologically wired to notice DIFFERENCES — contrast exploits this. Advertisers use contrast in: colour, size, shape, movement, sound level.
📌 EXAMPLE A tiny tuk-tuk sticker ad on a plain white wall in Kandy stands out MORE than a big colourful ad surrounded by other big colourful ads. Why? Because of CONTRAST.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide ILLUSTRATES the contrast concept introduced at the end of Slide 19. It gives us a real global ad campaign as evidence that contrast is the top attention tool.
SLIDE 21 — Shocking Images — Provoking Attention
How non-profit campaigns use shock to break through consumers' selective perception.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් customerට information එක notice, select, organize, interpret වෙන විදිහ සහ marketer ඒ perception එකට බලපාන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: How non-profit campaigns use shock to break through consumers' selective perception.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Shocking and Unrealistic Images Provoke Attention

How non-profit campaigns use shock to break through consumers' selective perception.

Children's Defense Fund Campaign

A powerful social message using shocking visual contrast

The campaign shows: baby's face digitally placed on an adult body (pregnant woman / tattooed prisoner).

Text: 'Cutting her/him from the budget now costs all of us later.'

This is shocking because it is UNEXPECTED and UNREALISTIC — babies do not look like this.

The shock FORCES the viewer to stop, look, and READ the message — breaking through selective perception.

The campaign communicates: cutting Medicaid/early education funding has serious long-term costs.

KEY CONCEPT Shock advertising = using surprising or disturbing images to break through selective perception. When something is UNEXPECTED, the brain cannot ignore it — it demands attention. Often used by social causes, public health campaigns, and non-profits for maximum impact.
📌 EXAMPLE Sri Lanka's road safety campaigns often show shocking crash statistics or images on billboards — the shock factor forces drivers to actually READ and REMEMBER the message.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide shows ANOTHER way marketers overcome selective perception (which started in Slide 19). If contrast (Slide 20) doesn't work, shock and unrealism are even more powerful tools.
SLIDE 22 — Expectations and Motives
Two internal factors that shape which stimuli a consumer chooses to notice and pay attention to.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් customerට information එක notice, select, organize, interpret වෙන විදිහ සහ marketer ඒ perception එකට බලපාන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: Two internal factors that shape which stimuli a consumer chooses to notice and pay attention to.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Expectations and Motives in Perceptual Selection

Two internal factors that shape which stimuli a consumer chooses to notice and pay attention to.

Expectations

What you EXPECT to see changes what you actually see

People usually see what they EXPECT to see — based on familiarity, past experience, or prior beliefs.

In marketing: a consumer tends to perceive products according to their OWN EXPECTATIONS.

Example: If you expect a premium hotel to have clean toilets, even an average toilet there feels acceptable.
Example: If you expect a cheap hotel to have bad food, even okay food seems bad.

Motives

What you NEED or WANT right now makes you notice relevant things more

People notice things they NEED or WANT — the stronger the need, the MORE they notice it.

They IGNORE stimuli that are unrelated to their current needs.

Example: When hungry, you notice every food ad and restaurant sign on the road.
Example: When looking to buy a phone, every smartphone billboard seems to jump out at you.
📌 EXAMPLE When you are THIRSTY and walking in Pettah market, your eyes automatically find 'Elephant House Ginger Beer' signs — even among hundreds of other signs. That is MOTIVES shaping perception.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide completes the THREE factors of perceptual selection: (1) The Stimulus — Slide 19/20/21, (2) Expectations — this slide, (3) Motives — this slide. All three determine what gets noticed.
SLIDE 23 — Selective Perception
How consumers filter and manage the enormous amount of information they receive every day.
සිංහල idea
Perception කියන්නේ environment එකෙන් ලැබෙන information brain එක select කරලා, arrange කරලා, meaning එකක් දෙන process එක.
•
එකම thing එක දෙන්නෙක්ට වෙනස් විදිහට පේන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Actual product එකට වඩා customerගේ perception එක buying decision එකට වැදගත් වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Keells product එක එක්කෙනෙක්ට 'cheap & good', තව කෙනෙක්ට 'low quality' වගේ පේන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Perception = how the mind sees/understands the world.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Selective Perception

How consumers filter and manage the enormous amount of information they receive every day.

Selective Exposure

Consumers CHOOSE which messages to encounter

Selective exposure = consumers tune INTO messages they find PLEASANT or SYMPATHETIC.

They ACTIVELY AVOID messages that are painful or threatening.

Example: A person who loves cars watches car ads eagerly. They skip ads about things they dislike.

Selective Attention

Consumers NOTICE some things and IGNORE others based on their needs

Selective attention = HEIGHTENED AWARENESS of stimuli that meet their needs or interests.

MINIMAL AWARENESS of stimuli irrelevant to their needs.

Consumers NOTICE ads for products they need and DISREGARD those they have no interest in.

Example: A person looking to buy a car notices every car ad on TV — but ignores baby product ads.
KEY CONCEPT Selective Exposure = choosing what you encounter (changing channel, skipping ad). Selective Attention = you encounter the stimulus but only pay attention if it is relevant. Marketers must make ads RELEVANT and INTERESTING to break through selective attention.
📌 EXAMPLE On YouTube, Sri Lankan viewers SKIP ads after 5 seconds — that is selective exposure in action. Marketers must put their KEY MESSAGE in the first 5 seconds before the skip.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces the formal term 'Selective Perception' — the umbrella concept covering what we have been building towards. Expectations and motives (Slide 22) EXPLAIN why selective perception happens.
SLIDE 24 — Perceptual Defense
How the mind protects itself by unconsciously blocking threatening or uncomfortable information.
සිංහල idea
මේ තුන එක වගේ පේනත් action එක වෙනස්.
•
Selective Exposure = බලන්න/නොබලන්න choice කරනවා; ad skip කරනවා.
•
Selective Attention = ad තිබුණත් relevant එකට විතරක් focus කරනවා.
•
Perceptual Defense = uncomfortable info එක brain එක mentally block/ignore කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: YouTube baby-cream ad skip = exposure; TV camera ad එකට focus = attention; smoker warning image mentally ignore = defense.
Exam මතකය: Skip / Focus / Brain block.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Perceptual Defense

How the mind protects itself by unconsciously blocking threatening or uncomfortable information.

DEFINITION Perceptual Defense = consumers SUBCONSCIOUSLY SCREEN OUT stimuli that they find psychologically threatening — even after exposure has already happened. Threatening stimuli are LESS LIKELY to be consciously perceived than neutral stimuli.

How Perceptual Defense Works

Your mind acts like a guard — blocking unpleasant information before it reaches full awareness

The consumer IS exposed to the stimulus — they see/hear it.

But the BRAIN automatically reduces awareness of threatening information.

This happens SUBCONSCIOUSLY — the person does not even know they are doing it.

Result: The threatening ad is seen but NOT truly registered or remembered.

Contrast with Selective Exposure

The key difference between avoiding and defending

Selective exposure = AVOIDING the stimulus (changing channel BEFORE seeing the ad).

Perceptual defense = SEEING the stimulus but BLOCKING it mentally (exposure has already taken place).

⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Perceptual Defense is different from Selective Exposure. Exposure = you avoid. Defense = you see it but your mind blocks the full impact. Exposure comes BEFORE. Defense comes AFTER.
📌 EXAMPLE A heavy smoker sees a graphic anti-smoking ad. Their mind 'sees' it but subconsciously blocks the message — perceptual defense protects their self-image from the threatening content.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces PERCEPTUAL DEFENSE — the third filtering mechanism after selective exposure and selective attention (Slide 23). Together they form the complete picture of how consumers filter information.
SLIDE 25 — Perceptual Organization — Gestalt Psychology
How the human brain automatically organises pieces of information into a complete, meaningful picture.
සිංහල idea
Brain එක pieces වෙන වෙනම බලන්නේ නැතුව whole pattern එකක් හදනවා.
•
Figure-Ground = main object එක සහ background එක වෙන් කරනවා.
•
Grouping = ළඟ/සමාන items එක group එකක් වගේ පේනවා.
•
Closure = missing part brain එකෙන් fill කරලා complete image එකක් හදනවා.
උදාහරණය: Movie scene එකේ product එක main figure එකක් වගේ place කරන product placement.
Exam මතකය: Figure / Group / Fill missing part.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Perceptual Organization — Gestalt Psychology

How the human brain automatically organises pieces of information into a complete, meaningful picture.

DEFINITION Perceptual Organization = the process by which we organise sensory inputs into a whole that is greater than the sum of its parts. The principles are called GESTALT PSYCHOLOGY.

Principle 1: Figure and Ground

Your brain separates what is 'important' (figure) from the 'background' (ground)

FIGURE = the main stimulus — the thing your brain focuses on.

GROUND = the background environment or context around the figure.

For something to be noticed as FIGURE, it must stand out from the ground.

A sound must be LOUDER or SOFTER than background noise to be noticed as the figure.

A colour must be BRIGHTER or PALER than the background to stand out as the figure.

KEY CONCEPT Gestalt Psychology = German word for 'shape/form' — the mind sees WHOLES, not just parts. The brain fills in missing pieces and creates complete, unified perceptions automatically. Marketers use Gestalt principles to design logos, ads, and store layouts.
📌 EXAMPLE The Cargills Food City logo stands out against the white packaging BECAUSE it contrasts — the red logo = FIGURE, the white background = GROUND. Your eye goes straight to the logo.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces PERCEPTUAL ORGANIZATION — a new major section. After learning about how we SELECT information (Slides 19–24), we now learn how we ORGANIZE and MAKE SENSE of it.
SLIDE 26 — Figure and Ground — Product Placement
How brands use the figure-ground principle to embed advertising into entertainment content.
සිංහල idea
Figure = customerට main එක විදිහට පේන object/message. Ground = background. Product placement වල product එක scene එකේ figure වෙන්න දාන විදිහ marketer decide කරනවා.
Simple English idea: How brands use the figure-ground principle to embed advertising into entertainment content.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Figure and Ground in Marketing — Product Placement

How brands use the figure-ground principle to embed advertising into entertainment content.

Marketing Technique: Inserting Ads into Entertainment

Brands become part of the story — not interruptions

A marketing technique based on figure-and-ground: INSERTING ADVERTISEMENTS INTO ENTERTAINMENT CONTENT.

Product placement = the advertised product (FIGURE) is deliberately placed INSIDE a TV show or film (GROUND).

The show/film is the background (ground). The product is the thing your eyes pick up (figure).

Because it is embedded naturally, consumers don't 'defend' against it — it bypasses perceptual defense.

KEY CONCEPT Product placement is extremely effective because it bypasses selective exposure and perceptual defense. The consumer CHOOSES to watch the movie — but the brand is smuggled in as the 'figure.' Famous examples: James Bond + Aston Martin, Apple products in nearly every Hollywood movie.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Product placement = figure (product) embedded in ground (TV show/film). This is a direct APPLICATION of figure-and-ground Gestalt principle in marketing.
📌 EXAMPLE In a popular Sri Lankan TV drama, the characters drink Nestea iced tea in every scene. The drama = ground. Nestea = figure. Viewers absorb the brand subconsciously while enjoying the show.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide takes the THEORETICAL figure-and-ground concept from Slide 25 and shows a real MARKETING APPLICATION: product placement. Theory → Practice.
SLIDE 27 — Gestalt — Grouping and Closure
Two more brain tendencies that marketers use to create powerful, unified brand messages.
සිංහල idea
Brain එක pieces වෙන වෙනම බලන්නේ නැතුව whole pattern එකක් හදනවා.
•
Figure-Ground = main object එක සහ background එක වෙන් කරනවා.
•
Grouping = ළඟ/සමාන items එක group එකක් වගේ පේනවා.
•
Closure = missing part brain එකෙන් fill කරලා complete image එකක් හදනවා.
උදාහරණය: Movie scene එකේ product එක main figure එකක් වගේ place කරන product placement.
Exam මතකය: Figure / Group / Fill missing part.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Gestalt Psychology — Grouping and Closure

Two more brain tendencies that marketers use to create powerful, unified brand messages.

Principle 2: Grouping

The brain automatically groups related things together

GROUPING = people's instinctive tendency to group stimuli together into a unified picture or impression.

When things look similar, are near each other, or form a pattern — the brain groups them as ONE unit.

Marketers use grouping by placing premium products NEAR other premium products — the association transfers.

Example: Displaying a budget watch next to a luxury Rolex — the budget watch looks more premium by association.

Principle 3: Closure

The brain fills in missing pieces to complete an incomplete image

CLOSURE = people's instinct to organise INCOMPLETE pieces of sensory input into a COMPLETE image or feeling.

Your brain HATES incompleteness — it automatically fills in the gaps.

Marketers use this: an incomplete logo or jingle makes consumers MENTALLY COMPLETE it — actively engaging their brain.

The Gillette razor images show clothes arranged without a person inside — your mind fills in 'a person wearing these clothes.'

📌 EXAMPLE McDonald's iconic 'M' arch — even if you only see HALF the golden arch (like on a foggy day), your brain automatically completes it. That is CLOSURE in action.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide completes the three Gestalt principles: (1) Figure & Ground (Slide 25), (2) Grouping, (3) Closure. All three explain HOW the brain organises what it perceives.
SLIDE 28 — Perceptual Interpretation — Stereotyping
How pre-existing mental images and biases shape how we interpret what we perceive.
සිංහල idea
අපි කලින් තියෙන beliefs/mental shortcuts use කරලා people/products ඉක්මනින් judge කරනවා.
•
Physical appearance stereotype trigger කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Descriptive words expectation එක වෙනස් කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
First impression එක පස්සේ judgement වලට බලපානවා.
උදාහරණය: 'Premium', 'organic', 'luxury' වචන product ගැන expectation එකක් හදනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Perceptual Interpretation — Stereotyping

How pre-existing mental images and biases shape how we interpret what we perceive.

DEFINITION Stereotypes = biased mental pictures that individuals carry about the meanings of various stimuli. We interpret new things through the lens of existing stereotypes.

How Stereotypes Affect Perception

Our mental shortcuts (stereotypes) can cause us to misinterpret things

Individuals carry BIASED PICTURES in their minds about what things mean.

These mental pictures (stereotypes) FILTER and COLOUR how we interpret new stimuli.

This is PERCEPTUAL INTERPRETATION — giving MEANING to what we have perceived.

Benetton Campaign Example

A real case where stereotyping caused public misinterpretation

United Colors of Benetton ad: one BLACK man and one WHITE man, HANDCUFFED together.

INTENDED message: racial harmony — two races united together (the handcuffs symbolise unity).

PERCEIVED message (by many viewers): a WHITE man ARRESTING a BLACK man.

Result: PUBLIC OUTCRY — people's racial stereotypes overrode the intended message.

This shows how POWERFUL stereotypes are — they can completely change interpretation.

📌 EXAMPLE If a Sri Lankan ad shows a woman cooking in the kitchen, many viewers stereotype it as 'women belong in the kitchen' — even if the brand intended to show a happy family scene. Stereotypes shape perception.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces PERCEPTUAL INTERPRETATION — the FINAL stage of perception after selection and organisation. We now give MEANING to what we have perceived.
SLIDE 29 — Triggers of Stereotyping — Physical Appearance
How the physical appearance of people and products triggers automatic stereotype-based judgments.
සිංහල idea
අපි කලින් තියෙන beliefs/mental shortcuts use කරලා people/products ඉක්මනින් judge කරනවා.
•
Physical appearance stereotype trigger කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Descriptive words expectation එක වෙනස් කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Triggers of Stereotyping — Physical Appearance

How the physical appearance of people and products triggers automatic stereotype-based judgments.

Physical Appearance of People

How models and celebrities in ads trigger stereotypes

People attribute QUALITIES they associate with certain TYPES of people to others who RESEMBLE them.

This happens whether or not they consciously recognise the similarity.

This is why the SELECTION OF MODELS for print ads and TV commercials is so critically important.

The right model = the consumer transfers positive qualities (trustworthy, attractive, expert) to the product.

Physical Appearance of Products

How a product looks changes how customers evaluate it

A product's PHYSICAL APPEARANCE often INFLUENCES consumers' judgments about quality and value.

Expensive-looking packaging → consumer thinks the product is HIGH QUALITY.
Cheap, flimsy packaging → consumer thinks the product is LOW QUALITY — even before testing it.
KEY CONCEPT Beautiful/attractive models → product perceived as desirable and premium. Expert-looking presenters (lab coats, suits) → product perceived as scientific and trustworthy. Heavy, solid product weight → perceived as durable and high quality.
📌 EXAMPLE A shampoo brand uses a celebrity with glossy, long hair in their ad. Consumers STEREOTYPE: 'This shampoo gives hair like the celebrity's hair.' Physical appearance of the model = product benefit perception.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide breaks stereotyping into specific TRIGGERS — starting with physical appearance. Slide 28 introduced stereotyping; now we learn WHAT CAUSES stereotyping to activate.
SLIDE 30 — Triggers of Stereotyping — Descriptive Terms
How the language used in advertising and communication triggers stereotype-based perceptions.
සිංහල idea
අපි කලින් තියෙන beliefs/mental shortcuts use කරලා people/products ඉක්මනින් judge කරනවා.
•
Physical appearance stereotype trigger කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Descriptive words expectation එක වෙනස් කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
First impression එක පස්සේ judgement වලට බලපානවා.
උදාහරණය: 'Premium', 'organic', 'luxury' වචන product ගැන expectation එකක් හදනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Triggers of Stereotyping — Descriptive Terms (Words)

How the language used in advertising and communication triggers stereotype-based perceptions.

Words That Trigger Stereotypes

The specific language used in ads and messages activates pre-existing mental biases

Stereotypes are often REFLECTED IN VERBAL MESSAGES — the words used shape perception.

Words carry cultural and social stereotypes beyond their literal meaning.

The Driving Example

How the same action is described differently based on gender stereotypes

Expression: 'He drives like a REAL MAN' → connotes: FAST, AGGRESSIVE, reckless driving.
Expression: 'He drives like a WOMAN' → connotes: cautious, slow, effeminate driving.

Both describe driving — but the WORDS carry very different stereotyped meanings.

Advertisers must be VERY CAREFUL about language — words can activate powerful stereotypes unintentionally.

KEY CONCEPT Descriptive terms in ads = one of the most powerful (and dangerous) stereotyping triggers. Words like 'strong', 'gentle', 'powerful', 'soft' all carry gender and cultural stereotypes. Modern marketing must be aware of and often actively challenge these stereotypes.
📌 EXAMPLE A Sri Lankan car ad saying 'The man's choice' for an SUV vs. 'The family choice' for a minivan — both use words to trigger gender stereotypes about who drives what vehicle.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide shows the SECOND trigger of stereotyping: descriptive terms/words. Slide 29 showed physical appearance. Both show that stereotyping is activated by many different types of stimuli.
SLIDE 31 — First Impressions
Why the very first perception of a brand or product sets a nearly permanent mental image.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් customerට information එක notice, select, organize, interpret වෙන විදිහ සහ marketer ඒ perception එකට බලපාන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: Why the very first perception of a brand or product sets a nearly permanent mental image.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
First Impressions

Why the very first perception of a brand or product sets a nearly permanent mental image.

DEFINITION First Impressions = the initial perception formed when encountering a product, brand, or person for the first time. These tend to be lasting and difficult to change.

Why First Impressions Last

The brain gives extra weight to the FIRST piece of information it receives

'You will never have a SECOND CHANCE to make a FIRST IMPRESSION.' — This famous saying is backed by psychology.

The FIRST information the brain receives about something creates a STRONG mental anchor.

All later information is evaluated AGAINST this first impression — not independently.

Marketing Implication: Do Not Launch Too Early

Launching a flawed product before it is perfect can permanently damage brand perception

Introducing a NEW PRODUCT before it has been PERFECTED may prove FATAL to its ultimate success.

Subsequent information about improvements — even if TRUE — will often be NEGATED by the memory of early poor performance.

Consumers say: 'I tried it before and it was bad' — and they may NEVER give it a second chance.

⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: First impressions are VERY hard to change. This is why marketers must ensure a product is fully ready before launch. A bad launch = lasting negative perception.
📌 EXAMPLE When Samsung Galaxy Note 7 phones caught fire in 2016, many consumers formed a permanent negative first impression of Samsung batteries — even after Samsung fixed the issue completely.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide continues PERCEPTUAL INTERPRETATION. We interpreted via stereotypes (Slides 28–30). Now we learn that the ORDER of information matters — FIRST impressions dominate all later ones.
SLIDE 32 — Halo Effect
When a good impression of ONE thing causes a person to judge EVERYTHING related to it positively.
සිංහල idea
එක strong good/bad feature එක නිසා whole person/brand/product එකම ඒ විදිහට judge කරන එක.
•
One positive feature → overall positive judgement.
•
Brand extension වලදී existing good image අලුත් product එකටත් යන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Apple phone ගැන trust තියෙන නිසා Apple Watch එකත් automatically good කියලා හිතන එක.
Exam මතකය: One feature colours the whole judgement.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Halo Effect

When a good impression of ONE thing causes a person to judge EVERYTHING related to it positively.

DEFINITION Halo Effect = the overall evaluation of an object based on the evaluation of just ONE or a FEW dimensions. One positive quality 'glows' onto everything else.

Original Halo Effect

The classic definition: one quality affects the overall judgment

If you think a person is very ATTRACTIVE, you also tend to assume they are smart, kind, and trustworthy.

That is the halo effect — one positive quality (attractiveness) 'glows' onto other qualities.

Halo Effect Expanded — Consumer Behavior

Consumer behaviorists apply this to brands and product lines

The halo effect can be applied to MULTIPLE OBJECTS (like a whole product line) based on one dimension.

That one dimension could be a BRAND NAME or a CELEBRITY SPOKESPERSON.

Consumers extend their positive feelings from ONE product to ALL products from the same brand.

Marketing Application — Brand Extension

How companies use halo effect to launch new products

Marketers can TAKE ADVANTAGE of the halo effect by extending a brand name to another product line.

Example: Apple makes great iPhones → so Apple's headphones (AirPods) and watches (Apple Watch) are trusted without much evaluation.

The iPhone's positive halo 'glows' onto every new Apple product.

KEY CONCEPT Halo Effect = one good thing makes everything else seem good too. Marketers use brand extension to transfer the halo from a successful product to new ones. A spokesperson's positive image creates a halo over the brand they endorse.
⭐ EXAM FOCUS EXAM TIP: Halo Effect in marketing = use one strong positive brand association (a trusted product or celebrity) to make ALL related products and lines seem better.
📌 EXAMPLE Keells supermarket is trusted for quality food. When Keells launches a new organic product line, consumers immediately TRUST it because of Keells' positive halo — without even trying the new product.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide is the FINAL concept in Perceptual Interpretation. First impressions (Slide 31) showed time matters. The Halo Effect shows that ONE positive feature can shape ALL perceptions.

SLIDE 3 — Motivation as a Psychological Force
Motivation is an inner energy that pushes you to do something because a need is not yet fulfilled.
සිංහල idea
Need එකක් satisfy වෙලා නැති නිසා tension එකක් එනවා. ඒ tension එක reduce කරන්න action එකකට push කරන inner force එක motivation.
•
Marketers needs create කරන්නේ නෑ; තිබෙන need එක remind/activate කරනවා.
•
Need → tension → action drive.
උදාහරණය: Thirsty → tension → water buy → thirst reduce.
Exam මතකය: Motivation = driving force from an unfulfilled need.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Motivation as a Psychological Force

Motivation is an inner energy that pushes you to do something because a need is not yet fulfilled.

DEFINITION Needs Needs are the essence of the marketing concept. Marketers do NOT create needs — they make consumers aware of needs. Consumer needs are the basis of all modern marketing. Smart companies define their business by the needs they satisfy, not just the products they sell. Motivation Motivation is the driving force within individuals that pushes them to take action. It is the set of forces that cause people to behave in certain ways. This driving force comes from a state of tension created by an unfulfilled need.
📌 Example A person feels thirsty (unfulfilled need) → feels tense → is driven to buy water (action) → thirst is gone (goal fulfilled). Dialog Sri Lanka markets mobile data by reminding you of your need to stay connected.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide This slide introduces the two most important words in this topic: 'needs' and 'motivation.' All coming slides will build on these two ideas.
SLIDE 4 — Model of the Motivation Process
A step-by-step diagram showing how a need turns into action and finally into goal fulfillment.
සිංහල idea
Need එකකින් action එකක් වෙන flow එක exam එකට වැදගත්.
•
Unfulfilled Need/Want → Tension.
•
Tension → Drive.
•
Drive + previous learning + thinking → Behavior.
•
Behavior → Goal/Need Fulfillment.
•
Goal fulfillment → Tension Reduction.
උදාහරණය: Hungry → McDonald's මතක් වෙනවා → යනවා → කනවා → tension අඩුවෙනවා.
Exam මතකය: Need → Tension → Drive → Behavior → Goal → Tension Reduction.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Model of the Motivation Process

A step-by-step diagram showing how a need turns into action and finally into goal fulfillment.

KEY CONCEPT The process flows in this order: 1. Unfulfilled Needs, Wants, and Desires → creates Tension 2. Tension → creates a Drive (urge to act) 3. Drive + Previous Learning + Cognitive Processes → shapes Behavior 4. Behavior → leads to Goal or Need Fulfillment 5. Goal Fulfillment → leads to Tension Reduction 6. Tension Reduction → feeds back into the cycle (new needs can emerge) Previous Learning and Cognitive Processes influence how the drive turns into behavior.
📌 Example You are hungry (unfulfilled need) → you feel tense → you remember McDonald's from last time (previous learning) → you decide to go there (behavior) → you eat a burger (goal fulfilled) → tension is reduced.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 3 explained what motivation IS. This slide shows HOW motivation works as a process — from an unmet need all the way to tension being reduced.
SLIDE 5 — Types of Needs — Innate & Acquired
There are different kinds of needs — some we are born with, others we learn from society.
සිංහල idea
Need දෙකේ difference එක born vs learned.
•
Innate = උපන් දවසේ ඉඳන් තියෙන body needs: food, water, shelter.
•
Acquired = culture/social life එකෙන් ඉගෙනගත් psychological needs: status, power, belonging.
උදාහරණය: Water ඕන = innate; iPhone එකෙන් status ඕන = acquired.
Exam මතකය: Born / Learned.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Types of Needs

There are different kinds of needs — some we are born with, others we learn from society.

DEFINITION Innate Needs Needs we are born with — also called primary needs or biogenic needs. Physiological needs that exist from birth. Also called biogenic needs or primary motives. Examples: food, water, clothing, sex, shelter. Acquired Needs Needs we learn from our culture and environment — also called secondary needs. Learned in response to our culture or environment. Generally psychological in nature. Also called secondary needs. Examples: esteem, prestige, power, sense of belonging.
📌 Example Innate: You MUST eat rice and curry every day to survive. Acquired: You WANT to buy an iPhone because your friends all have one and you want to feel accepted.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 3 said that needs create motivation. This slide now classifies those needs into two main types — innate (born with) and acquired (learned).
SLIDE 6 — Types of Needs — Extrinsic & Intrinsic
Two more types of needs based on whether the reward comes from outside or from inside you.
සිංහල idea
Need එකක් satisfy වෙලා නැති නිසා tension එකක් එනවා. ඒ tension එක reduce කරන්න action එකකට push කරන inner force එක motivation.
•
Marketers needs create කරන්නේ නෑ; තිබෙන need එක remind/activate කරනවා.
•
Need → tension → action drive.
උදාහරණය: Thirsty → tension → water buy → thirst reduce.
Exam මතකය: Motivation = driving force from an unfulfilled need.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Types of Needs — Extrinsic & Intrinsic

Two more types of needs based on whether the reward comes from outside or from inside you.

DEFINITION Extrinsic Needs Needs driven by external rewards — impressing others, gaining status, avoiding punishment. Motivates an individual to achieve an end result. Buying a product to show status or impress others. Extrinsic motivation = performing a behavior to earn a reward or avoid punishment. Intrinsic Needs Needs driven by personal satisfaction — doing something because YOU enjoy it. Involves engaging in a behavior because it is personally rewarding. Performing an activity for its own sake — not for an external reward.
📌 Example Extrinsic: Buying a Mercedes-Benz car to impress your neighbours. Intrinsic: Taking a cooking class at a Sri Lankan culinary school because you genuinely love cooking.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 5 showed innate vs acquired needs. This slide adds two more dimensions: extrinsic (from outside) vs intrinsic (from inside). Together, these 4 types give a complete picture of needs.
SLIDE 7 — Goals
Goals are the specific things we try to achieve in order to satisfy our needs.
සිංහල idea
Need එක satisfy කරන්න අපි reach කරන්න try කරන target එක goal.
•
Generic goal = need satisfy කරන general solution.
•
Product-specific goal = specific brand/product එකක්.
උදාහරණය: 'Thirst stop කරන්න drink එකක්' = generic; 'Coca-Cola එකක්' = product-specific.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Goals

Goals are the specific things we try to achieve in order to satisfy our needs.

DEFINITION Goals are the sought-after results of motivated behavior. Generic Goals General categories — a broad type of outcome that fulfills a need. General categories of goals that consumers see as ways to fulfill their needs. Not tied to a specific brand or product. Product-Specific Goals A specific brand or product chosen to fulfill a need. Specifically branded products or services that consumers select as their goals. Marketers are particularly concerned with product-specific goals.
📌 Example Generic goal: 'I want to quench my thirst.' Product-specific goal: 'I want to drink a Coca-Cola.' Keells supermarket targets both — it offers any drink (generic) but promotes its own brand water (product-specific).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 5 & 6 explained the types of needs. This slide introduces Goals — the destination that needs push us toward. Needs create motivation; goals are what that motivation aims for.
SLIDE 8 — Positive vs Negative Goals
Goals can pull you TOWARD something you want, or push you AWAY from something you don't want.
සිංහල idea
Positive goal එකට අපි move toward කරනවා; negative goal එකෙන් away වෙන්න try කරනවා.
•
Positive = desired result reach කරන්න.
•
Negative = bad result avoid කරන්න.
උදාහරණය: Shampoo buy කරලා good hair ගන්න = positive; helmet buy කරලා injury avoid කරන්න = negative.
Exam මතකය: Toward / Away.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Positive vs Negative Goals

Goals can pull you TOWARD something you want, or push you AWAY from something you don't want.

KEY CONCEPT Motives or needs can have a positive OR negative direction. Like needs, goals can be positive or negative. A POSITIVE GOAL is one toward which behavior is directed — you move TOWARD it. A NEGATIVE GOAL is one from which behavior is directed away — you move AWAY from it.
📌 Example Positive goal: Buying Sunsilk shampoo to get beautiful hair (moving TOWARD beauty). Negative goal: Buying a helmet to avoid injury when riding a motorbike (moving AWAY from danger).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 7 introduced goals. This slide now adds an important dimension: the direction of goals. Goals are not just things you want — they can also be things you want to avoid.
SLIDE 9 — The Selection of Goals
The goals a person picks depend on many personal and social factors.
සිංහල idea
Need එක satisfy කරන්න අපි reach කරන්න try කරන target එක goal.
•
Generic goal = need satisfy කරන general solution.
•
Product-specific goal = specific brand/product එකක්.
උදාහරණය: 'Thirst stop කරන්න drink එකක්' = generic; 'Coca-Cola එකක්' = product-specific.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Selection of Goals

The goals a person picks depend on many personal and social factors.

KEY CONCEPT The goals selected by an individual depend on their: Personal experiences — what they have been through before. Physical capacity — what their body can actually do. Prevailing cultural norms and values — what is normal or acceptable in their society. Goal's accessibility in the physical and social environment — whether the goal is actually available to them.
📌 Example A Sri Lankan student may set the goal of studying abroad (personal ambition) but is limited by financial capacity and whether a visa is accessible — all these factors shape the final goal they choose.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 8 showed that goals can be positive or negative. This slide now explains WHAT determines which goals a person actually picks — it is not random, it depends on experience, culture, and environment.
SLIDE 10 — Rational versus Emotional Motives
Sometimes people buy with logic; sometimes they buy with feelings.
සිංහල idea
Buying reason එක logic ද feeling ද කියන difference එක.
•
Rational = price, size, quality, performance වගේ objective facts.
•
Emotional = pride, love, fear, status වගේ feelings.
උදාහරණය: Cheapest rice brand choose = rational; child happy කරන්න expensive toy buy = emotional.
Exam මතකය: Facts / Feelings.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Rational versus Emotional Motives

Sometimes people buy with logic; sometimes they buy with feelings.

DEFINITION Rational Motives Buying based on objective facts like price, weight, and size. Assumes consumers behave rationally — they carefully consider all alternatives. They choose options that give the greatest utility (best value). Consumers select goals based on totally objective criteria: size, weight, price, etc. Emotional Motives Buying based on feelings like pride, love, fear, and status. The selection of goals is based on personal or subjective criteria. Examples of emotional criteria: affection, status, pride, fear.
📌 Example Rational: Choosing the cheapest rice brand at Keells because it offers the most kilos for the money. Emotional: Buying a Disney toy for your child because you love seeing them happy — price is not the main factor.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 7–9 covered how goals are selected. This slide adds a new layer: the TYPE of reasoning behind goal selection — rational (logical) vs emotional (feelings-based).
SLIDE 11 — The Dynamics of Motivation
Motivation is not fixed — it keeps changing as needs are satisfied and new ones appear.
සිංහල idea
Need එකක් satisfy උනාම motivation ඉවර වෙන්නේ නෑ. අලුත් need එකක් එනවා.
•
Needs never fully end.
•
Old goal reach උනාම new/higher goal set කරනවා.
•
Life experience අනුව motivation change වෙනවා.
උදාහරණය: Job → promotion → own business.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Dynamics of Motivation

Motivation is not fixed — it keeps changing as needs are satisfied and new ones appear.

KEY CONCEPT Motivation is a highly dynamic construct — it constantly changes in reaction to life experiences. Needs are NEVER fully satisfied — there is always something more we want. New needs emerge as old needs are satisfied — once one need is met, another appears. People who achieve their goals set new and higher goals for themselves.
📌 Example A Sri Lankan graduate gets a job (need for income satisfied) → now wants a promotion (new need for status) → gets promoted → now wants to start their own business. The cycle never ends — just like climbing Maslow's pyramid.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Previous slides showed how needs and goals work. This slide shows that motivation is NOT a one-time event — it is a continuous, ever-changing cycle throughout life.
SLIDE 12 — Substitute Goals
When you cannot reach your main goal, you pick a replacement goal that still reduces tension.
සිංහල idea
Need එක satisfy කරන්න අපි reach කරන්න try කරන target එක goal.
•
Generic goal = need satisfy කරන general solution.
•
Product-specific goal = specific brand/product එකක්.
උදාහරණය: 'Thirst stop කරන්න drink එකක්' = generic; 'Coca-Cola එකක්' = product-specific.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Substitute Goals

When you cannot reach your main goal, you pick a replacement goal that still reduces tension.

DEFINITION Used when a consumer cannot attain a specific goal they anticipated would satisfy a need. The substitute goal will dispel (reduce) the tension caused by the unfulfilled need. Substitute goals may actually REPLACE the primary goal over time.
📌 Example You want to buy a BMW (primary goal) but cannot afford it → you buy a Toyota (substitute goal). Over time, you may be fully happy with the Toyota and forget about the BMW.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 11 said needs are never fully satisfied and we always set new goals. This slide introduces what happens when you CANNOT reach a goal — you use a substitute instead of giving up.
SLIDE 13 — Frustration
When a goal cannot be reached, a person may feel frustrated and react in different ways.
සිංහල idea
Main goal එක reach කරන්න බැරි උනොත් replacement goal එකක් ගන්න පුළුවන්. Goal එක reach වෙන්නේ නැත්නම් frustration එන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Substitute goal = main goal වෙනුවට tension reduce කරන වෙන option එක.
•
Frustration = goal blocked වෙනකොට ඇතිවන negative feeling.
උදාහරණය: BMW afford බැරි → Toyota buy = substitute.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Frustration

When a goal cannot be reached, a person may feel frustrated and react in different ways.

DEFINITION Failure to achieve a goal may result in frustration. Frustration may be due to personal limitations (limited resources, skills, or finances). OR it may be due to an obstacle in the physical or social environment. Some people adapt to frustration and find new solutions. Others adopt defense mechanisms to protect their ego from the pain of failure.
📌 Example A student applies to the University of Colombo but does not get in (frustrated) → Some adapt by applying to another university; others use defense mechanisms like blaming the exam system.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 12 showed that substitute goals help when the primary goal is unavailable. This slide goes deeper — if even the substitute does not work, the result is frustration, which leads to defense mechanisms (next slide).
SLIDE 14 — Defense Mechanisms
Mental strategies people use to protect their feelings when they fail to reach a goal.
සිංහල idea
Frustration handle කරන්න mind එක use කරන protection methods.
•
Aggression = anger පිට කරනවා.
•
Rationalization = excuse හදනවා.
•
Regression = childish behaviour.
•
Withdrawal = situation එකෙන් අයින් වෙනවා.
•
Projection = blame others.
•
Daydreaming = fantasy එකට යනවා.
•
Identification = successful person copy කරනවා.
•
Repression = painful memory push away කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Exam fail වෙලා 'paper unfair' කියන එක rationalization වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Meaning දන්නවා නම් term අමතක උනත් simple explanation එකෙන් ලියන්න.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Defense Mechanisms

Mental strategies people use to protect their feelings when they fail to reach a goal.

KEY CONCEPT Types of Defense Mechanisms: Aggression — taking out frustration through anger or hostility. Rationalization — making up logical-sounding excuses for failure. Regression — going back to childish behavior when frustrated. Withdrawal — giving up and avoiding the situation entirely. Projection — blaming others for your own failures. Daydreaming — escaping into fantasies instead of dealing with the problem. Identification — copying successful people to feel better about yourself. Repression — pushing the painful memory out of your conscious mind.
📌 Example Rationalization: 'I didn't get the job at SriLankan Airlines because the interview was unfair' (instead of admitting lack of preparation). Projection: 'My friend made me fail the exam' (instead of taking personal responsibility).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 13 explained frustration. This slide lists the 8 specific defense mechanisms that people use when frustration becomes too strong to handle directly.
SLIDE 15 — Arousal of Motives — Overview
Motives don't just happen on their own — they are triggered (aroused) by different types of factors.
සිංහල idea
Motivation activate වෙන trigger 4ක්.
•
Physiological = hunger/thirst/fatigue.
•
Emotional = fear/love/pride.
•
Cognitive = new information/thinking.
•
Environmental = ad, discount, smell, social influence.
උදාහරණය: 50% OFF board එක දැකලා buy urge එකක් එනවා = environmental arousal.
Exam මතකය: Body / Feeling / Thinking / Outside.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Arousal of Motives

Motives don't just happen on their own — they are triggered (aroused) by different types of factors.

KEY CONCEPT There are 4 types of arousal: Physiological Arousal — triggered by the body. Emotional Arousal — triggered by feelings. Cognitive Arousal — triggered by thinking. Environmental Arousal — triggered by the outside world.
📌 Example Think of these 4 types as 4 different switches that can turn on your motivation to buy something — your body, your feelings, your thoughts, or something you see/hear around you.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 11–14 showed how motivation changes and what happens when it fails. This slide introduces a new question: What STARTS motivation in the first place? The answer is arousal.
SLIDE 16 — Arousal of Motives — Physiological & Emotional
Your body and your emotions are two powerful triggers of motivation to buy or act.
සිංහල idea
Motivation activate වෙන trigger 4ක්.

Physiological = hunger/thirst/fatigue.
•
Emotional = fear/love/pride.
•
Cognitive = new information/thinking.

Environmental = ad, discount, smell, social influence.

Arousal of Motives — Physiological & Emotional

Your body and your emotions are two powerful triggers of motivation to buy or act.

DEFINITION Physiological Arousal Motives triggered by biological or physical needs — hunger, thirst, fatigue. Motives are triggered by biological or physical needs such as hunger, thirst, or fatigue. Example: Feeling hungry leads you to buy food or visit a restaurant. Emotional Arousal Motives that arise from feelings — fear, love, pride, or anxiety. Motives arise from feelings and emotions such as fear, love, pride, or anxiety. Example: Purchasing a life insurance policy due to fear of future uncertainty.
📌 Example Physiological: Walking past a kottu roti stall, smelling the food, and immediately buying some because you feel hungry. Emotional: Buying Apple AirPods because you feel proud wearing them and it reduces your anxiety about fitting in.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 15 listed all 4 types of arousal. This slide explains the first two — physiological (body) and emotional (feelings) — with specific examples for each.
SLIDE 17 — Arousal of Motives — Cognitive & Environmental
Your thoughts and your surroundings can also trigger the desire to buy or act.
සිංහල idea
Motivation activate වෙන trigger 4ක්.
•
Physiological = hunger/thirst/fatigue.
•
Emotional = fear/love/pride.
•
Cognitive = new information/thinking.
•
Environmental = ad, discount, smell, social influence.
උදාහරණය: 50% OFF board එක දැකලා buy urge එකක් එනවා = environmental arousal.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Arousal of Motives — Cognitive & Environmental

Your thoughts and your surroundings can also trigger the desire to buy or act.

DEFINITION Cognitive Arousal Motives activated by the process of thinking — learning new information triggers action. Motives are activated through the process of thinking. Example: Learning about the health benefits of organic food motivates a consumer to buy it. Environmental Arousal Motives stimulated by external factors — ads, social influence, surroundings. Motives are stimulated by external environmental factors such as advertisements, social influence, or surroundings. Example: Seeing a promotional advertisement or a discount offer encourages a person to purchase.
📌 Example Cognitive: Reading a health article about green tea motivates you to buy Dilmah organic tea. Environmental: Seeing a '50% OFF' banner at a Keells supermarket triggers you to buy something you didn't plan to buy.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 16 covered the first two types of arousal (body and feelings). This slide completes the picture with the remaining two — cognitive (thinking) and environmental (surroundings).
SLIDE 18 — Philosophies — Behaviorist School
This school says people react to their environment — they don't think deeply; they respond to rewards and punishments.
සිංහල idea
Motivation/behaviour explain කරන thinking styles දෙකක්.
•
Behaviorist = environment/reward/punishment වලට response කරනවා.
•
Cognitive = person think කරලා beliefs/expectations අනුව act කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Outside response / Inside thinking.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Behaviorist School of Thought

This school says people react to their environment — they don't think deeply; they respond to rewards and punishments.

KEY CONCEPT Behavior is LEARNED through stimulus–response (S–R) connections. Elements of conscious thought are to be IGNORED. The consumer does NOT act — the consumer REACTS. This approach argues that motives are aroused by EXTERNAL STIMULI in the environment. Human behavior is largely a response to rewards, punishments, and conditioning.
📌 Example Stimulus: McDonald's runs a 'Buy 1 Get 1 Free' promotion (external stimulus). Response: You go to McDonald's even though you were not hungry — you reacted to the reward, not a conscious decision.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 15–17 listed the 4 types of arousal. This slide introduces the FIRST philosophy explaining WHY arousal happens — the Behaviorist view that external triggers cause automatic reactions.
SLIDE 19 — Philosophies — Cognitive School
This school says people THINK before they act — their behavior is driven by internal mental processes like beliefs and expectations.
සිංහල idea
Motivation/behaviour explain කරන thinking styles දෙකක්.
•
Behaviorist = environment/reward/punishment වලට response කරනවා.
•
Cognitive = person think කරලා beliefs/expectations අනුව act කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Outside response / Inside thinking.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Cognitive School of Thought

This school says people THINK before they act — their behavior is driven by internal mental processes like beliefs and expectations.

KEY CONCEPT Behavior is DIRECTED AT GOAL ACHIEVEMENT — not just a reaction. Needs and past experiences are reasoned, categorized, and transformed into attitudes and beliefs. This approach emphasizes that motives are aroused by INTERNAL mental processes: thinking, perception, beliefs, and expectations.
📌 Example A person thinks 'I believe Dialog has the best network coverage in Sri Lanka' (internal belief) → this thought motivates them to choose Dialog over Mobitel — not just because of an ad, but because of their own reasoning.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 18 presented the Behaviorist view: external stimuli cause reactions. This slide presents the OPPOSITE view — the Cognitive School says internal thinking drives behavior. These two schools are contrasting philosophies.
SLIDE 20 — Types of Needs — Researchers' Systems
Researchers have developed organized lists and models to classify all types of human needs.
සිංහල idea
Need එකක් satisfy වෙලා නැති නිසා tension එකක් එනවා. ඒ tension එක reduce කරන්න action එකකට push කරන inner force එක motivation.
•
Marketers needs create කරන්නේ නෑ; තිබෙන need එක remind/activate කරනවා.
•
Need → tension → action drive.
උදාහරණය: Thirsty → tension → water buy → thirst reduce.
Exam මතකය: Motivation = driving force from an unfulfilled need.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Types of Needs — Research Systems

Researchers have developed organized lists and models to classify all types of human needs.

KEY CONCEPT Three major systems of needs studied in Consumer Behaviour: Henry Murray's 28 psychogenic needs — a detailed list of psychological needs. Abraham Maslow's hierarchy of needs — a pyramid model ranking needs by priority. A trio of needs — three core needs: power, affiliation, and achievement. Note: Basic biological needs are easy to agree upon — everyone needs food and water. Psychological and psychosocial needs differ from researcher to researcher — that is why multiple systems exist.
📌 Example Think of these three systems as three different textbooks written by three different scholars — each one has its own way of organizing and explaining human needs.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 18–19 covered the philosophical schools of thought. This slide introduces the third learning objective: the formal SYSTEMS used to classify needs. The next slides will cover each system in detail.
SLIDE 21 — Murray's List of Psychogenic Needs (Part 1)
Henry Murray identified 28 psychological needs and grouped them into categories.
සිංහල idea
Need එකක් satisfy වෙලා නැති නිසා tension එකක් එනවා. ඒ tension එක reduce කරන්න action එකකට push කරන inner force එක motivation.
•
Marketers needs create කරන්නේ නෑ; තිබෙන need එක remind/activate කරනවා.
•
Need → tension → action drive.
උදාහරණය: Thirsty → tension → water buy → thirst reduce.

Murray's List of Psychogenic Needs

Henry Murray identified 28 psychological needs and grouped them into categories.

CategoryNeeds
Needs Associated with Inanimate ObjectsAcquisition, Conservancy, Order, Retention, Construction
Needs Reflecting Ambition, Power, Accomplishment, and PrestigeSuperiority, Achievement, Recognition, Exhibition, Infavoidance
Needs Connected with Human PowerDominance, Deferrence, Similance, Autonomy, Contrariance
📌 Example Need for Order: A person obsessively organizes their spice rack at home. Need for Recognition: Posting every achievement on Facebook to get likes and comments from others.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 20 introduced the three research systems. This slide begins Murray's system — a detailed list of 28 psychogenic (psychological) needs organized into specific categories.

Murray's List of Psychogenic Needs (continued)

More categories from Murray's 28 psychogenic needs framework.

CategoryNeeds
Sado-Masochistic NeedsAggression, Abasement
Needs Concerned with Affection between PeopleAffiliation, Rejection, Nurturance, Succorance, Play
Needs Concerned with Social IntercourseCognizance, Exposition
📌 Example Affiliation need: Joining a university sports team just to belong to a group and make friends. Nurturance need: A mother buying extra snacks for her children even when they did not ask.

SLIDE 23 — Maslow's Hierarchy of Needs
Abraham Maslow organized all human needs into a pyramid — lower needs must be satisfied before higher ones become important.
සිංහල idea
Human needs lower level ඉඳන් higher level දක්වා pyramid එකක් වගේ arrange කරලා තියෙන model එක.
•
Physiological.
•
Safety.
•
Social/Belonging.
•
Esteem.
•
Self-Actualization.
උදාහරණය: Hungry කෙනාට luxury status ad එකට වඩා food need එක first.
Exam මතකය: Body → Safety → Belong → Esteem → Full potential.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Maslow's Hierarchy of Needs

Abraham Maslow organized all human needs into a pyramid — lower needs must be satisfied before higher ones become important.

LevelType of NeedExamples
Level 5 (Top)Self-Actualization — Self-fulfillmentBecoming the best version of yourself, reaching your full potential
Level 4Ego Needs — Prestige, status, self-esteemA promotion at work, winning an award, being respected
Level 3Social Needs — Affection, friendship, belongingHaving close friends, feeling part of a group or community
Level 2Safety and Security Needs — Protection, order, stabilityHome security system, health insurance, stable job
Level 1 (Base)Physiological Needs — Food, water, air, shelter, sexDaily meals, drinking water, a place to sleep
📌 Example A daily-wage worker in Colombo first focuses on buying rice (Level 1 — physiological). Once income is stable, they get a locked home (Level 2 — safety). Later, they join a community group (Level 3 — social).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 21–22 covered Murray's flat list of 28 needs. Maslow's model is different — it RANKS needs in a hierarchy (pyramid) and says lower needs must be met before higher needs become relevant.
SLIDE 24 — A Trio of Needs
Three important needs that are closely linked to personality and motivation in consumer behaviour.
සිංහල idea
Consumer behavior එකට ගොඩක් use වෙන social needs 3ක්.
•
Power = control/influence කරන්න ඕන.
•
Affiliation = people එක්ක belong වෙන්න ඕන.
•
Achievement = success/goal reach කරන්න ඕන.
උදාහරණය: Manager decision control = power; club join = affiliation; business grow = achievement.
Exam මතකය: Power / Friends / Win.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
A Trio of Needs

Three important needs that are closely linked to personality and motivation in consumer behaviour.

KEY CONCEPT Power The need to control your environment and influence others. An individual's desire to control their environment. People with high power needs want to influence or dominate others. Affiliation The need to connect with others — friendship, acceptance, and belonging. Need for friendship, acceptance, and belonging. People with high affiliation needs are very social and relationship-focused. Achievement The need to accomplish personal goals and succeed. Need for personal accomplishment. Closely related to egoistic needs (ego) and self-actualization needs (Maslow's top two levels).
📌 Example Power: A manager who always insists on making every decision alone. Affiliation: A university student who joins every club just to have more friends. Achievement: A Sri Lankan entrepreneur who works 16 hours a day to grow their business.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 23 presented Maslow's hierarchy (5 broad levels). This slide presents a simpler 3-need model — Power, Affiliation, Achievement — which focuses on social and personal motivations.
SLIDE 25 — Measurement of Motives — Introduction
Motives are hard to measure because they are invisible, personal, and psychological.
සිංහල idea
Need එකක් satisfy උනාම motivation ඉවර වෙන්නේ නෑ. අලුත් need එකක් එනවා.
•
Needs never fully end.
•
Old goal reach උනාම new/higher goal set කරනවා.
•
Life experience අනුව motivation change වෙනවා.
උදාහරණය: Job → promotion → own business.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Measurement of Motives

Motives are hard to measure because they are invisible, personal, and psychological.

KEY CONCEPT Question 1: How are motives identified? Question 2: How are they measured? Question 3: Which motives are responsible for certain kinds of behaviour? Why is this hard? Motives are intangible — you cannot see or touch them. Motives are psychological — they exist only in the mind. Motives are hypothetical — we guess they exist based on behavior.
📌 Example Measuring hunger is easy — a person will say 'I'm hungry.' But measuring WHY someone chose a Rolex over a Seiko is hard — it could be pride, status, or habit. You cannot just ask and expect the full truth.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 20–24 identified and classified different types of needs. This slide now moves to the fourth learning objective: how do researchers actually MEASURE and STUDY these motives?
SLIDE 26 — Measurement of Motives — Methods
Researchers use a combination of techniques because no single method is fully reliable.
සිංහල idea
Motivation activate වෙන trigger 4ක්.
•
Physiological = hunger/thirst/fatigue.
•
Emotional = fear/love/pride.
•
Cognitive = new information/thinking.
•
Environmental = ad, discount, smell, social influence.
උදාහරණය: 50% OFF board එක දැකලා buy urge එකක් එනවා = environmental arousal.
Exam මතකය: Body / Feeling / Thinking / Outside.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Measurement of Motives — Methods

Researchers use a combination of techniques because no single method is fully reliable.

DEFINITION Consumers may be UNAWARE of their motives — they don't always know WHY they buy. Consumers may be UNWILLING to reveal their motives — they may feel embarrassed or private. No single measurement method can be used as a reliable index on its own. Researchers rely on a COMBINATION of techniques. Qualitative research is widely used to identify consumers' unconscious or hidden motivations. Projective techniques are often very successful in identifying motives.
📌 Example A shopper says 'I buy Anchor butter because it tastes better' — but the real motive might be that their mother always bought Anchor and they associate it with comfort. Qualitative research can uncover this hidden truth.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 25 explained WHY motives are hard to measure. This slide explains HOW researchers overcome that difficulty — using qualitative methods and projective techniques rather than just simple surveys.

•
Physiological = hunger/thirst/fatigue.
•
Emotional = fear/love/pride.
•
Cognitive = new information/thinking.
•
Environmental = ad, discount, smell, social influence.
උදාහරණය: 50% OFF board එක දැකලා buy urge එකක් එනවා = environmental arousal.
