Qualitative Measures of Motives

Five key qualitative tools researchers use to uncover hidden or unconscious consumer motivations.

KEY CONCEPT Five qualitative research techniques: (1) Metaphor Analysis (2) Storytelling (3) Word Association and Sentence Completion (4) Drawing Pictures and Photo Sorts (5) Thematic Apperception Test (TAT)
📌 Example These 5 techniques are like 5 different ways to open a locked box (the consumer's hidden mind). Each key works differently, but they all aim to reveal the same thing — the TRUE motivation behind buying behavior.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 26 said researchers use a COMBINATION of techniques. This slide lists those techniques specifically — the 5 qualitative tools most commonly used.
SLIDE 28 — Metaphor Analysis
A research technique that finds hidden feelings by studying the symbols and comparisons people use to describe their experiences.
සිංහල idea
Metaphor Analysis කියන්නේ consumerගේ hidden feelings/motives direct අහන්නේ නැතුව images, comparisons, symbolic ideas use කරලා ඇතුළේ meaning එක හොයන qualitative method එක.
Simple English idea: A research technique that finds hidden feelings by studying the symbols and comparisons people use to describe their experiences.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Metaphor Analysis

A research technique that finds hidden feelings by studying the symbols and comparisons people use to describe their experiences.

DEFINITION Metaphor Analysis is a qualitative research technique. It interprets individuals' underlying thoughts and emotions. It examines the symbolic language and comparisons they use to describe experiences. It reveals hidden feelings, attitudes, and beliefs. It also reveals personal meanings attached to products, brands, or experiences.
📌 Example A consumer says 'My iPhone is like my best friend — it is always there for me.' The metaphor 'best friend' reveals a hidden emotional attachment to the product — not just satisfaction, but deep loyalty and trust.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 27 listed all 5 qualitative techniques. This slide now zooms into Technique 1 — Metaphor Analysis — and explains exactly how it works to uncover hidden consumer feelings.
SLIDE 29 — Word Association and Sentence Completion
Two quick techniques that reveal a consumer's instant emotional or mental reactions to brands and topics.
සිංහල idea
Word Association / Sentence Completion කියන්නේ word එකක් හෝ incomplete sentence එකක් දීලා consumerට ඉක්මනින් response දෙන්න කියන method එක. Hidden feelings/motives හොයන්න use කරනවා.
Simple English idea: Two quick techniques that reveal a consumer's instant emotional or mental reactions to brands and topics.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Word Association and Sentence Completion

Two quick techniques that reveal a consumer's instant emotional or mental reactions to brands and topics.

DEFINITION Word Association Give a stimulus word — the participant says the FIRST word that comes to mind. Participants are given a stimulus word and asked to respond with the FIRST word that comes to mind. Reveals subconscious associations with brands and concepts. Examples of stimulus words: Quality, Luxury, Fast Food, Bank, Premium Price. Sentence Completion Give an incomplete sentence — the participant finishes it in their own words. Participants are given incomplete sentences and asked to complete them in their own words. Examples: 'I prefer this brand because ……' 'Shopping online is ……' 'Advertisements are ……'
📌 Example Researcher says 'Fast Food' → Sri Lankan student instantly says 'McDonald's' → this reveals that McDonald's dominates the fast-food space in that student's mind, even in Sri Lanka. Sentence completion: 'Shopping at Keells is ____' → 'expensive but reliable.'
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 28 explained Metaphor Analysis. This slide covers TWO more techniques — Word Association and Sentence Completion — which are faster, more structured ways to reveal subconscious consumer thoughts.
SLIDE 30 — Thematic Apperception Test (TAT)
A projective technique where participants create stories from ambiguous pictures — revealing their hidden motives, emotions, and attitudes.
සිංහල idea
TAT කියන්නේ picture එකක් දීලා ඒ ගැන story එකක් හදන්න කියන projective technique එක. Consumer direct කියන්නේ නැති hidden motives story එකෙන් හොයනවා.
Simple English idea: A projective technique where participants create stories from ambiguous pictures — revealing their hidden motives, emotions, and attitudes.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Thematic Apperception Test (TAT)

A projective technique where participants create stories from ambiguous pictures — revealing their hidden motives, emotions, and attitudes.

DEFINITION TAT is a qualitative PROJECTIVE technique. Used to uncover an individual's hidden motives, attitudes, emotions, feelings, and personality traits. Participants are shown AMBIGUOUS PICTURES and asked to create stories based on them. Participants may be asked to describe: Who are the characters in the picture? What is happening in the picture? What led to this situation? What will happen next?
EXAM FOCUS TAT = Projective technique — people PROJECT their own feelings onto ambiguous images. The stories participants tell actually reveal THEIR OWN hidden motivations and emotions. It is used when consumers are unable or unwilling to directly state their true feelings.
📌 Example A researcher shows a vague image of a person standing in a supermarket aisle looking confused. One consumer says 'She is stressed because she cannot afford what she wants' — this projects the consumer's OWN financial anxiety onto the image.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 28–29 covered Metaphor Analysis, Word Association, and Sentence Completion. This final slide presents the TAT — the most powerful projective technique — and completes the full set of qualitative measurement tools for consumer motives.

What is Personality?

Personality is the collection of inner mental qualities that make every person unique and shape how they think, feel, and act.

DEFINITION Personality consists of the inner psychological characteristics that both determine and reflect how we think and act. The emphasis is on INNER characteristics — specific qualities, attributes, traits, factors, and mannerisms. These inner qualities distinguish one individual from all other individuals. How personality affects consumers: Personality influences the individual's product choices. It affects how consumers respond to marketers' promotional efforts. It determines when, where, and how they consume many products or services.
📌 Example One person's personality makes them always choose the cheapest option; another's personality drives them to always pick premium brands like Apple — the same ad reaches both but affects them differently.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 1 introduced the topic title. This slide now defines exactly what personality means — both in general psychology and in the context of consumer behaviour.
SLIDE 3 — The Nature of Personality
Personality has three key characteristics — and understanding them helps marketers predict and influence consumer behaviour.
සිංහල idea
Personality කියන්නේ එක් එක් කෙනා unique කරන, ඔහු හිතන/feel කරන/act කරන විදිහ shape කරන inner psychological characteristics set එක.
•

•
Generally consistent/enduring.
•

Exam මතකය: Inner traits → different consumer choices.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Nature of Personality

Personality has three key characteristics — and understanding them helps marketers predict and influence consumer behaviour.

KEY CONCEPT 1. Personality Reflects Individual Differences Every person's personality is unique — no two people are identical. Each individual has a unique combination of traits, making them different from everyone else. 2. Personality is Consistent and Enduring Personality stays stable over time — people behave the same way in similar situations. A person's personality does not change every day — it is relatively stable and long-lasting. 3. Personality Can Change Despite being stable, personality can change due to major life events or personal growth. Major events like marriage, illness, or a career change can alter personality over time.
DEFINITION Understanding personality helps marketers determine: Brand and store preferences — which brands or shops a consumer likes. Media usage patterns — whether they watch TV, use social media, or read newspapers. Susceptibility to persuasion — how easily a consumer can be influenced by advertising.
📌 Example A person with a consistently adventurous personality will always prefer Dialog's boldest data plans and be the first to try new tech gadgets — year after year, the same pattern.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 2 defined personality. This slide explains the NATURE of personality — 3 key characteristics that all marketers must understand before applying personality to marketing strategy.

Generally consistent/enduring.

Exam මතකය: Inner traits → different consumer choices.

Theories of Personality

Three major theories explain where personality comes from and what shapes it.

KEY CONCEPT
📌 Example Freudian: A perfume ad using sensual imagery appeals to unconscious drives. Neo-Freudian: A person becomes extroverted because of their social friendships. Trait Theory: A survey measures how adventurous a consumer is on a scale of 1–10.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 3 explained the nature of personality. This slide introduces the THREE theories that explain its origins and structure. The next slides explain each theory in full detail.
SLIDE 5 — Freudian Theory
Sigmund Freud believed that hidden, unconscious drives — especially biological ones — are the real reason people think and act the way they do.
සිංහල idea
අපිට conscious නැති hidden/unconscious drives — especially biological drives — behaviour එකට බලපානවා කියන idea එක.
•
ID = immediate pleasure ඕන part.
•
EGO = reality බලලා balance කරන part.
•
SUPEREGO = right/wrong, moral rules.
උදාහරණය: Perfume ad එක unconscious attraction/desire target කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Hidden drives.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Freudian Theory

Sigmund Freud believed that hidden, unconscious drives — especially biological ones — are the real reason people think and act the way they do.

DEFINITION The premise: unconscious needs or drives, especially sexual and other biological drives, are at the heart of human motivation and personality. Freud built his theory based on patients' recollections of early childhood experiences. He also studied the analysis of their dreams. And the specific nature of their mental and physical adjustment problems. The three systems of human personality (ID, EGO, SUPEREGO):
📌 Example ID: You desperately want to eat a luxurious chocolate cake right now. SUPEREGO: Your conscience says 'you are on a diet — it is wrong to eat that.' EGO: You compromise and buy a small piece to satisfy both sides.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 4 introduced all 3 personality theories. This slide zooms into the FIRST theory — Freudian Theory — and explains its core idea (unconscious drives) and the 3 systems of personality (ID, Ego, Superego).
SLIDE 6 — Freudian Stages of Personality Development
Freud believed personality is shaped as a person grows through 5 specific stages from infancy to adulthood.
සිංහල idea
Personality කියන්නේ එක් එක් කෙනා unique කරන, ඔහු හිතන/feel කරන/act කරන විදිහ shape කරන inner psychological characteristics set එක.
•
Individual differences තියෙනවා.
•
Generally consistent/enduring.
•
Major life events නිසා change වෙන්නත් පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Inner traits → different consumer choices.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Freudian Stages of Personality Development

Freud believed personality is shaped as a person grows through 5 specific stages from infancy to adulthood.

KEY CONCEPT An individual's personality is formed as he/she passes through a number of stages of infant and childhood development:
📌 Example A person who had insecurity in the Oral Stage may grow up with a habit of comfort eating when stressed — buying snacks like Milo or biscuits to emotionally self-soothe (a Freudian personality influence on buying behaviour).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 5 introduced Freudian Theory and the ID/Ego/Superego model. This slide adds another dimension — the 5 developmental STAGES — showing that personality is also shaped by the experiences of childhood growth.
SLIDE 7 — Freudian Theory — Marketing Application
Marketers use Freudian ideas to tap into consumers' unconscious desires and 'primal forces' through advertising.
සිංහල idea
අපිට conscious නැති hidden/unconscious drives — especially biological drives — behaviour එකට බලපානවා කියන idea එක.
•
ID = immediate pleasure ඕන part.
•
EGO = reality බලලා balance කරන part.
•
SUPEREGO = right/wrong, moral rules.
උදාහරණය: Perfume ad එක unconscious attraction/desire target කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Hidden drives.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Freudian Theory in Marketing

Marketers use Freudian ideas to tap into consumers' unconscious desires and 'primal forces' through advertising.

KEY CONCEPT Advertisements can appeal to consumers' hidden, unconscious drives (the ID). Using mystery, desire, and excitement in ads connects to deep 'primal forces.' The Godiva chocolate ad uses sensual imagery and the tagline 'Every woman is one part DIVA' to appeal to unconscious desires for indulgence and luxury. 'Primal forces' = deep, instinctual emotional drives that people are often unaware of.
EXAM FOCUS Freudian advertising = appealing to the ID (unconscious desires), not rational thinking. Luxury, sensuality, and mystery in ads are all Freudian marketing techniques.
📌 Example A high-end perfume ad shows a glamorous, mysterious woman — it does not describe the scent at all. It appeals to the unconscious desire to be admired and desired (Freudian primal drive = ID).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 5–6 explained Freudian theory in detail. This slide shows the PRACTICAL application — how Freudian concepts are used in real advertising to reach consumers' unconscious minds.
SLIDE 8 — Neo-Freudian Personality Theory
These thinkers agreed with some of Freud's ideas but believed that SOCIAL RELATIONSHIPS — not just unconscious drives — shape personality.
සිංහල idea
Personality කියන්නේ එක් එක් කෙනා unique කරන, ඔහු හිතන/feel කරන/act කරන විදිහ shape කරන inner psychological characteristics set එක.
•
Individual differences තියෙනවා.
•
Generally consistent/enduring.
•
Major life events නිසා change වෙන්නත් පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Inner traits → different consumer choices.

Neo-Freudian Personality Theory

These thinkers agreed with some of Freud's ideas but believed that SOCIAL RELATIONSHIPS — not just unconscious drives — shape personality.

DEFINITION Neo-Freudian theory maintains that social relationships play a crucial role in the development of personality. Several of Freud's colleagues disagreed that personality is primarily instinctual and sexual in nature. Neo-Freudians believed social relationships are FUNDAMENTAL to the formation and development of personality. Karen Horney's 3 Personality Groups:
📌 Example Compliant: A consumer buys the same brand as their friends to feel accepted. Aggressive: A person buys a luxury BMW to win admiration at work. Detached: A minimalist who avoids brands and buys only what they need, alone.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 5–7 covered Freudian Theory (unconscious drives). This slide introduces Neo-Freudian Theory — which ADDS social relationships as a key driver of personality, challenging Freud's purely instinctual view.
SLIDE 9 — Trait Theory
Personality can be measured and scored using specific psychological traits — this is a scientific, data-based approach.
සිංහල idea
Personality එක measurable psychological traits ගොඩක් එකතුවක් විදිහට බලන approach එක.
•
Traits score/measure කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
High/low levels compare කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Marketers segments identify කරන්න use කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Openness 8/10 consumerට new-tech ad target කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Measurable traits.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Trait Theory

Personality can be measured and scored using specific psychological traits — this is a scientific, data-based approach.

DEFINITION Trait theory focuses on empirical (measurable) measures of personality in terms of specific psychological characteristics called 'traits.' Traits are personal characteristics or features that set one person apart from another person. Trait theorists use personality tests or inventories to pinpoint individual differences. Results show 'high' versus 'low' scores on specific traits.
📌 Example A personality test gives a consumer a score of 8/10 on 'openness to new experiences' — a marketer uses this to target them with ads for new product launches and innovations.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 8 covered Neo-Freudian Theory (social relationships). This slide introduces the THIRD theory — Trait Theory — which is more scientific and quantitative, measuring personality through scores on specific traits.
SLIDE 10 — Consumer Innovativeness
How willing a consumer is to try new products early — innovators are the first to adopt new things.
සිංහල idea
අලුත් product/service එක market එකට ආවම ඉක්මනින් try කරන්න consumer කොච්චර willing ද කියන එක.
•
High innovativeness = new things early try කරනවා.
•
Low = wait කරනවා, others බලලා පස්සේ buy කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: New phone launch day එකේම buy කරන කෙනා high innovativeness.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Consumer Innovativeness

How willing a consumer is to try new products early — innovators are the first to adopt new things.

DEFINITION Innovators are the first to try new products, product line extensions, and services because they are open to new ideas and practices. Innovativeness is the degree of a consumer's willingness to adopt new products and services shortly after they are introduced. Three levels of innovativeness:
📌 Example Global: A person who tries every new thing in life. Domain-specific: A Sri Lankan who always buys the latest Dialog phone plan but ignores new food products. Innovative behaviour: Queuing overnight to buy the new iPhone on launch day.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 9 introduced Trait Theory as a scientific measurement of personality. This slide presents the FIRST major consumer trait — Innovativeness — showing how openness to new products is a measurable personality dimension.
SLIDE 11 — Dogmatism
A personality trait that shows how open or closed a person is to new ideas, unfamiliar things, or information that contradicts their beliefs.
සිංහල idea
Mind එක අලුත් ideas වලට open ද closed ද කියන trait එක.
•
High dogmatism = beliefs strong, unfamiliar ideas accept කරන්න අමාරු.
•
Low dogmatism = flexible, new ideas hear කරන්න ready.
උදාහරණය: Strong Apple-only fan කෙනෙක් new unknown brand එක try නොකරන එක high dogmatism.
Exam මතකය: Open mind / Closed mind.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Dogmatism

A personality trait that shows how open or closed a person is to new ideas, unfamiliar things, or information that contradicts their beliefs.

DEFINITION Dogmatism reflects the degree of rigidity a person displays toward the unfamiliar. It also measures rigidity toward information that is contrary to his or her established beliefs.
📌 Example Low dogmatism: A Colombo student who tries a new restaurant every week and loves discovering new food. High dogmatism: A person who has bought the same brand of tea — Dilmah only — for 20 years and refuses to try anything else.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 10 covered Innovativeness (openness to new products). This slide introduces Dogmatism — a closely related trait that measures how OPEN or CLOSED a person's mind is to change and new information.
SLIDE 12 — Social Character
Whether a consumer is guided by their OWN values or by what OTHER PEOPLE think — this shapes their buying decisions.
සිංහල idea
Decision එක තමන්ගේ own values අනුවද, අනිත් people කියන දේ අනුවද ගන්නේ කියන difference එක.
•
Inner-directed = own standards.
•
Other-directed = social approval/others opinions.
Exam මතකය: Own mind / Other people.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Social Character

Whether a consumer is guided by their OWN values or by what OTHER PEOPLE think — this shapes their buying decisions.

DEFINITION Inner-Directedness These consumers rely on their own personal values when evaluating products. Rely on their own values and internal standards to evaluate new products. More likely to be innovators — they do not need others' approval before buying. Other-Directedness These consumers look to other people for guidance on what to buy. Look to others — friends, social norms, trends — when making purchase decisions. Less likely to be innovators — they wait to see what others do first.
📌 Example Inner-directed: A designer who buys unique, handmade Sri Lankan batik outfits based purely on personal taste. Other-directed: A teenager who only buys Adidas because all their friends wear it.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 11 covered Dogmatism (open vs closed mind). This slide introduces Social Character — another trait dimension that shows whether the source of a consumer's buying decisions is internal (self) or external (others).
SLIDE 13 — Need for Uniqueness
Some consumers actively want to stand out from the crowd — they buy things that make them different, not things that are popular.
සිංහල idea
Others වගේ නොවී different වෙන්න කැමති tendency එක.
•
Unique design/limited edition products attractive වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Mass popular product එක avoid කරන්නත් පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Limited-edition sneaker buy කරන්නේ crowd එකෙන් stand out වෙන්න.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Need for Uniqueness

Some consumers actively want to stand out from the crowd — they buy things that make them different, not things that are popular.

DEFINITION Need for uniqueness = an individual's pursuit of differentness relative to others. Achieved through the acquisition of consumer goods to enhance one's personal and social identity. Individuals with HIGH need for uniqueness adopt new products and brands quicker than others. They prefer creative products that counter conformity and are outside group norms. They avoid mainstream products that everyone else is buying.
📌 Example A Sri Lankan fashion student avoids buying mass-market clothes from Odel and instead seeks out rare, locally handmade batik designs that no one else has — their need for uniqueness drives this choice.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 12 showed Inner vs Other-directedness. This slide introduces Need for Uniqueness — closely related, but specifically about wanting to STAND OUT and avoid being the same as others through product choices.
SLIDE 14 — Optimum Stimulation Level (OSL)
People differ in how much novelty, complexity, and excitement they enjoy — this directly affects their shopping behaviour.
සිංහල idea
Personට comfortable/interesting වෙන්න ඕන excitement/newness level එක.
•
High OSL = variety/newness/complexity කැමති.
•
Low OSL = familiar/simple things කැමති.
උදාහරණය: High OSL consumer new restaurant, new gadgets try කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: How much stimulation is enough for me?
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Optimum Stimulation Level (OSL)

People differ in how much novelty, complexity, and excitement they enjoy — this directly affects their shopping behaviour.

DEFINITION Optimum Stimulation Level (OSL) = the degree to which people like novel, complex, and unusual experiences. Research shows: consumers seeking high OSL are more willing to take risks, try new products, be innovative, and seek high stimulation while shopping.
📌 Example High OSL: A tourist who skips all the familiar restaurants and hunts for the most unusual street food in Pettah market. Low OSL: A retiree who shops at the same Keells branch every Saturday and buys the same 10 items each time.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 13 covered Need for Uniqueness (wanting to stand out). This slide introduces OSL — how much stimulation and novelty a person's personality CRAVES, which drives their attraction to new and exciting products.
SLIDE 15 — Sensation Seeking
Some people's personalities crave intense, varied experiences — and they are willing to take risks just to feel those experiences.
සිංහල idea
Strong, new, exciting experience ඕන නිසා risk එකත් accept කරන්න ready trait එක.
•
Novel + intense experiences.
•
Risk-taking element එක OSLට වඩා clear.
උදාහරණය: Bungee jumping + new energy drinks try කරන කෙනා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Sensation Seeking

Some people's personalities crave intense, varied experiences — and they are willing to take risks just to feel those experiences.

DEFINITION Sensation seeking = the need for varied, novel, and complex sensations and experiences. Includes the willingness to take social and physical risks for the sake of those sensations. High sensation seekers are natural early adopters of exciting, edgy, or risky products.
📌 Example A young Sri Lankan extreme sports fan buys every new energy drink brand and tries bungee jumping and kitesurfing — all driven by their sensation-seeking personality trait.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 14 introduced Optimum Stimulation Level (desire for novelty and complexity). Sensation Seeking is closely related — but adds the key element of RISK-TAKING for the sake of new experiences, not just shopping excitement.
SLIDE 16 — Variety and Novelty Seeking
This trait measures how much a consumer actively seeks out variety and change in their buying behaviour — even when they are already satisfied.
සිංහල idea
Current product එක satisfactory උනත් change/newness ඕන නිසා වෙනස් කරන behaviour.
•
Exploratory Purchase = වෙනසකට new brand buy කරනවා.
•
Vicarious Exploration = buy නොකර info search කරනවා.
•
Use Innovativeness = already-owned product එක new way එකකට use කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Milo හොඳයි; වෙනසකට Nestea buy = exploratory. New phones බලනවා but buy නෑ = vicarious. Shopping bag plant pot = use innovativeness.
Exam මතකය: Buy new / Search only / Use differently.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Variety and Novelty Seeking

This trait measures how much a consumer actively seeks out variety and change in their buying behaviour — even when they are already satisfied.

DEFINITION Measures a consumer's degree of variety-seeking behaviour.
📌 Example Exploratory: A consumer who loves Milo but randomly buys Nestea one week just to try something different. Vicarious: Browsing every new phone model online but not buying. Use Innovativeness: Using a Keells shopping bag as a lunch bag.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 15 introduced Sensation Seeking (craving intense experiences). This slide introduces Variety Seeking — a more everyday version of novelty-seeking, focused specifically on changing brands and products for the sake of trying something different.
SLIDE 17 — Need for Cognition (NFC)
Some consumers LOVE to think deeply about products — others prefer simple, flashy ads. This trait determines which type of ad works best for each person.
සිංහල idea
Product ගැන deep thinking/details බලන්න consumer කැමතිද කියන trait එක.
•
High NFC = specs, details, evidence කැමති.
•
Low NFC = simple image, music, celebrity, quick message වලට react කරන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Laptop specs line by line බලන කෙනා high NFC.
Exam මතකය: Loves thinking / Prefers simple.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Need for Cognition (NFC)

Some consumers LOVE to think deeply about products — others prefer simple, flashy ads. This trait determines which type of ad works best for each person.

DEFINITION Need for Cognition (NFC) measures a person's craving for or enjoyment of thinking.
📌 Example High NFC: A consumer who reads every technical specification before buying a laptop online. Low NFC: A consumer who buys a shampoo just because a famous actress appeared in the ad — they did not read any product details.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 16 covered Variety Seeking (desire to try new things). This slide introduces Need for Cognition — which measures how much mental EFFORT a consumer enjoys putting into their purchase decisions, affecting which type of marketing message works for them.

Visualizers vs Verbalizers & Consumer Materialism

Two more personality traits: one about HOW consumers prefer to receive information, and one about HOW MUCH they value possessions.

DEFINITION Visualizers vs Verbalizers Do they prefer pictures or words? Consumer Materialism How much does a person value owning and showing off physical things? Materialism gauges the extent to which an individual is preoccupied with purchasing and showing off physical possessions. These possessions are mostly nonessential and often conspicuous luxury goods.
📌 Example Visualizer: A consumer who scrolls through Instagram for product photos before buying. Verbalizer: A consumer who reads every line of a product manual. Materialistic: Someone who buys a Rolex watch primarily to show it off at social events.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 17 covered Need for Cognition (depth of thinking). This slide adds two more traits — Visualizers vs Verbalizers (information format preference) and Consumer Materialism (attachment to possessions and luxury).

Characteristics of Materialistic People

Materialistic people have four key traits that define their relationship with possessions.

KEY CONCEPT
📌 Example A highly materialistic person buys a new luxury car, posts it on social media, feels good for a week — then immediately starts wanting the next new thing. The satisfaction is short-lived.

SLIDE 20 — Fixated Consumption & Compulsive Consumption
Two extreme forms of consumer behaviour driven by personality — obsessive collecting and out-of-control buying

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Fixated Consumption & Compulsive Consumption

Two extreme forms of consumer behaviour driven by personality — obsessive collecting and out-of-control buying

.

DEFINITION Fixated Consumption Behaviour Consumers who are intensely focused on collecting or obtaining specific products or categories. Consumers fixated on certain products or categories of products. Characteristics of fixated consumers: Passionate interest in a specific product category. Willingness to go to great lengths to secure desired objects. Dedication of significant time and money to collecting. Compulsive Consumption Behaviour Consumers who are 'addicted' or 'out-of-control' in their buying — shopping is an uncontrollable urge. 'Addicted' or 'out-of-control' consumers — buying is compulsive and cannot be stopped easily. Unlike fixated consumers (who have a passion), compulsive consumers are driven by anxiety or emotional need.
📌 Example Fixated: A Sri Lankan cricket memorabilia collector who travels internationally and spends thousands to get signed items. Compulsive: A person who buys clothes online every day even though they have no storage space — they cannot stop.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 19 described the 4 characteristics of materialistic people. This slide introduces two more extreme consumer behaviour patterns — Fixated (passionate collecting) and Compulsive (addicted buying) — both driven by personality traits.
SLIDE 21 — Consumer Ethnocentrism
Some consumers refuse to buy foreign products because they feel it is wrong to support another country's economy over their own.
සිංහල idea
Foreign product buy කරන එක තමන්ගේ country/economyට wrong කියලා හිතලා local products prefer කරන tendency එක.
•
High ethnocentrism = local product support strong.
•
Foreign brand quality good උනත් avoid කරන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Local-only mindset.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Consumer Ethnocentrism

Some consumers refuse to buy foreign products because they feel it is wrong to support another country's economy over their own.

DEFINITION Ethnocentrism = the consumer's willingness to buy — or NOT buy — foreign-made products. Highly ethnocentric consumers feel it is INAPPROPRIATE or WRONG to purchase foreign-made products. Reason: they believe buying foreign products damages the domestic economy. They prefer locally made products out of a sense of patriotism and national identity.
EXAM FOCUS Low ethnocentrism = open to buying imported goods (e.g., iPhones, foreign cars). High ethnocentrism = strongly prefers local brands out of national loyalty. SCALE used to measure it: CETSCALE (Consumer Ethnocentric Tendencies Scale).
📌 Example A highly ethnocentric Sri Lankan consumer always buys Maliban biscuits instead of imported brands — they believe buying local supports Sri Lankan workers and the national economy.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 19–20 covered fixated and compulsive behaviours. This slide introduces Consumer Ethnocentrism — a trait that affects whether a consumer is willing to buy FOREIGN products based on national identity and patriotism.
SLIDE 22 — Personality and Color
Different colors communicate different personality traits and emotions — marketers choose brand colors very carefully to match the personality they want to project.
සිංහල idea
Personality කියන්නේ එක් එක් කෙනා unique කරන, ඔහු හිතන/feel කරන/act කරන විදිහ shape කරන inner psychological characteristics set එක.
•
Individual differences තියෙනවා.
•
Generally consistent/enduring.
•
Major life events නිසා change වෙන්නත් පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Inner traits → different consumer choices.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Personality and Color

Different colors communicate different personality traits and emotions — marketers choose brand colors very carefully to match the personality they want to project.

KEY CONCEPT
📌 Example Dialog Sri Lanka uses red in its branding — red signals excitement and passion, making consumers feel energized and engaged with the brand. Apple uses white packaging to signal purity, simplicity, and cleanliness.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 21 covered Ethnocentrism (product origin preference). This slide introduces a completely new and visual dimension of personality — how COLORS are linked to personality traits and how marketers use color psychology in branding.
SLIDE 23 — Product and Brand Personification
When consumers think of brands as if they have human personalities — brands take on character traits just like people do.
සිංහල idea
Brand එක human person කෙනෙක් වගේ traits තියෙනවා කියලා customerගේ mind එකේ හදන image එක.
•
Sincerity = honest.
•
Excitement = energetic.
•
Competence = capable/trustworthy.
•
Sophistication = elegant/premium.
•
Ruggedness = strong/tough.
උදාහරණය: Jeep = strong/tough; luxury perfume brand = elegant.
Exam මතකය: Honest / Energy / Capable / Elegant / Strong.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Product and Brand Personification

When consumers think of brands as if they have human personalities — brands take on character traits just like people do.

DEFINITION Brand personification occurs when consumers attribute human traits or characteristics to a brand. A 'brand personality' provides an emotional identity for a brand. This emotional identity produces sentiments and feelings toward the brand among consumers. Examples of personality-like traits associated with brands:
📌 Example Dialog Sri Lanka's brand personality is 'connected, caring, and modern' — consumers feel a human-like emotional bond with the brand, not just a service provider relationship.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 22 showed how COLORS communicate personality. This slide builds on that — explaining how entire BRANDS are given human personality traits, and how consumers form emotional relationships with brands as if they were people.
SLIDE 24 — Brand Personality Framework
A structured model that organizes all possible brand personalities into 5 core dimensions — each with its own set of traits.
සිංහල idea
Brand Personality framework එකේ brand එක human personality එකක් වගේ dimensions වලින් describe කරනවා. Easy memory: Sincerity = honest, Excitement = energy, Competence = capable/trustable, Sophistication = elegant, Ruggedness = strong/tough.
Simple English idea: A structured model that organizes all possible brand personalities into 5 core dimensions — each with its own set of traits.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Brand Personality Framework

A structured model that organizes all possible brand personalities into 5 core dimensions — each with its own set of traits.

KEY CONCEPT Source: Journal of Marketing Research, 1997, American Marketing Association
EXAM FOCUS The 5 dimensions are: Sincerity, Excitement, Competence, Sophistication, Ruggedness. Each brand fits into one (or more) of these dimensions based on its personality traits. Consumers choose brands whose personalities MATCH their own self-concept.
📌 Example A consumer who sees themselves as 'tough and outdoorsy' will choose a Jeep (Ruggedness) over a Mercedes (Sophistication) — their self-concept matches the brand personality dimension.
Link to Previous Slide Slide 23 introduced Brand Personification (brands having human traits). This final slide presents the complete FRAMEWORK — the 5 organized dimensions used to classify and measure any brand's personality. It connects personality theory directly to brand strategy.

Learning
Knowledge + experience used in future behavior.
අනාගත buying සඳහා දැනුම/experience ගන්න එක.
Classical conditioning
Pair things until one signal alone creates the feeling.
bell + food type idea; marketing වල celebrity/jingle + brand.
3 classical tools
Repetition / Generalization / Discrimination.
repeat / trust similar / stand out.
Operant conditioning
Try → result → repeat or stop.
good result නම් ආයෙ කරනවා.
Do not mix
Positive reinforcement = give good; Negative reinforcement = avoid bad; Punishment = reduce behavior; Extinction = reward stops and behavior fades.
good add / bad avoid / bad consequence / fade.

Watch another person and copy.

SLIDE 2 — Why Marketers Are Concerned About How Consumers Learn
Marketers need to 'teach' consumers about products — and then check if the teaching worked.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් experience, stimulus, reward හෝ thinking හරහා consumer future behaviour learn/change කරන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: Marketers need to 'teach' consumers about products — and then check if the teaching worked.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Why Marketers Are Concerned About How Consumers Learn

Marketers need to 'teach' consumers about products — and then check if the teaching worked.

KEY CONCEPT What marketers want to teach consumers: Product attributes — what the product does and how it works. Where to buy them — which stores or websites sell the product. How to use and dispose of them — correct usage and safe disposal. How marketers check if learning worked: Directly — through advertisements that deliver the message clearly. Indirectly — through product appearance, packaging, price, and distribution channels.
📌 Example Keells supermarket teaches consumers through shelf placement (indirect) and TV ads (direct) that their private-label products are affordable and good quality.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide The cover slide introduced Consumer Learning. This slide explains WHY marketers care — they want to teach consumers, and they need to know if their teaching was effective.
SLIDE 3 — What is Learning?
Learning is the process of gaining new knowledge or skills that change how we think and behave in the future.
සිංහල idea
Marketing පැත්තෙන් learning කියන්නේ future buying/use decisions වලට use කරන්න product knowledge සහ experience ගන්න process එක.
•
Past experience future behaviour change කරනවා.
•
Marketers product attributes, where to buy, how to use වගේ දේ teach කරන්න try කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Big Mac එක try කරලා කැමති උනා → next time same brand choose.
Exam මතකය: Knowledge + experience → future behaviour.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
What is Learning?

Learning is the process of gaining new knowledge or skills that change how we think and behave in the future.

DEFINITION General Definition Learning changes what is stored in a person's long-term memory. Learning is a process by which changes occur in the content or organization of an individual's long-term memory. Marketing Definition From a marketing standpoint, learning is about gaining purchase and consumption knowledge. The process by which individuals acquire the purchase and consumption knowledge and experience. This knowledge and experience is applied to future related behavior.
📌 Example A consumer tries McDonald's Big Mac for the first time → learns they love it → applies this knowledge by going back to McDonald's on future visits.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 2 explained WHY marketers care about learning. This slide defines WHAT learning actually is — both generally and from a marketing perspective.
SLIDE 4 — Range of Learning Situations
Not all learning is the same — it depends on how much the consumer cares about the product or information.

Marketing පැත්තෙන් learning කියන්නේ future buying/use decisions වලට use කරන්න product knowledge සහ experience ගන්න process එක.

උදාහරණය: Big Mac එක try කරලා කැමති උනා → next time same brand choose.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Range of Learning Situations

Not all learning is the same — it depends on how much the consumer cares about the product or information.

KEY CONCEPT Learning occurs at various levels of consumer involvement. Low-Level Involvement Consumer has little or no motivation to process the information. Consumers have little or no motivation to process the information. Learning happens passively — without much thought or effort. High-Involvement Learning Consumer is highly motivated and actively processes the information. Consumers are highly motivated to process the information. Learning is active and intentional — the consumer seeks out information.
📌 Example Low involvement: Seeing a Milo ad on TV while eating dinner — you barely notice. High involvement: Carefully researching specifications before buying a new iPhone.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 3 defined learning. This slide shows that learning is NOT the same for every situation — it varies depending on how involved or interested the consumer is.
SLIDE 5 — Learning Theories
There are two main categories of theories that explain how people learn.
සිංහල idea
Marketing පැත්තෙන් learning කියන්නේ future buying/use decisions වලට use කරන්න product knowledge සහ experience ගන්න process එක.
•
Past experience future behaviour change කරනවා.
•
Marketers product attributes, where to buy, how to use වගේ දේ teach කරන්න try කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Big Mac එක try කරලා කැමති උනා → next time same brand choose.
Exam මතකය: Knowledge + experience → future behaviour.

Learning Theories

There are two main categories of theories that explain how people learn.

KEY CONCEPT There are many theories about how people learn. They fall into two general categories:
📌 Example Behavioral: You see a McDonald's ad (stimulus) and feel hungry (response) — that is behavioral learning. Cognitive: You research all mobile plans before choosing Dialog — that is cognitive learning.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 4 showed that learning varies by involvement level. This slide introduces the TWO big theory families that explain how learning actually happens — behavioral and cognitive.
SLIDE 6 — Behavioral Learning Theories
Learning is shown through observable reactions — if someone responds predictably to a trigger, they have learned.
සිංහල idea
Marketing පැත්තෙන් learning කියන්නේ future buying/use decisions වලට use කරන්න product knowledge සහ experience ගන්න process එක.
•
Past experience future behaviour change කරනවා.
•
Marketers product attributes, where to buy, how to use වගේ දේ teach කරන්න try කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Big Mac එක try කරලා කැමති උනා → next time same brand choose.
Exam මතකය: Knowledge + experience → future behaviour.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Behavioral Learning Theories

Learning is shown through observable reactions — if someone responds predictably to a trigger, they have learned.

DEFINITION Also referred to as stimulus-response (S-R) theories. Based on the premise that observable responses to specific external stimuli signal that learning has taken place. When a person responds in a predictable way to a known stimulus, they are said to have learned. Two behavioral theories relevant to marketing: 1. Classical Conditioning 2. Instrumental / Operant Conditioning
📌 Example Every time a Sri Lankan consumer hears the Dialog jingle on TV, they feel a sense of trust. The jingle (stimulus) consistently produces trust (response) — that is behavioral learning.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 5 listed the two theory categories. This slide zooms into the FIRST category — Behavioral Learning — and sets up the two specific behavioral theories explained in the next slides.
SLIDE 7 — Classical Conditioning
When a neutral signal is paired with a powerful trigger often enough, the neutral signal alone starts to produce the same reaction.
සිංහල idea
Neutral signal එක strong natural trigger එකක් එක්ක repeatedly pair කරද්දි, පස්සේ neutral signal එක තනියම same feeling/response එක trigger කරනවා.
•
US = natural trigger.
•
UR = natural response.
•
CS = initially neutral signal.
•
CR = learned response to CS.
උදාහරණය: Famous cricketerට positive feeling තියෙනවා → tea brand එක්ක repeatedly show → brandට positive feeling transfer වෙනවා.
Exam මතකය: Pair again and again until signal alone gives feeling.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Classical Conditioning

When a neutral signal is paired with a powerful trigger often enough, the neutral signal alone starts to produce the same reaction.

DEFINITION Conditioned learning results when a stimulus that is paired with a stimulus that elicits a known response serves to produce the same response when used alone. Using a well-known, admired individual to advertise a product conditions consumers to have positive feelings about the product. 'Cause marketing' — linking a product to a charitable cause conditions consumers to feel good about the brand.
📌 Example A famous Sri Lankan cricketer (admired individual = US) endorses a tea brand → consumers feel positive about the cricketer → they transfer those positive feelings to the tea brand (CS) → they buy it.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 6 introduced behavioral learning theories. This slide explains the FIRST behavioral theory in detail — Classical Conditioning — showing how repeated pairing of stimuli creates learned responses.
SLIDE 8 — Strategic Applications of Classical Conditioning
Marketers use three key principles from classical conditioning to shape consumer behavior.
සිංහල idea
Neutral signal එක strong natural trigger එකක් එක්ක repeatedly pair කරද්දි, පස්සේ neutral signal එක තනියම same feeling/response එක trigger කරනවා.
•
US = natural trigger.
•
UR = natural response.
•
CS = initially neutral signal.
•
CR = learned response to CS.
උදාහරණය: Famous cricketerට positive feeling තියෙනවා → tea brand එක්ක repeatedly show → brandට positive feeling transfer වෙනවා.
Exam මතකය: Pair again and again until signal alone gives feeling.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Strategic Applications of Classical Conditioning

Marketers use three key principles from classical conditioning to shape consumer behavior.

KEY CONCEPT Three basic concepts derive from classical conditioning: 1. Repetition 2. Stimulus Generalization 3. Stimulus Discrimination
📌 Example Think of these 3 as 3 marketing tools — repeat the message, extend it to similar products, or use it to stand out from competitors.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 7 explained how classical conditioning works (the theory). This slide shows HOW marketers APPLY those principles in real marketing strategies.
SLIDE 9 — Strategic Application 1 — Repetition
Showing the same ad many times strengthens the link between the product and positive feelings — but too much can backfire.
සිංහල idea
Same message repeatedly show කරලා memory/association strengthen කරනවා.
•
Enough repetition = remember.
•
Too much = wearout/annoyance → negative image.
උදාහරණය: Cricket match එකේ ad 3-4 times useful; 20 times annoy වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Repeat, but not overdo.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Repetition

Showing the same ad many times strengthens the link between the product and positive feelings — but too much can backfire.

DEFINITION Increases the strength of the association between a conditioned stimulus and an unconditioned stimulus. Used by advertisers when scheduling media exposure for an advertising campaign. Warning: Too much repetition can lead to advertising wearout. Consumers may become annoyed with repetitive ads. This can develop a NEGATIVE image of the product as a result.
📌 Example SriLankan Airlines plays the same TV commercial every ad break during a cricket match — effective at first, but if shown 20 times, viewers become irritated and start disliking the brand.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 8 listed the 3 strategic applications. This slide explains the FIRST one — Repetition — and warns that too much repetition can actually hurt the brand.
SLIDE 10 — Strategic Application 2 — Stimulus Generalization
Consumers apply what they know about one product to similar products — marketers use this to extend trusted brands.
සිංහල idea
එක product/brand ගැන ඉගෙනගත්ත positive response එක similar product එකකටත් apply කරන එක.
•
Product line extension = same product new flavour/size.
•
Product form extension = same product different form.
•
Product category extension = same brand new category.
•
Halo effect මෙතනත් useful.
උදාහරණය: iPhone trust → Apple Watch trust.
Exam මතකය: Same feeling to similar thing.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Stimulus Generalization

Consumers apply what they know about one product to similar products — marketers use this to extend trusted brands.

DEFINITION Learning relies not only on repetition, but on people's ability to generalize. Generalize = respond in the same way to slightly different stimuli. Explains why some manufacturers try to make their generic/store brands similar in appearance to name brands. Marketers use stimulus generalization through brand extensions: (1) Product line extension — new variants of the same product (e.g., new flavour, new size). (2) Product form extension — same product in a different form (e.g., liquid to tablet). (3) Product category extension — moving into a completely new category using the same brand name. This effect is also referred to as the HALO EFFECT — positive feelings about one product spread to related products.
📌 Example Apple iPhone consumers love the brand → Apple launches Apple Watch → consumers immediately trust it too (halo effect). In Sri Lanka, Keells' trusted supermarket brand extends to Keells-branded packaged foods.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 9 explained Repetition. This slide covers the SECOND application — Stimulus Generalization — which is how brand trust spreads from one product to related ones.
SLIDE 11 — Strategic Application 3 — Stimulus Discrimination
The opposite of generalization — consumers learn to tell one brand apart from similar brands.
සිංහල idea
Similar options අතරින් particular brand එක වෙනස් කියලා learn කරලා ඒක select කරන එක.
•
Generalization එකේ opposite.
•
Positioning/differentiation එකේ base එක.
•
Colour, package, features, ingredients use කරලා stand out කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola red can එක competitors අතරින් immediately identify කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Tell one brand apart.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Stimulus Discrimination

The opposite of generalization — consumers learn to tell one brand apart from similar brands.

DEFINITION Stimulus Discrimination is the OPPOSITE of stimulus generalization. Results in the selection of a specific stimulus from among similar stimuli. Forms the basis for marketers' positioning strategy. Permits marketers to differentiate their product from competitors' products. Differentiation achieved through: different features, colors, ingredients, packaging, etc.
📌 Example Coca-Cola's distinctive red can and curved bottle teach consumers to pick it instantly from a fridge full of similar drinks — discrimination means choosing Coke, not a copy.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 10 showed Stimulus Generalization (spreading the brand). This slide shows the OPPOSITE strategy — Stimulus Discrimination — where the goal is to stand out and be chosen over similar competitors.
SLIDE 12 — Operant (Instrumental) Conditioning
People learn by trying things — if the result is good, they repeat the behavior; if it is bad, they stop.
සිංහල idea
Trial and error හරහා learn කරනවා. Good result ලැබුණොත් behaviour repeat; bad result නම් stop/avoid.
•
Try product.
•
Consequence/reward බලනවා.
•
Good result → repeat habit.
උදාහරණය: Restaurant එක හොඳ service දුන්නා → ආයෙත් යනවා.
Exam මතකය: Try → result → repeat/stop.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Operant (Instrumental) Conditioning

People learn by trying things — if the result is good, they repeat the behavior; if it is bad, they stop.

DEFINITION Developed by B.F. Skinner. Learning occurs through TRIAL AND ERROR. Habits are formed as a result of rewards received for certain behaviors. Consumers who try different brands, models, or styles until they find the one that 'fits' are engaged in instrumental learning.
📌 Example A customer tries three different brands of kottu roti at three different restaurants (trial and error) → finds that one is the best → goes back to that restaurant every time (habit formed through reward).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 7–11 covered Classical Conditioning (learning through pairing). This slide introduces the SECOND behavioral theory — Operant Conditioning — where learning comes from the consequences of one's own actions.
SLIDE 13 — Reinforcement
A reward or consequence that makes a person more likely to repeat (or avoid) a behavior.
සිංහල idea
දෙකම behaviour repeat කරන්න encourage කරනවා, difference එක reward vs bad thing avoid කරන එක.
•
Positive reinforcement = good reward add කරනවා.
•
Negative reinforcement = bad/unpleasant result avoid කරන්න behaviour කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Free data for early recharge = positive; sunscreen use නොකළොත් skin damage කියලා avoid කරගන්න buy = negative reinforcement.
Exam මතකය: Good added / Bad avoided.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Reinforcement

A reward or consequence that makes a person more likely to repeat (or avoid) a behavior.

DEFINITION A reward given to acknowledge a desired behavior and increase the probability it will be repeated. 1. Positive Reinforcement A good outcome that makes you want to do it again. Events that strengthen the likelihood of a specific response. Example: Getting a free gift with a purchase → consumer buys again to get another reward. 2. Negative Reinforcement An unpleasant outcome that you want to avoid — motivates action to escape it. A negative outcome that also serves to ENCOURAGE a specific behavior. Often used through fear appeals in advertising. Example: 'If you don't use sunscreen, you will get skin damage' → fear motivates sunscreen purchase.
📌 Example Positive: Dialog gives you free data for recharging early (reward = repeat the behavior). Negative: A health insurance ad shows hospital bills piling up (fear) → you buy insurance to avoid that outcome.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 12 introduced Operant Conditioning. This slide explains the KEY mechanism inside it — Reinforcement — which determines whether a behavior is repeated or not.
SLIDE 14 — Punishment
A negative consequence that discourages a behavior — the opposite of reinforcement.
සිංහල idea
Behaviour reduce වෙන ways දෙක.
•
Punishment = bad consequence දීලා behaviour discourage.
•
Extinction = reward stop උනාම learned behaviour slow slow disappear.
උදාහරණය: Late payment fine = punishment; loyalty reward permanently stop → purchase habit weaken = extinction.
Exam මතකය: Bad consequence / Reward disappears.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Punishment

A negative consequence that discourages a behavior — the opposite of reinforcement.

DEFINITION Punishment DISCOURAGES behavior — it reduces the chance of a behavior being repeated. This is the opposite of reinforcement, which ENCOURAGES behavior. When consumers have a bad experience with a product, that negative outcome acts as punishment — they avoid buying it again.
📌 Example A customer buys a cheap local phone brand that breaks within a week (punishment = bad experience) → they never buy that brand again and warn their friends too.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 13 covered reinforcement (encouraging behavior). This slide shows the opposite — punishment — which discourages behavior. Together, reinforcement and punishment shape what consumers continue to do or stop doing.
SLIDE 15 — Extinction
When rewards stop coming, a learned behavior slowly disappears — the consumer 'forgets' the habit.
සිංහල idea
Behaviour reduce වෙන ways දෙක.
•
Punishment = bad consequence දීලා behaviour discourage.
•
Extinction = reward stop උනාම learned behaviour slow slow disappear.
උදාහරණය: Late payment fine = punishment; loyalty reward permanently stop → purchase habit weaken = extinction.
Exam මතකය: Bad consequence / Reward disappears.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Extinction

When rewards stop coming, a learned behavior slowly disappears — the consumer 'forgets' the habit.

DEFINITION When a learned response is no longer reinforced, it diminishes to the point of extinction. There is no longer a link between the stimulus and the expected reward. The consumer stops repeating the behavior because no reward is coming.
📌 Example A coffee shop gave a free coffee stamp card → customer visits every week to collect stamps → shop cancels the program (no more reward) → customer gradually stops visiting as often (extinction of the habit).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 13–14 showed how reinforcement and punishment shape behavior. This slide adds the concept of Extinction — what happens when reinforcement is completely removed and the learned behavior disappears over time.
SLIDE 16 — Strategic Applications of Instrumental Conditioning
Marketers use operant conditioning to maximize customer satisfaction and keep consumers coming back.
සිංහල idea
Neutral signal එක strong natural trigger එකක් එක්ක repeatedly pair කරද්දි, පස්සේ neutral signal එක තනියම same feeling/response එක trigger කරනවා.
•
US = natural trigger.
•
UR = natural response.
•
CS = initially neutral signal.
•
CR = learned response to CS.
උදාහරණය: Famous cricketerට positive feeling තියෙනවා → tea brand එක්ක repeatedly show → brandට positive feeling transfer වෙනවා.
Exam මතකය: Pair again and again until signal alone gives feeling.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Strategic Applications of Instrumental Conditioning

Marketers use operant conditioning to maximize customer satisfaction and keep consumers coming back.

KEY CONCEPT Customer Satisfaction The most important strategic application — happy customers return. In order to keep its customers, a marketer or business must maximize positive reinforcement. This can be done through the PRODUCT ITSELF — high quality, value, and performance. Can also be done with OTHER ELEMENTS of the purchase situation — friendly staff, nice store, fast delivery.
📌 Example Amazon maximizes positive reinforcement through fast delivery, easy returns, and Prime rewards — making customers want to shop there again and again.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 12–15 explained the theory behind operant conditioning. This slide shows the PRACTICAL USE — how marketers use it to keep customers satisfied and loyal.
SLIDE 17 — Reinforcement Schedules
The pattern in which rewards are given — this affects how strongly a behavior is learned and maintained.
සිංහල idea
Reward හැම වෙලාවෙම දෙනවද, certain pattern එකකට දෙනවද කියන එක behaviour strength එකට බලපානවා.
•
Continuous = every time reward.
•
Fixed ratio/systematic = every nth purchase වගේ.
උදාහරණය: Every 5th coffee free.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Reinforcement Schedules

The pattern in which rewards are given — this affects how strongly a behavior is learned and maintained.

DEFINITION A reinforcement schedule is the pattern in which reinforcements (rewards) are given. Product QUALITY must remain consistently high to keep consumers satisfied. But non-product positive reinforcement (like coupons or gifts) does NOT have to be offered every single time. Continuous reinforcement → forgetting occurs more quickly when rewards stop. Intermittent reinforcement → forgetting occurs gradually and residual effects of learning persist longer.
📌 Example Fixed ratio: Loyalty stamp card — buy 9 coffees, get the 10th free. Variable ratio: Lucky draw promotions (like a McDonald's Monopoly game) — you never know when you'll win, so you keep buying!
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 16 showed how instrumental conditioning is applied through customer satisfaction. This slide adds detail on HOW OFTEN rewards should be given — the schedule matters because it affects how long the learned behavior lasts.
SLIDE 18 — Cognitive Learning Theory
Learning through thinking — people search for information, evaluate it, and decide what is best for them.
සිංහල idea
Consumer info search කරලා, compare කරලා, reason කරලා, best option choose කරමින් learn කරනවා.
•
Detailed useful information important.
•
High involvement products වල common.
උදාහරණය: Mobile plans compare කරලා best plan select කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Think → understand → decide.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Cognitive Learning Theory

Learning through thinking — people search for information, evaluate it, and decide what is best for them.

DEFINITION Learning is based on mental activity — thinking and problem-solving. Unlike simpler organisms, humans do not only learn through trial and error. Humans also learn by: → Searching for information. → Evaluating the information. → Making a decision about what is best for them.
📌 Example Before buying a new laptop, a University of Colombo student searches online reviews, compares specifications, watches YouTube comparisons, and then chooses the best option — that is cognitive learning.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 6–17 covered Behavioral Learning theories (stimulus-response, classical and operant conditioning). This slide begins the SECOND major category — Cognitive Learning — which focuses on the thinking mind, not just reactions.
SLIDE 19 — Marketing Implications of Cognitive Learning Theory
Because consumers think before buying, marketers must provide clear, useful information as a key strategy.
සිංහල idea
Marketing පැත්තෙන් learning කියන්නේ future buying/use decisions වලට use කරන්න product knowledge සහ experience ගන්න process එක.
•
Past experience future behaviour change කරනවා.
•
Marketers product attributes, where to buy, how to use වගේ දේ teach කරන්න try කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Big Mac එක try කරලා කැමති උනා → next time same brand choose.
Exam මතකය: Knowledge + experience → future behaviour.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Marketing Implications of Cognitive Learning Theory

Because consumers think before buying, marketers must provide clear, useful information as a key strategy.

KEY CONCEPT Primary implication: emphasize the importance of providing INFORMATION to consumers. Providing information can itself be a PROMOTIONAL STRATEGY — educating the consumer builds trust and drives purchase.
📌 Example A pharmaceutical company runs an ad explaining the science behind their vitamin supplement (information-based promotion) → educated consumers feel confident and choose that brand over competitors.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 18 introduced Cognitive Learning Theory. This slide applies it — showing that the main marketing implication is to provide information, because cognitively active consumers need facts to make decisions.
SLIDE 20 — Cognitive Associative Learning (Neo-Pavlovian Theory)
An advanced form of learning — consumers actively connect new information with what they already know to build a full picture of the world.
සිංහල idea
Marketing පැත්තෙන් learning කියන්නේ future buying/use decisions වලට use කරන්න product knowledge සහ experience ගන්න process එක.
•
Past experience future behaviour change කරනවා.
•
Marketers product attributes, where to buy, how to use වගේ දේ teach කරන්න try කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Big Mac එක try කරලා කැමති උනා → next time same brand choose.
Exam මතකය: Knowledge + experience → future behaviour.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Cognitive Associative Learning

An advanced form of learning — consumers actively connect new information with what they already know to build a full picture of the world.

DEFINITION Also called Neo-Pavlovian Theory. Learning involves not only the acquisition of new reflexes. It is the acquisition of new KNOWLEDGE about the world. Consumers are viewed as INFORMATION SEEKERS. They use logical and perceptual relations among events, along with their own preconceptions. This helps them form a sophisticated representation of the world.
📌 Example A consumer reads that organic foods are healthier (new knowledge) → connects this with their existing belief in healthy living → builds a comprehensive view that organic = better → starts buying organic products at Keells.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 19 explained implications of basic cognitive theory. This slide introduces a more advanced version — Cognitive Associative (Neo-Pavlovian) Learning — where consumers actively BUILD knowledge frameworks, not just respond to stimuli.
SLIDE 21 — Implications for Marketers — Both Theories Combined
Smart marketers combine BOTH cognitive and operant conditioning strategies for maximum effect.
සිංහල idea
Real marketing එකේ Behavioral සහ Cognitive learning දෙකම එකට use වෙනවා. Ad repetition/response වගේ behavioral side එකත්, compare/think/understand කරන cognitive side එකත් එකම decision එකට බලපානවා.
Simple English idea: Smart marketers combine BOTH cognitive and operant conditioning strategies for maximum effect.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Implications for Marketers

Smart marketers combine BOTH cognitive and operant conditioning strategies for maximum effect.

KEY CONCEPT Marketers draw on BOTH cognitive and operant/classical conditioning theories.
📌 Example McDonald's uses cognitive learning (nutritional information on packaging) AND classical conditioning (Happy Meal toys as rewards for children) — both theories working together at the same time.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slides 18–20 covered Cognitive Learning Theory in detail. This slide brings BOTH major theory families (behavioral + cognitive) together and shows how real marketers use them simultaneously.
SLIDE 22 — Vicarious (Observational) Learning
Learning by watching other people — consumers copy behaviors they see in role models or ads.
සිංහල idea
තමන් try නොකර අනිත් කෙනා කරන දේ සහ result බලලා learn කරනවා.
•
Positive role model copy කරනවා.
•
Negative consequence දැකලා avoid කරන්න learn කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Favourite cricketer product use කරනවා දැකලා same brand buy කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Learn by watching.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Vicarious (Observational) Learning

Learning by watching other people — consumers copy behaviors they see in role models or ads.

DEFINITION The process through which individuals learn behavior by observing the behavior of others. They also observe the CONSEQUENCES of that behavior — what happens to others after they act. Role models tend to be people consumers admire because of traits such as appearance, accomplishment, skill, or social class. Two forms of observational learning in marketing: (1) Positive role model — consumer sees admired person using a product and copies the behavior. (2) Negative consequences — ads show what happens when you do NOT use the advertised product (fear-based observation).
📌 Example A Sri Lankan teenager sees their favourite cricketer using a specific brand of protein supplement (positive role model) → they buy the same brand because they want to be like him.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 21 showed how both theories are used together. This slide introduces a THIRD type of learning — Vicarious/Observational — where consumers learn not from their own experience but by watching others.
SLIDE 23 — Brand Loyalty
When a consumer consistently chooses the same brand again and again — the ultimate result of successful consumer learning.
සිංහල idea
Repeated positive learning/experience නිසා same brand එක consistently choose කරන එක.
•
High involvement වල risk reduce කරනවා.
•
Low involvement වල time/effort save කරනවා.
•
Consistent quality loyalty build කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: 10 years Dilmah tea එකෙන් good experience → same brand buy.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Brand Loyalty

When a consumer consistently chooses the same brand again and again — the ultimate result of successful consumer learning.

DEFINITION Brand loyalty = a consumer's consistent preference for and purchase of a specific brand. Why Brand Loyalty Matters It reduces risk and saves time for consumers, depending on the type of purchase. (1) High-involvement purchases (e.g., a car) — loyalty reduces risk and makes selection easier. (2) Low-involvement purchases (e.g., tissues) — loyalty saves time and effort. Brand loyalty generally results from CONSISTENT POSITIVE EXPERIENCES with a company and/or its products.
📌 Example A consumer always buys Dilmah tea (brand loyalty) because every cup has been consistently good for 10 years — the consistency of the experience built deep loyalty.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 22 introduced observational learning. This slide presents Brand Loyalty — the powerful OUTCOME that results from repeated positive consumer learning experiences over time.
SLIDE 24 — Decline in Brand Loyalty
In recent times, consumers are becoming less loyal to brands — and marketers must fight back with loyalty programs.
සිංහල idea
Repeated positive learning/experience නිසා same brand එක consistently choose කරන එක.
•
High involvement වල risk reduce කරනවා.
•
Low involvement වල time/effort save කරනවා.
•
Consistent quality loyalty build කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: 10 years Dilmah tea එකෙන් good experience → same brand buy.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Decline in Brand Loyalty

In recent times, consumers are becoming less loyal to brands — and marketers must fight back with loyalty programs.

KEY CONCEPT There has been a recent decline in brand loyalty. Reasons: (1) Boredom or dissatisfaction with the products — consumers want something new. (2) Variety-seeking — consumers enjoy trying different options even when satisfied. (3) Increased concern with price — consumers switch to cheaper alternatives. How marketers counter the decline: Marketers have adopted programs that REWARD brand loyalty. Example: frequent flyer programs, points systems, exclusive member discounts.
EXAM FOCUS Know all 3 reasons for declining brand loyalty. Know how marketers respond — loyalty rewards programs. Link this to operant conditioning: rewards reinforce repeat purchase behavior.
📌 Example A Keells frequent shopper switches to Arpico due to lower prices (price concern = decline in loyalty) → Keells responds with a loyalty card program offering points and discounts to win them back.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide Slide 23 showed how brand loyalty is built through consistent positive learning. This final slide shows the CHALLENGE — that loyalty is declining — and how marketers use reinforcement strategies (from operant conditioning) to counter it. This connects learning theory directly back to brand behavior.

06. Consumer Attitude

Attitude
Learned like/dislike that guides action.
brand/product එකක් ගැන කැමැත්ත/අකමැත්ත.
ABC / Tri-component
Cognitive = think; Affective = feel; Conative = act.
think / feel / act.
Reasoned Action flow
Outcome beliefs + good/bad judgement → attitude; people’s views + how much you listen → social pressure; both → intention → action.
ඔයා දුන්න simple box logic එක.
5 change strategies
Need → admired people → fix conflict → change feature judgement → change competitor beliefs.
exam-friendly 5-step memory.
Cognitive dissonance
Doubt/regret after purchase.
buy කළාට පස්සේ “හරිද ගත්තේ?” කියන feeling එක.
Marketing fix for dissonance
Warranty, support, easy returns, follow-up, proof/reviews, clear information.
purchase පස්සේ customer reassure කරන එක.

Consumer Attitude Formation and Change

(This topic teaches how people build likes and dislikes about products, and how sellers can change those feelings.)

DEFINITION What is Attitude Research? Attitude research is a way to study how people feel about things they buy. • People have many attitudes. They have feelings about products, shops, and adverts. • Marketers study these feelings to understand what customers really want. • This research checks if people will accept a new product idea. • It also checks why people did not like a new advert or promotion. • The main goal is to know current feelings, so companies can serve customers better. 📌 Example: A tea company asks people what they think about a new tea flavour before selling it.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This is the very first slide. It has no earlier slide to connect to. It simply opens the topic of consumer attitudes.
SLIDE 2 — Definition of Attitude
(An attitude is simply how much someone likes or dislikes something.)

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
What are Attitudes?

(An attitude is simply how much someone likes or dislikes something.)

DEFINITION The Meaning of Attitude An attitude is a feeling that a person learns. This feeling makes them act in a good way or bad way towards something. • An attitude is 'learned'. This means nobody is born with it. We pick it up over time. • An attitude is 'consistent'. This means the person usually behaves the same way each time. • An attitude is about a 'given object'. This object can be a product, brand, or advert. • Attitudes guide real actions. If someone likes a brand, they usually buy it again. 📌 Example: A student always chooses Coca-Cola over Pepsi because she learned to like its taste.
EXAM FOCUS Three Key Properties Every attitude definition has three important parts that exams often ask about. • Property 1: The attitude object. • Property 2: Attitudes are learned. • Property 3: Attitudes have consistency. 📌 Example: Remember these three properties as 'Object, Learned, Consistent' for exams.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide gives the full definition. The next three slides will explain each of the three properties one by one.
SLIDE 3 — Property 1 — The Attitude Object
(The 'object' is just the thing a person has feelings about.)

Attitude object = feeling එක තියෙන thing එක.
•

•
Attitude usually consistent.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola taste කැමති නිසා repeated buy කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Object + Learned + Consistent.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Attitude 'Object'

(The 'object' is just the thing a person has feelings about.)

DEFINITION Meaning of 'Object' in Attitude The word 'object' does not only mean a physical item. It means anything a person can have a feeling about. • An object can be a product, like a phone or a car. • An object can be a whole product category, like 'laptops' in general. • An object can be a brand name, like Apple or Samsung. • An object can even be a service, an advertisement, or how a product is used. 📌 Example: When studying laptop attitudes, the 'objects' are brands like Dell, Apple, and Toshiba.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: The previous slide named three properties of attitude. This slide explains the first one, the 'object', in full detail.
SLIDE 4 — Property 2 — Attitudes are Learned
(People are not born liking or disliking a brand. They learn this feeling from life.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude කියන්නේ product/brand/ad/service එකක් ගැන person ඉගෙනගත්ත relatively consistent like/dislike feeling එක.
•
Attitude object = feeling එක තියෙන thing එක.
•
Attitude learned — born with නෑ.
•
Attitude usually consistent.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola taste කැමති නිසා repeated buy කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Object + Learned + Consistent.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Attitudes are a Learned Predisposition

(People are not born liking or disliking a brand. They learn this feeling from life.)

DEFINITION How Learning Happens Experts agree that all attitudes are learned. Nobody is born with an attitude about a brand. • Attitudes form from direct experience, like actually using the product yourself. • Attitudes form from word-of-mouth, like a friend telling you a product is good. • Attitudes form from mass media, like seeing an advert on TV or social media. 📌 Example: A boy learns to trust Dialog network after his father used it for many years.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: The last slide explained the attitude 'object'. This slide moves to the second property: how that feeling is learned.
SLIDE 5 — Property 3 — Attitudes have Consistency
(Consistency means a feeling usually stays the same and matches how a person acts.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude කියන්නේ product/brand/ad/service එකක් ගැන person ඉගෙනගත්ත relatively consistent like/dislike feeling එක.
•
Attitude object = feeling එක තියෙන thing එක.
•
Attitude learned — born with නෑ.
•
Attitude usually consistent.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola taste කැමති නිසා repeated buy කරනවා.
