Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Attitudes have Consistency

(Consistency means a feeling usually stays the same and matches how a person acts.)

DEFINITION What Consistency Means An attitude is usually steady. It matches the behaviour it is connected to, most of the time. • If someone has a good attitude toward a brand, they usually buy it again and again. • Consistency does not mean an attitude can never change. • Attitudes can still change if something new happens, like a bad experience. 📌 Example: A man always buys Anchor milk powder, but he may switch if the price suddenly increases a lot.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This is the third and last property from Slide 2. Together, the three slides fully explain the definition of attitude.
SLIDE 6 — Overview of Structural Models
(These are different theories that explain what parts make up an attitude.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude explain කරන්න models කිහිපයක් තියෙනවා. Tri-component, multi-attribute, trying-to-consume, attitude-toward-ad වගේ models එක එක angle එකෙන් attitude එක structure වෙන හැටි explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: (These are different theories that explain what parts make up an attitude.)
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Structural Models of Attitudes

(These are different theories that explain what parts make up an attitude.)

KEY CONCEPT The Four Model Groups Researchers built several models to break an attitude into smaller, easier-to-study parts. • Model 1: The Tri-component attitude model. • Model 2: The Multi-attribute attitude model (with three versions inside it). • Model 3: The Theory of Trying to Consume. • Model 4: The Attitude-toward-the-Ad model. 📌 Example: Think of these four models like four different lenses to look at the same feeling.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: The earlier slides explained what an attitude IS. This slide now lists the models that explain its STRUCTURE.
SLIDE 7 — Tri-Component Attitude Model
(This model says every attitude has three connected parts working together.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude එක THINK + FEEL + ACT කියන connected parts 3කින් බලන model එක.
•
Cognitive = thinking/knowledge/beliefs.
•
Affective = feelings/emotions.
•
Conative = intention/action/behaviour.
උදාහරණය: Phone camera good කියලා know → phone එක love → buy කරන්න plan.
Exam මතකය: THINK / FEEL / ACT.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
1. Tri-Component Attitude Model

(This model says every attitude has three connected parts working together.)

KEY CONCEPT The Three Components According to this model, one single attitude is actually made of three linked pieces. • Part 1: A cognitive component (thinking and knowledge). • Part 2: An affective component (feelings and emotions). • Part 3: A conative component (action and behaviour). • The diagram shows these three parts pointing to each other in a circle, showing they affect one another. 📌 Example: Liking a smartphone (affect) can come from knowing its features (cognition), which leads to buying it (conation).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide picks the first model from Slide 6's list and introduces its three parts. The next three slides explain each part.
SLIDE 8 — The Cognitive Component
(Cognitive means 'thinking'. This is what a person knows or believes about a product.)
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් product/brand එකක් ගැන consumerගේ thought, feeling හෝ action tendency එක form/change වෙන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: (Cognitive means 'thinking'. This is what a person knows or believes about a product.)
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Cognitive Component

(Cognitive means 'thinking'. This is what a person knows or believes about a product.)

DEFINITION Knowledge and Beliefs This component is the knowledge a person gets from using a product and from other information sources. • It comes from direct experience with the object. • It also comes from information from other sources, like reviews or friends. • This knowledge usually becomes a 'belief', like believing a brand is good quality. • A belief includes thinking a specific action will lead to a specific result. 📌 Example: A person believes that Lysol spray kills germs because they read this on the label.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide zooms into the first part of the Tri-component model shown in Slide 7: the cognitive component.
SLIDE 9 — The Affective Component
(Affective means 'feelings'. This is the emotion a person feels about a brand.)
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් product/brand එකක් ගැන consumerගේ thought, feeling හෝ action tendency එක form/change වෙන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: (Affective means 'feelings'. This is the emotion a person feels about a brand.)
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Affective Component

(Affective means 'feelings'. This is the emotion a person feels about a brand.)

DEFINITION Emotions Toward a Brand This component is simply the emotions or feelings a consumer has about a product or brand. • It is not about facts or knowledge, only about feelings. • The feeling can be positive, like happiness or excitement. • The feeling can also be negative, like dislike or annoyance. 📌 Example: A girl feels joyful whenever she sees a McDonald's Happy Meal box.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide continues from Slide 7 and 8, now explaining the second part of the Tri-component model: affect.
SLIDE 10 — The Conative Component
(Conative means 'action'. This is what a person is likely to actually do.)
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් product/brand එකක් ගැන consumerගේ thought, feeling හෝ action tendency එක form/change වෙන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: (Conative means 'action'. This is what a person is likely to actually do.)
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Conative Component

(Conative means 'action'. This is what a person is likely to actually do.)

DEFINITION Likelihood of Action This component is the chance that a person will act, or actually behave, in a certain way toward the object. • It is about the likelihood or tendency to do something, like buying a product. • Some experts say this part can even include the real action itself, not just the plan to act. 📌 Example: A man plans to buy a new tuk-tuk next month because he trusts the Bajaj brand.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide finishes the Tri-component model. Slides 8, 9, and 10 together cover cognition, affect, and conation.
SLIDE 11 — Multi-Attribute Models — Attitude Toward Object
(This model measures attitude by looking at a product's specific features.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude කියන්නේ product/brand/ad/service එකක් ගැන person ඉගෙනගත්ත relatively consistent like/dislike feeling එක.

Attitude object = feeling එක තියෙන thing එක.
•

•
Attitude usually consistent.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola taste කැමති නිසා repeated buy කරනවා.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Multi-Attribute Attitude Models: 1. Attitude-Toward-Object Model

(This model measures attitude by looking at a product's specific features.)

DEFINITION Measuring Attitude by Attributes This model is very useful for measuring feelings toward a product category or specific brand. • A consumer's attitude depends on whether a brand has certain attributes. • It also depends on how the consumer rates or evaluates those attributes. • People like brands that they feel have enough good attributes. • People dislike brands that lack good attributes or have too many bad ones. 📌 Example: A shopper likes Keells supermarket because it has cleanliness and good prices, two attributes she values.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 6 introduced the multi-attribute model as one of four models. This slide explains its first version.
SLIDE 12 — Multi-Attribute Models — Attitude Toward Behavior
(This model looks at the attitude toward DOING something, not the product itself.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude කියන්නේ product/brand/ad/service එකක් ගැන person ඉගෙනගත්ත relatively consistent like/dislike feeling එක.
•
Attitude object = feeling එක තියෙන thing එක.
•
Attitude learned — born with නෑ.
•
Attitude usually consistent.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola taste කැමති නිසා repeated buy කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Object + Learned + Consistent.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Multi-Attribute Attitude Models: 2. Attitude-Toward-Behavior Model

(This model looks at the attitude toward DOING something, not the product itself.)

DEFINITION Attitude Toward the Action This model studies how a person feels about the act of using or buying something, not the object alone. • It focuses on 'behaving' or 'acting' with respect to the object. • This is different from liking the object itself. • A person may like a brand but still feel uneasy about the act of buying it, for example due to price. 📌 Example: A man likes SriLankan Airlines but feels attitude toward 'booking online' is stressful, so he calls the office instead.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide gives the second version of the multi-attribute model, right after the object-based version in Slide 11.
SLIDE 13 — Multi-Attribute Models — Theory of Reasoned Action
(This bigger model connects beliefs, feelings, other people's opinions, and final behaviour.)
සිංහල idea
Personගේ own attitude + important peopleගේ opinion/social pressure එක එකතු වෙලා intention එක හදනවා; intention behaviour වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Belief about outcome = මොන result එකක් එයිද.
•
Evaluate outcome = ඒ result good/bad ද.
•
Attitude toward behavior = action ගැන own feeling.
•
Beliefs about referents = others think I should do what?
•
Motivation to comply = ඔවුන්ට මම කොච්චර listen කරනවද.
•
Subjective norm = social pressure.
•
Attitude + subjective norm → intention → behaviour.
උදාහරණය: Gym join කරන එක good කියලා හිතනවා + friends encourage කරනවා → join plan → join.
Exam මතකය: Own view + social pressure → plan → action.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Multi-Attribute Attitude Models: 3. Theory of Reasoned Action Model

(This bigger model connects beliefs, feelings, other people's opinions, and final behaviour.)

KEY CONCEPT How the Model Flows This model shows a chain of boxes that lead from beliefs all the way to real behaviour. Beliefs about outcomes – the person believes an action leads to a certain result. Evaluation of outcomes – the person judges if that result is good or bad. Attitude toward the behaviour – these two beliefs combine into a feeling about the action. Beliefs about referents – the person believes what other people think they should do. Motivation to comply – how much the person wants to follow those other people. Subjective norm – these two combine into social pressure. Intention and Behaviour – attitude plus subjective norm create an intention, which leads to real behaviour.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This is the third and last version of the multi-attribute model, building on the simpler models in Slides 11 and 12.
SLIDE 14 — Theory of Trying to Consume
(This theory is for cases where a person tries to buy something, but success is not certain.)
සිංහල idea
Outcome sure නැති situation එකක final behaviour එකට වඩා 'try කරන එක' explain කරන theory එක.
•
Personal impediment = person ඇතුළේ problem.
•
Environmental impediment = outside problem.
උදාහරණය: Diet කරන්න try කරනවා but fried food love = personal; limited tickets = environmental.
Exam මතකය: Trying when success is uncertain.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
3. Theory of Trying to Consume

(This theory is for cases where a person tries to buy something, but success is not certain.)

DEFINITION Trying, Not Just Doing This theory explains situations where the outcome is uncertain. It looks at the 'trying' instead of the final result. • There are two kinds of obstacles that can stop the desired outcome. Personal impediments – a problem inside the person, like trying to lose weight but loving fried food. Environmental impediments – a problem from outside, like only 50 iPods being sold at $100 each.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 6 listed this as Model 3 in the overview. This slide finally explains it in detail, after covering multi-attribute models.
SLIDE 15 — Attitude Toward the Ad Model
(This model shows how an advertisement itself can shape feelings about a brand.)
සිංහල idea
Ad එක ගැන ඇති feeling එක brand එක ගැන attitude එකට transfer වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Ad like → brand feeling better වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Ad annoying/boring → brandට negative feeling යන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Funny ad එක enjoy කරලා brand එක මතක තියාගන්නවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
4. Attitude Toward the Ad Model

(This model shows how an advertisement itself can shape feelings about a brand.)

DEFINITION How an Ad Changes Brand Attitude This model was made to understand how adverts and promotions affect attitudes toward products or brands. • It starts with 'Exposure to an Ad', meaning the person sees the advert. • This creates 'Judgments about the Ad' (thinking) and 'Feelings from the Ad' (emotion). • These two combine into 'Beliefs about the Brand' and 'Attitude toward the Ad'. • Finally, all of this creates the final 'Attitude toward the Brand'. 📌 Example: A funny Dialog advert makes viewers feel happy, and this happy feeling then makes them like the Dialog brand.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This is the fourth and final model from the Slide 6 list, completing the full set of Structural Models of Attitudes.

Attitude Formation

(This section explains HOW attitudes are first created inside a person's mind.)

KEY CONCEPT Three Areas of Attitude Formation Attitude formation can be understood by looking at three connected areas. • Area 1: How attitudes are learned. • Area 2: Sources of influence on attitude formation. • Area 3: The impact of personality on attitude formation. 📌 Example: Think of these three areas as: the process, the influencers, and the person's own character.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: The previous slides explained the STRUCTURE of attitudes (their parts). This slide switches topic to how attitudes FORM.
SLIDE 17 — How Attitudes are Learned
(An attitude changes from 'no feeling' to 'some feeling' through a learning process.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude කියන්නේ product/brand/ad/service එකක් ගැන person ඉගෙනගත්ත relatively consistent like/dislike feeling එක.
•
Attitude object = feeling එක තියෙන thing එක.
•
Attitude learned — born with නෑ.
•
Attitude usually consistent.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola taste කැමති නිසා repeated buy කරනවා.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
How Attitudes are Learned

(An attitude changes from 'no feeling' to 'some feeling' through a learning process.)

DEFINITION The Learning Process Before learning, a person has no attitude toward a new object. Learning creates a shift toward having some attitude. • People often buy new products linked to a brand name they already like. • A good attitude toward a brand name usually comes from repeated satisfaction with that company's other products. • An established brand name acts like an 'unconditioned stimulus' that already causes a good feeling. • A new product linked to that brand becomes a 'conditioned stimulus' through stimulus generalisation. • Sometimes the attitude only forms after actually trying the product. • Sometimes attitudes form purely from a person's own existing knowledge and beliefs. 📌 Example: Because people already trust Apple, they quickly form a good attitude toward a brand-new Apple product.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide explains the first area named in Slide 16: the actual process of how the learning happens.
SLIDE 18 — Sources of Influence on Attitude Formation
(These are the different places or people that shape a person's attitude.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude කියන්නේ product/brand/ad/service එකක් ගැන person ඉගෙනගත්ත relatively consistent like/dislike feeling එක.
•
Attitude object = feeling එක තියෙන thing එක.
•
Attitude learned — born with නෑ.
•
Attitude usually consistent.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola taste කැමති නිසා repeated buy කරනවා.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Sources of Influence on Attitude Formation

(These are the different places or people that shape a person's attitude.)

KEY CONCEPT Five Main Sources A consumer's attitude is shaped strongly by several outside sources. • Source 1: Personal experience with the product. • Source 2: Family members and their opinions. • Source 3: Friends and their recommendations. • Source 4: Direct marketing, like SMS offers or emails from a company. • Source 5: Mass media, like TV, radio, and social media adverts. 📌 Example: A boy forms a liking for Milo because his mother always bought it for him as a child.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide covers the second area from Slide 16's overview: WHO or WHAT influences the attitude.
SLIDE 19 — Impact of Personality on Attitude Formation
(A person's own character type changes how easily they are convinced by adverts.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude කියන්නේ product/brand/ad/service එකක් ගැන person ඉගෙනගත්ත relatively consistent like/dislike feeling එක.
•
Attitude object = feeling එක තියෙන thing එක.
•
Attitude learned — born with නෑ.
•
Attitude usually consistent.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola taste කැමති නිසා repeated buy කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Object + Learned + Consistent.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Impact of Personality on Attitude Formation

(A person's own character type changes how easily they are convinced by adverts.)

DEFINITION Two Personality Types Personality plays an important role in how a person forms an attitude from an advert. • People with a high 'need for cognition' enjoy thinking and crave detailed information. • These people form attitudes more easily from ads full of facts and product details. • People with a low 'need for cognition' do not enjoy deep thinking as much. • These people form positive attitudes more easily from ads using an attractive model or celebrity. 📌 Example: A tech-lover reads every spec sheet before liking a new phone, while another person just likes it because a celebrity used it.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This is the third and final area from Slide 16, completing the full picture of how attitudes are formed.
SLIDE 20 — Overview of Strategies of Attitude Change
(These are the methods marketers use to CHANGE how people already feel.)
සිංහල idea
Marketer customerගේ existing attitude change කරන්න main strategies 5ක් use කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
1. Change basic motivation = product එක customerගේ strong need එකකට connect කරන්න.
•
2. Connect with admired people/group = good feeling brand එකට transfer කරන්න.
•
3. Resolve conflict = negative idea එක වෙන positive value එකක් එක්ක reconcile කරන්න.
•
4. Change how product is judged = feature belief/importance/performance change කරන්න.
•
5. Change beliefs about competitors = comparison use කරලා other brand image change කරන්න.
උදාහරණය: Expensive laptop එක 'one-time quality investment' කියලා reframe කරලා negative price attitude change කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Need / Admire / Fix conflict / Change judgement / Change competitor belief.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Strategies of Attitude Change

(These are the methods marketers use to CHANGE how people already feel.)

KEY CONCEPT Why Companies Change Attitudes Changing consumer attitudes is a very important strategy for marketers to grow their business. • Market leaders (brands with strong loyalty) try to keep and strengthen the existing good feelings. • Challenger brands try to change the attitude of the leader's customers to win them over instead.
EXAM FOCUS Five Change Strategies There are five well-known strategies used to change a consumer's attitude. • Strategy 1: Changing the basic motivational function. • Strategy 2: Associating the product with an admired group. • Strategy 3: Resolving two conflicting attitudes. • Strategy 4: Altering components of the multi-attribute model. • Strategy 5: Changing beliefs about competitor brands. 📌 Example: Remember these five as ways to 'push' a customer's mind from disliking to liking a brand.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Earlier slides explained how attitudes FORM. This slide begins a new topic: how attitudes can be CHANGED.
SLIDE 21 — Strategy 1 — Changing the Basic Motivational Function
(This strategy changes attitude by making a certain NEED feel more important.)
සිංහල idea
Strategy 1 ඇතුළේ customerගේ need reason එක වෙනස් කරන්න functions 4ක් තියෙනවා.
•
Utilitarian = useful/value.
•
Ego-defensive = security/confidence.
•
Value-expressive = own values/lifestyle show.
•
Knowledge = understand/learn.
උදාහරණය: Skincare ad එක confidence දෙනවා = ego-defensive.
Exam මතකය: Useful / Confident / My values / Know.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
1. Changing the Basic Motivational Function

(This strategy changes attitude by making a certain NEED feel more important.)

DEFINITION The Functional Approach One method changes attitude by connecting the product to a strong reason, called a 'function', behind the person's feelings. • This method of appealing to motivation is called the 'functional approach'. • Attitudes can be sorted into four different functions. Utilitarian function – about the brand's usefulness or value, e.g. Lysol kills harmful germs. Ego-defensive function – gives a sense of security and confidence, e.g. cosmetics and skincare. Value-expressive function – reflects a person's values and lifestyle, e.g. buying eco-friendly products. Knowledge function – meets a person's need to know and understand something new.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide explains the first strategy listed in Slide 20's overview, giving all four functions in full detail.
SLIDE 22 — Strategy 2 & 3 — Groups and Resolving Conflicts
(These two strategies link a product to something people admire, or fix a feeling of conflict.)
සිංහල idea
Attitude change කරන්න product එක people admire කරන group/event/cause එකකට connect කරන්න පුළුවන්. නැත්තම් customerගේ head එකේ තියෙන conflicting attitudes දෙක reconcile කරලා negative feeling එක positive කරන්න පුළුවන්.
Simple English idea: (These two strategies link a product to something people admire, or fix a feeling of conflict.)
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
2. Associating Product with an Admired Group + 3. Resolving Two Conflicting Attitudes

(These two strategies link a product to something people admire, or fix a feeling of conflict.)

DEFINITION Strategy 2: Admired Groups Companies can change attitudes by showing a link between their brand and a social group, event, or cause that people admire. • This works because people transfer their good feelings about the group onto the brand. 📌 Example: A clothing brand supports a local cricket team, so cricket fans start liking that clothing brand too.
DEFINITION Strategy 3: Resolving Conflicting Attitudes Sometimes a person can be shown that their negative attitude is not really in conflict with another attitude they hold. • Once the conflict is resolved, the person may change their evaluation of the brand. • This usually moves the attitude from negative to positive. 📌 Example: A man dislikes an expensive laptop, but changes his mind once he sees it matches his value for 'buying quality once'.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide covers the second and third strategies from Slide 20's list, right after the motivational function strategy.
SLIDE 23 — Strategy 4 & 5 — Multi-Attribute Changes and Competitor Beliefs
(These strategies change specific product features or attack competitor brands directly.)
සිංහල idea
Marketer customerගේ existing attitude change කරන්න main strategies 5ක් use කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
1. Change basic motivation = product එක customerගේ strong need එකකට connect කරන්න.
•
2. Connect with admired people/group = good feeling brand එකට transfer කරන්න.
•
3. Resolve conflict = negative idea එක වෙන positive value එකක් එක්ක reconcile කරන්න.
•
4. Change how product is judged = feature belief/importance/performance change කරන්න.
•
5. Change beliefs about competitors = comparison use කරලා other brand image change කරන්න.
උදාහරණය: Expensive laptop එක 'one-time quality investment' කියලා reframe කරලා negative price attitude change කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Need / Admire / Fix conflict / Change judgement / Change competitor belief.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
4. Altering Components of the Multi-Attribute Model + 5. Changing Beliefs about Competitor Brands

(These strategies change specific product features or attack competitor brands directly.)

KEY CONCEPT Strategy 4: Four Ways to Alter the Model The multi-attribute models from earlier slides can be used to design attitude change strategies. • Way 1: Changing the relative evaluation of an attribute. • Way 2: Changing brand beliefs. • Way 3: Adding a completely new attribute. • Way 4: Changing the overall brand rating. 📌 Example: A phone brand adds a new attribute, 'better camera', to shift attitudes toward the newer model.
DEFINITION Strategy 5: Comparative Advertising This strategy tries to change what people believe about competing brands. • It can use a direct comparison, naming the competitor clearly. • It can also use an implied comparison, hinting at the competitor without naming it. 📌 Example: A fast-food advert says 'our burgers use 100% real beef, unlike some other chains', implying a comparison.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide finishes the five strategies started in Slide 20, following the earlier strategies from Slides 21 and 22.
SLIDE 24 — Behavior can precede or follow attitude formation
- Cognitive Dissonance Theory
සිංහල idea
Attitude කියන්නේ product/brand/ad/service එකක් ගැන person ඉගෙනගත්ත relatively consistent like/dislike feeling එක.
•
Attitude object = feeling එක තියෙන thing එක.
•
Attitude learned — born with නෑ.
•
Attitude usually consistent.
උදාහරණය: Coca-Cola taste කැමති නිසා repeated buy කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Object + Learned + Consistent.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ

- Cognitive Dissonance Theory

Cognitive Dissonance Theory

(This theory explains the uncomfortable feeling of doubt AFTER making a choice.)

DEFINITION Conflicting Thoughts After a Decision Cognitive dissonance happens when a person holds two conflicting thoughts about a belief or a chosen product. • It often happens after a big commitment, especially for an expensive item. • The person feels dissonance (discomfort) about the option they did NOT choose. • When this feeling happens after buying something, it is called 'post-purchase dissonance'. • Marketers try to reduce this discomfort using reinforcement advertising, warranties, and after-sales service. 📌 Example: After buying an iPhone, a man feels unsure if the Samsung model was better, so Apple sends a reassuring email.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide starts a new theory topic. It comes right after the five attitude change strategies from Slides 20 to 23.
SLIDE 25 — Attribution Theory
(This theory explains how people decide who or what to BLAME or CREDIT for an outcome.)
සිංහල idea
Good/bad result එකකට reason එක කාට/මොකට දාන්නේද කියලා person explain කරන විදිහ.
•
Internal reason = මගේ action/skill.
•
External reason = product/company/situation.
•
Marketer complaint එකේ customer blame product එකට දාලා තියෙනවද බලන්න ඕන.
උදාහරණය: Device fail → 'brand bad' කියනවා vs 'මම wrong use කළා' කියනවා.
Exam මතකය: Why did this happen?
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Attribution Theory

(This theory explains how people decide who or what to BLAME or CREDIT for an outcome.)

DEFINITION Assigning Blame or Credit Attribution theory tries to explain how people decide the cause of an event, whether it is blame or credit. • A person may attribute (assign) the blame or credit to themselves. • A person may instead attribute the blame or credit to someone else. • The main underlying question in this theory is simply: 'Why did this happen?' 📌 Example: A student who fails an exam may blame the hard questions (external) or blame their own lack of study (internal).

SLIDE 2 — Family vs Household
What is the difference between a family and a household?
සිංහල idea
Family කියන්නේ blood/marriage/adoption relation තියෙන people group එක. Household කියන්නේ එක ගෙදර/space එකේ live කරන people; family relation අනිවාර්ය නෑ.
•
All families can be households.
•
Not all households are families.
උදාහරණය: University friends දෙන්නා room share = household; parents + children = family + household.
Exam මතකය: Relation / Just live together.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Family vs Household

What is the difference between a family and a household?

DEFINITION Family = Most important consumer buying group in society. Household = Any group of people living together — but NOT always a family. All families can be called households, but not all households are families.
KEY CONCEPT A family has a relationship (blood, marriage, adoption). A household is just people sharing a home. Marketers care about this difference because buying behaviour differs between family households and non-family households.
📌 Example: Two university students sharing a room in Colombo = Household, NOT a family. Parents + children at home = Family AND a household.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces the unit of study — the Family — as we begin to explore consumer behaviour.
SLIDE 3 — Family — Types
Families come in different forms — all are important in marketing.
සිංහල idea
Marketing එකේ family structure අනුව needs/spending change වෙනවා.
•
Nuclear = parents + children.
•
Extended = grandparents/aunts/uncles ඇතුළු larger family.
•
Married couple = husband + wife.
•
Single parent = one parent + children.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Family — Types of Families

Families come in different forms — all are important in marketing.

DEFINITION Two or more persons related by blood, marriage, or adoption who live together.

Nuclear Family

Parents + children only — the small core family

Mother, father, and their children living under one roof.

This is the most common family type targeted by marketers.

Extended Family

Nuclear family + grandparents/aunts/uncles etc.

Multiple generations living together in one home.

Very common in Sri Lanka — influences group buying decisions.

Married Couple

Husband and wife only — no children yet (or no children at all)

Often have high disposable income — targeted for luxury goods.

Single Parent

One parent raising children alone

Due to divorce, death of spouse, or choice.

Different consumption needs — e.g., more ready-made meals, childcare products.

📌 Example: Sri Lanka: A grandparents + parents + children household = Extended family. McDonald's ads targeting 'mum, dad, and kids' = Nuclear family marketing.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide expands the definition of 'family' from Slide 2, showing the different structures marketers must consider.
SLIDE 4 — The Family Life Cycle
A tool that combines family variables to describe life stages.
සිංහල idea
Marketing එකේ family structure අනුව needs/spending change වෙනවා.
•
Nuclear = parents + children.
•
Extended = grandparents/aunts/uncles ඇතුළු larger family.
•
Married couple = husband + wife.
•
Single parent = one parent + children.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Family Life Cycle

A tool that combines family variables to describe life stages.

DEFINITION A composite variable (combination of many factors) that describes what stage a family is at in life.
KEY CONCEPT — Four Variables Combined: 1. Marital Status — Single, married, divorced, widowed 2. Size of Family — How many people in the family 3. Age of Family Members — Young, middle-aged, elderly 4. Employment Status of the Head — Working, retired, unemployed
EXAM FOCUS Marketers use the Family Life Cycle to predict what products a family needs at each stage of life.
📌 Example: A young married couple with no children (newly employed) = Different buying needs than a retired couple with grown-up children.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces a key marketing tool that uses the family types from Slide 3 to map out life stages.
SLIDE 5 — Family Life Cycle — Stages
Each stage of life has different consumer needs and spending patterns.
සිංහල idea
Marketing එකේ family structure අනුව needs/spending change වෙනවා.
•
Nuclear = parents + children.
•
Extended = grandparents/aunts/uncles ඇතුළු larger family.
•
Married couple = husband + wife.
•
Single parent = one parent + children.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Family Life Cycle — The 5 Stages

Each stage of life has different consumer needs and spending patterns.

StageNameDescriptionConsumer Behaviour
Stage IBachelorhoodYoung single adult living away from parentsSpends on fashion, entertainment, travel, own food
Stage IIHoneymoonersYoung married couple, no children yetSpends on home setup, furniture, holidays, appliances
Stage IIIParenthood / Full NestMarried couple with at least one child at homeSpends on baby products, education, family cars, food
Stage IVPost-Parenthood / Empty NestOlder married couple, children have left homeSpends on health, travel, luxury, investment
Stage VDissolutionOne surviving spouse after the death of their partnerSpends on health care, medical, social support services
📌 Example: Stage II (Honeymooners): A newly married couple in Colombo buys a refrigerator, sofa set, and kitchen equipment from Singer. Stage III (Full Nest): Same couple now buys Milo, diapers, school bags, and family vehicles.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide breaks down the FLC concept from Slide 4 into specific life stages with practical marketing implications.
SLIDE 6 — Non-Traditional FLC Stages
Modern families do not always follow the traditional path — marketers must adapt.
සිංහල idea
හැම family එකම traditional life-cycle path එක follow කරන්නේ නෑ. Single-parent, child-free, remarried, unmarried households වගේ modern family forms වල needs සහ spending වෙනස්; marketer ඒකට adapt වෙන්න ඕන.
Simple English idea: Modern families do not always follow the traditional path — marketers must adapt.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Non-Traditional Family Life Cycle Stages

Modern families do not always follow the traditional path — marketers must adapt.

KEY CONCEPT Society has changed. Many families do not match the 5 traditional stages. Marketers must understand these alternative family structures to sell effectively.
Alternative StageDefinition / Comment
Childless CouplesMore career-oriented women, delayed marriages — increasingly acceptable choice
Couples Who Marry Later in LifeLikely to have fewer or no children
Couples with First Child in Late 30s+Want the best quality — live quality lifestyle with fewer children
Single Parents IHigh divorce rate (~50%) leads to single-parent households
Single Parents IIChild born outside of marriage
Single Parents IIISingle person who adopts a child
Extended FamilyAdult children return home, divorced adult returns home, elderly move in with children, newlyweds live with in-laws
📌 Example: In Sri Lanka, many young professionals delay marriage to focus on careers — a 'Childless Couple' stage that marketers target with travel packages and luxury products.

SLIDE 7 — Consumer Socialization — Definition
How children learn to become consumers — taught by family and society.
සිංහල idea
Children smart consumers වෙන්න skills, knowledge, attitudes, shopping experience learn කරන process එක.
•
Family first major teacher.
•
Media, friends, school, stores/social mediaත් agents.
•
Core beliefs deep; secondary beliefs easier to change.
උදාහරණය: Child parent shopping බලලා price compare කරන්න learn කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Children learn how to be consumers.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Consumer Socialization (Childhood Socialization)

How children learn to become consumers — taught by family and society.

DEFINITION Process by which children acquire the skills, knowledge, and attitudes needed to function as consumers. Also = The process of teaching people to behave in a way acceptable to their society.
KEY CONCEPT Children are not born knowing how to shop or spend money. They LEARN it. This learning happens through family, school, peers, media, and experience. Marketers study this to understand how brand preferences form in childhood.
📌 Example: A child learns to prefer Milo over other drinks because their parents always bought it — this is consumer socialization.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces a new sub-topic: how children become consumers — linked to the family's role from earlier slides.
SLIDE 8 — Consumer Socialization — Core vs Secondary Beliefs
Not all beliefs are equally fixed — some are deep-rooted, others can change.
සිංහල idea

•
Media, friends, school, stores/social mediaත් agents.

උදාහරණය: Child parent shopping බලලා price compare කරන්න learn කරනවා.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Consumer Socialization — Types of Beliefs

Not all beliefs are equally fixed — some are deep-rooted, others can change.

Core Beliefs & Values

Deep, permanent beliefs passed from parents to children

Persistent — hard to change once formed.

Passed from parents to children.

Reinforced by: schools, churches, businesses, and government.

Secondary Beliefs & Values

More flexible beliefs — open to change over time

People's views of themselves, others, organizations, society, nature, and the universe.

These CAN change based on experience, education, and social influences.

EXAM FOCUS Core beliefs = hard to change (e.g., honesty, religion). Secondary beliefs = easier to change (e.g., brand preference, fashion taste).
📌 Example: Core belief: 'Buying locally made products supports our country' (taught by parents). Secondary belief: 'I prefer Samsung' (can change to Apple after seeing ads).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide deepens the socialization concept from Slide 7, explaining the two levels of beliefs that children develop.
SLIDE 9 — Consumer Socialization — What Parents Teach
Parents are the first and most important consumer teachers for children.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Consumer Socialization — What Parents Teach Children

Parents are the first and most important consumer teachers for children.

KEY CONCEPT Parents teach basic values and behaviours consistent with the culture. These lessons include moral and religious principles. Also includes interpersonal skills — how to talk and deal with people. Dress and grooming standards — how to present yourself. Appropriate manners and speech. How to select suitable educational and career goals. Consumption skills — spending vs saving, how to shop, how to make purchase decisions.
EXAM FOCUS Parents teach BOTH life values AND consumer/shopping skills. This is key!
📌 Example: A Sri Lankan mother taking her daughter to Keells Super and explaining 'buy what is on the list, don't ask for extras' is teaching consumer discipline — a consumption skill.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide lists the specific things parents teach in the socialization process discussed in Slides 7 and 8.

Consumer Socialization — Marketing to Young Children

Children are exposed to marketing very early — this is a big opportunity for brands.

KEY CONCEPT Children today are exposed to marketing messages at a very young age. Especially through television advertising and social media. Marketers can build relationships between children and brands very early in life. These early connections often last into adolescence, teenage years, and adulthood.
EXAM FOCUS Brand loyalty formed in childhood often lasts for life — this is why companies like McDonald's use Happy Meals to attract children.
📌 Example: A child who grows up watching Disney ads on YouTube develops strong love for Disney products — this connection lasts into their adult life.

Consumer Socialization — Pre-Adolescents vs Teenagers

Different age groups are influenced by different people when making consumer decisions.

Pre-Adolescents (Young Children)

They copy and learn from parents and older siblings

Observe parents and older siblings closely.

Trust family more than advertising.

Family is the most reliable source of consumer information for them.

Teenagers

Peer influence becomes dominant — friends matter most

Peers (friends) are the most influential group for teenage consumers.

Interestingly, teens sometimes LIKE products more when parents disapprove.

Rebellion against parents can drive brand preference.

📌 Example: Young child: Wants Milo because Ammi buys it. Teenager: Wants AirPods because all friends have them — and because parents say it is too expensive (making it more desirable).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide shows HOW socialization changes as children grow — from family influence to peer influence, building on Slides 7–10.

Socialization Agents

Who are the people and organisations that teach children about consumption?

DEFINITION Socialization Agent = A person or organization involved in the socialization process.
KEY CONCEPT — Mothers as Primary Socialization Agents: Mothers are stronger socialization agents than fathers. Mothers are usually MORE involved in children's daily lives. Mothers control children's exposure to commercial messages (TV, ads). Mothers regulate how children spend money.
📌 Example: An Ammi who limits her child's screen time on YouTube is controlling exposure to ads — she is acting as a socialization agent protecting the child from excessive commercial influence.

A Simple Model of the Socialization Process

How family and friends influence young people differently — a visual model.

KEY CONCEPT Young persons are influenced by TWO main groups: Other Family Members AND Friends.
Influence SourceType of InfluenceWhat They TeachAge Most Active
Other Family MembersBasic Values & BehaviourMoral/religious principles, interpersonal skills, dress/grooming, manners, educational motivation, career goals, consumer behaviour normsPreadolescent
Friends (Peers)Expressive Attitudes & BehaviourStyle, fashion, fads, what is 'in/out', acceptable consumer behaviourAdolescent to Teenager
📌 Example: As a child: Thathi teaches you to save money (basic values). As a teenager: Friends influence you to buy Nike shoes because they are 'in fashion' (expressive behaviour).

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Parental Styles and Socialization

Different parenting styles create different consumer behaviour in children.

KEY CONCEPT Parents differ in TWO dimensions: How Nurturing they are & How Permissive/Restrictive they are. Combining these creates 4 parenting styles.
StyleNurturing?Permissive?Key Behaviour Towards Advertising & Children
IndulgentVery NurturingPermissiveHighly aware of ads, talk to children about ads, yield to children's requests, give children consumption freedom
NeglectingNot NurturingPermissiveIndifferent to ads, yield to children's requests too easily, grant children consumption freedom
AuthoritativeVery NurturingRestrictiveRestrict ad exposure, suspicious of ads, watch TV with children, discuss consumption, use consumer guidebooks
AuthoritarianNot NurturingRestrictiveChildren must obey rules, restrict environmental influences, very negative towards ads, unlikely to yield to children's requests
📌 Example: An Authoritative parent watches TV commercials WITH their child and then explains 'that toy ad is trying to make you want to buy it — you don't need it.' This is the most educationally effective style.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Family's Supportive Roles

The three main roles a family plays in supporting its members.

Economic Well-Being

The family provides financial support for its members

Family earns income and shares it to meet everyone's needs.

Determines what products the family can afford to buy.

Emotional Support

Family provides love, comfort, and psychological security

Creates a safe environment that affects consumer confidence.

Happy families tend to spend more freely; stressed families may cut spending.

Suitable Family Lifestyles

Family shapes the overall way of living

Includes choices about where to live, what to eat, entertainment, travel.

Lifestyle decisions are major consumer decisions.

📌 Example: A family in Colombo 7 = high economic well-being + certain lifestyle (private school, imported cars, branded clothing). A family in a rural area = different lifestyle and different consumer needs.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide explains what the family DOES for its members — these roles directly affect what the family buys and how much it spends.
SLIDE 16 — Family Decision Making
Who makes buying decisions in a family — and how do children get involved?
සිංහල idea
Husband-wife decisions who has final say කියන pattern අනුව classify කරනවා.
•
Husband-dominated = husband final say.
•
Wife-dominated = wife final say.
•
Joint = both together.
•
Autonomic = one person independently.
උදාහරණය: House buy = joint; personal clothes = autonomic.
Exam මතකය: Who has final say?
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Family Decision Making

Who makes buying decisions in a family — and how do children get involved?

Dynamics of Husband-Wife Decision Making

How married couples divide buying decisions

Husband-Dominated Decisions: Husband has the final say (e.g., car purchase, insurance).

Wife-Dominated Decisions: Wife makes the call (e.g., groceries, household items, children's clothes).

Joint Decisions: Both husband and wife decide together (e.g., buying a house, family holiday).

Autonomic Decisions: Either one decides independently without consulting the other (e.g., personal clothing, personal care items).

Expanding Role of Children in Family Decision Making

Children have growing influence on family purchases

Children today have more influence on family buying decisions than ever before.

They request specific brands, places to eat, and holiday destinations.

📌 Example: Joint Decision: A Sri Lankan couple together deciding to buy a house at a Softlogic Housing Fair. Wife-dominated: Wife choosing which brand of rice or dhal to buy at Keells.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide moves from the family's roles (Slide 15) to HOW families actually make buying decisions together.
SLIDE 17 — Children's Influence on Family Decision Making
Children use different tactics to influence parents when they want something.
සිංහල idea

Children's Influence Strategies on Family Decisions

Children use different tactics to influence parents when they want something.

StrategyWhat the Child DoesExample
PressureDemands, threatens, and tries to intimidate parentsChild cries and refuses to leave the toy section at Arpico until parents buy the toy
ExchangePromises something in exchange for the purchase'I will study hard and get A's if you buy me an iPhone'
RationalUses logical arguments and factual evidence'Thathi, this laptop is faster and actually cheaper — look at the spec sheet'
ConsultationAsks the parent to be involved in the decision'Ammi, can you help me choose which school bag to buy?'
IntegrationGets parent in a good mood first, then makes the requestChild cleans the house, says 'Ammi you look nice today' — then asks for a new PlayStation
📌 Example: A child who first helps with household chores, gives compliments, and THEN asks for a new bicycle is using the Integration strategy — most sophisticated approach.

SLIDE 18 — Children as Three Markets
Children are important to marketers in three different ways.
සිංහල idea
Marketersට child එක today එකේත් future එකේත් important.
•
Primary market = තමන්ටම money/spending power තියෙන current buyer.
•
Influence market = parents purchase influence කරනවා.
•
Future market = grow වෙලා future customer වෙනවා.
උදාහරණය: Child snack එක තමන් buy කරනවා + family restaurant choose කරවන්න influence කරනවා + future brand loyal customer වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Buy now / Influence now / Buy later.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Children as Three Markets

Children are important to marketers in three different ways.

Market RoleWho / WhatExamples of Products
Children as InfluencersChildren who request items while shopping with parents or at homeItems for themselves, items for the home, services for the entire family
Children as a Primary MarketChildren who shop independently — with parents or their own moneySweets, toys, clothes, movies, video games
Children as Future ConsumersChildren who plan and dream about future purchases: 'When I grow up I will buy...'Selecting product categories, fashions, styles, and brands for the future
EXAM FOCUS Remember these 3 market roles: Influencers (affect family buying), Primary Market (buy for themselves), Future Consumers (will buy as adults).
📌 Example: Influencer: Child asks parents to go to McDonald's instead of a local restaurant. Primary Market: Child buys bubble gum and chips with pocket money. Future Consumer: Child says 'When I am rich I will buy a BMW.'

SLIDE 19 — Family Buying Roles
Different family members play different roles in the buying process.
සිංහල idea
Purchase process එකේ different family members different roles play කරනවා.
•

•
Gatekeeper = information control.
•
Decider = final choice.
•
Buyer = actually buys.
•
User = uses.
•

උදාහරණය: Child influencer, father decider, mother buyer, whole family users වගේ.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Family Buying Roles

Different family members play different roles in the buying process.

RoleWhat This Person Does
InitiatorThe person who FIRST suggests buying something
InfluencerThe person who influences the final decision
DeciderThe person who makes the FINAL decision on what to buy
BuyerThe person who physically GOES and buys the product
UserThe person who actually USES the product
KEY CONCEPT One person can play MULTIPLE roles. Example: A child can be both the Initiator AND the User. Different family members can be each role for different purchases.
📌 Example: Child (Initiator): 'Ammi, I want that cereal!' → Mother (Influencer): 'It has good nutrition.' → Father (Decider): 'OK, we buy it.' → Mother (Buyer): buys at Keells. → Child (User): eats it.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces the 5 key buying roles — a framework for understanding who does what in a family purchase.
SLIDE 20 — Family Members' Consumption-Related Roles
A more complete picture of roles — from planning to disposal.
සිංහල idea
Family කියන්නේ blood/marriage/adoption relation තියෙන people group එක. Household කියන්නේ එක ගෙදර/space එකේ live කරන people; family relation අනිවාර්ය නෑ.
•
All families can be households.
•
Not all households are families.
උදාහරණය: University friends දෙන්නා room share = household; parents + children = family + household.
Exam මතකය: Relation / Just live together.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Family Members' Full Consumption-Related Roles

A more complete picture of roles — from planning to disposal.

RoleWhat This Person Does
InfluencerInfluences what the family buys through opinions and recommendations
GatekeeperControls the flow of information about products to the family
DeciderMakes the final purchase decision
BuyerActually goes and purchases the product
PreparerPrepares the product for use (e.g., cooks food, assembles furniture)
UserThe person who uses or consumes the product
MaintainerMaintains and repairs the product to keep it working
DisposerDecides when to throw away or replace the product
EXAM FOCUS This extended list adds Gatekeeper, Preparer, Maintainer, and Disposer to the 5 roles from Slide 19. These extra roles are important for exam!
📌 Example: Gatekeeper: Father only allows children to watch certain TV channels, so they only see certain product ads. Maintainer: Son services the family car. Disposer: Mother decides it is time to throw out the old washing machine.

SLIDE 21 — Social Class — Definition
Society is divided into levels — each level has different status and power.
සිංහල idea
Society එක income එකෙන් විතරක් නෙමෙයි occupation, education, status, lifestyle වගේ factors අනුව levels වලට stratify වෙනවා.
•
Same class members similar tastes/behaviour show කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Product, store, media, brand choices වලට class බලපානවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Social Class

Society is divided into levels — each level has different status and power.

DEFINITION Social Class = The division of members of a society into a hierarchy of distinct status classes. Members of each class have either HIGHER or LOWER status than members of other classes.
KEY CONCEPT Think of society as a ladder. Some people are at the top, some in the middle, some at the bottom. Your position on this ladder = your Social Class. Your social class strongly affects what you buy and how you spend.
📌 Example: In Sri Lanka: A wealthy Colombo businessman = Upper Class. A schoolteacher = Middle Class. A daily wage worker = Working/Lower Class. Each buys very differently.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces a completely new topic — Social Class — the second major theme of this handout after Family.
SLIDE 22 — Social Class — Social Stratification
Social class is a permanent division of society based on multiple combined factors.
සිංහල idea
Society එක income එකෙන් විතරක් නෙමෙයි occupation, education, status, lifestyle වගේ factors අනුව levels වලට stratify වෙනවා.
•
Same class members similar tastes/behaviour show කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Product, store, media, brand choices වලට class බලපානවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Social Class (Social Stratification)

Social class is a permanent division of society based on multiple combined factors.

DEFINITION Social Classes = Society's relatively permanent and ordered divisions. Members of each class share similar values, interests, and behaviors. Measured by a combination of: Occupation, Income, Education, Wealth, and other variables.
KEY CONCEPT — Social Stratification: Stratification = Arranging into layers (like strata of rock). This is NOT just about money — education, job type, and values all count. People in the same social class tend to have similar consumer behaviour.
📌 Example: A doctor, a lawyer, and a senior engineer all earn high incomes AND have high education — they likely share similar consumption habits (private schools for kids, luxury cars, foreign holidays) = Same social class.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide gives a more detailed academic definition of Social Class introduced in Slide 21.
SLIDE 23 — Social Class — Characteristics (Behaviour)
How social class shows up in how people act and what they buy.
සිංහල idea
Society එක income එකෙන් විතරක් නෙමෙයි occupation, education, status, lifestyle වගේ factors අනුව levels වලට stratify වෙනවා.
•
Same class members similar tastes/behaviour show කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Product, store, media, brand choices වලට class බලපානවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Social Class — Characteristics (Behavioural)

How social class shows up in how people act and what they buy.

People within the same class behave more similarly to each other.

They differ in dress, speech patterns, recreational preferences, and many other characteristics.

People are perceived as occupying inferior or superior positions according to their social class.

Social class is indicated by a cluster of variables: occupation, income, wealth, education, and value orientation.

Individuals CAN move up or down the social-class ladder during their lifetimes — it is not totally fixed.

EXAM FOCUS Social class affects behaviour AND perception. You are judged by others based on your class signals (clothing, speech, car, etc.).
📌 Example: Upper class person in Sri Lanka: Speaks English fluently, drives a Benz, dresses in branded clothing. Working class: Travels by bus, speaks mostly Sinhala/Tamil, shops at local markets. These are visible class signals.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide moves from the definition of social class (Slides 21–22) to how it SHOWS in real behaviour.
SLIDE 24 — Social Class — Characteristics (Products Affected)
Social class influences purchasing decisions across many product categories.
සිංහල idea
Society එක income එකෙන් විතරක් නෙමෙයි occupation, education, status, lifestyle වගේ factors අනුව levels වලට stratify වෙනවා.
•
Same class members similar tastes/behaviour show කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Product, store, media, brand choices වලට class බලපානවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Social Class — Products and Services Affected

Social class influences purchasing decisions across many product categories.

KEY CONCEPT — Product Categories Influenced by Social Class: Clothing — type, brand, and quality of clothes worn Jewellery — type of metals and stones, branded vs. unbranded Automobiles — type of car (local, imported, brand, size) Housing — location, size, type (apartment, house, villa) Home Furnishing — furniture brands, design, imported vs. local Leisure Activities — clubs, travel, sports, dining out Media Preferences — which TV channels, newspapers, social media platforms consumed
📌 Example: Upper class Sri Lanka: Reads Daily FT, drives a Land Cruiser, members of a golf club, home in Cinnamon Gardens. Lower class: Reads Lankadeepa, travels by bus, home in a village house.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide gives practical marketing examples of how social class from Slide 23 affects specific product categories.
SLIDE 25 — Social Class Measurement
How do researchers find out which social class a person belongs to?
සිංහල idea
Society එක income එකෙන් විතරක් නෙමෙයි occupation, education, status, lifestyle වගේ factors අනුව levels වලට stratify වෙනවා.
•
Same class members similar tastes/behaviour show කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Product, store, media, brand choices වලට class බලපානවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Social Class Measurement

How do researchers find out which social class a person belongs to?

Subjective Measures

Ask people to rate themselves

Individuals are asked to estimate their own social class positions.

Problem: People may over-estimate or under-estimate their class.

Objective Measures

Use specific socioeconomic questions to categorize people

Individuals answer specific socioeconomic questions.

Answers are used to place them into a class category.

More accurate and scientific than subjective measures.

EXAM FOCUS Know the difference: Subjective = self-rating. Objective = measured by factual answers.
📌 Example: Subjective: Survey asks 'What class do you think you belong to?' Objective: Survey asks 'What is your monthly income? What is your highest education level? What is your job title?' — then uses a formula to classify.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide explains HOW social class is measured in research — following the definition from Slides 21–22.
SLIDE 26 — Objective Measures — Detail
Objective measures can use one variable or a combination of variables.
සිංහල idea
Social standing measure කරන objective approach එකේ income, occupation, education වගේ measurable facts use කරනවා. එක factor එකක් විතරක් බලනවට වඩා combine කරලා බලන එක හොඳයි.
Simple English idea: Objective measures can use one variable or a combination of variables.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Objective Measures of Social Class — Single vs Composite

Objective measures can use one variable or a combination of variables.

TypeMethodVariables Used
Single-Variable IndexUses ONE factor alone to classify social classOccupation OR Education OR Income — just one of these
Composite Variable IndexCombines MULTIPLE factors for a more accurate pictureIndex of Status Characteristics (ISC), Socioeconomic Status Score (SES)
KEY CONCEPT Single-variable: Simple but less accurate. Composite: Complex but more accurate. Index of Status Characteristics (ISC) = combines occupation, source of income, house type, and residential area. Socioeconomic Status Score (SES) = combines occupation, family income, and educational attainment.
📌 Example: Simple example: 'He earns Rs. 500,000/month' = single-variable (income only). But he may have very low education and an unskilled job. The composite measure would give a more accurate class picture.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide breaks down the Objective Measures category from Slide 25 into single vs composite indexes.
SLIDE 27 — Social Classes — The Hierarchy
Society is typically divided into five main class levels.
සිංහල idea
Society එක income එකෙන් විතරක් නෙමෙයි occupation, education, status, lifestyle වගේ factors අනුව levels වලට stratify වෙනවා.
•
Same class members similar tastes/behaviour show කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Product, store, media, brand choices වලට class බලපානවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Social Classes — The Five Levels

Society is typically divided into five main class levels.

LevelClassGeneral Profile
1 (Top)Upper ClassInherited wealth, top professionals, CEOs, politicians. Buy luxury brands. Influence culture and trends.
2Upper-Middle ClassSuccessful professionals, managers, high earners. Buy quality and status brands.
3Middle ClassWhite-collar workers, small business owners. Buy comfortable, reliable products.
4Working ClassBlue-collar workers, skilled labour. Price-conscious. Buy practical, functional products.
5 (Bottom)Lower ClassUnskilled workers, unemployed. Very price sensitive. Buy basic necessities.
EXAM FOCUS Remember the 5 levels from top to bottom: Upper → Upper-Middle → Middle → Working → Lower.
📌 Example: Sri Lanka: Upper Class = wealthy Colombo 7 families with foreign-educated children. Middle Class = government officers and teachers. Working Class = factory workers and farmers. Lower Class = day labourers.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide presents the actual class categories that the measurement tools from Slides 25–26 classify people into.
SLIDE 28 — Social Class Mobility
Can people change their social class? Yes — and it works both ways.
සිංහල idea
Person/family එක class level එක change කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Upward mobility = higher class.
•
Downward mobility = lower class.
උදාහරණය: Education + career growth නිසා upward mobility.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Social Class Mobility

Can people change their social class? Yes — and it works both ways.

Upward Mobility

Moving UP to a higher social class

Getting a better education leads to a better job and higher income.

Starting a successful business can move you up quickly.

Marriage into a higher class can also raise one's social standing.

Downward Mobility

Moving DOWN to a lower social class

Job loss, bankruptcy, or economic crisis can push people down.

Divorce, health problems, or poor financial decisions can reduce class.

Rags to Riches?

Can someone go from very poor to very wealthy?

Yes — it is possible but rare. Examples exist in business, entertainment, and sport.

Education and entrepreneurship are the most common paths upward.

EXAM FOCUS Social class is 'relatively permanent' but NOT fixed forever. Mobility happens in BOTH directions.
📌 Example: Rags to Riches: A young person from a poor Sri Lankan village wins a scholarship, studies abroad, becomes a successful tech entrepreneur = dramatic upward mobility. Downward: A middle-class businessman goes bankrupt = downward mobility.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This final slide completes the Social Class topic by showing that class is not permanent — people can move up or down, which affects their consumption patterns over time.

08. Persuading Consumers

company idea එක customer response එක දක්වා.
Noise

distraction / skip / other thoughts.

save 500 vs do not lose 500.

first vs last.

scare / laugh / attraction / current situation.

SLIDE 1 — Persuading Consumers
This chapter is about how ads try to change our minds and get us to buy things.
සිංහල idea
මේ lesson එකේ main idea එක customerගේ mind/attitude change කරන්න communication සහ advertising appeals කොහොම use කරනවද කියන එක. Humor, fear, message source, media, framing වගේ areas මෙතනට අයිති.
Simple English idea: This chapter is about how ads try to change our minds and get us to buy things.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Persuading Consumers

This chapter is about how ads try to change our minds and get us to buy things.

KEY CONCEPT • Marketers use special tricks in ads to make people want a product. • This chapter looks at humor, fear, sex, and timing as ways to persuade us.
Example: A funny Dialog data-package ad makes you remember the brand and smile.
SLIDE 2 — Humor and Fear Appeals
Ads can make us laugh (humor) or make us worried (fear) to sell products.
සිංහල idea
Bad result එක පෙන්වලා action කරන්න motivate කරන ad.
•
Fear too low = ignore.
•
Fear too high = reject/avoid/boomerang වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Clear solution/what to do කියලා show කරන්න.
උදාහරණය: Smoking damage + quit line/help number.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Humor and Fear Appeals

Ads can make us laugh (humor) or make us worried (fear) to sell products.

DEFINITION Humor A funny ad that makes people smile or laugh. Fear An ad that shows a scary result if you don't buy the product.
Example: McDonald's uses funny jokes; a dental clinic ad shows a scary toothache.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces the two main ideas (humor and fear) that the rest of the chapter explains in detail.
SLIDE 3 — The Communication Model
We need to learn the parts of communication and what can go wrong when a company talks to a customer.
සිංහල idea
Company message එක customerට යන flow එක.
•
Sender = company/source.
•
Encoding = idea words/images වලට හරවනවා.
•
Message = ad/content.
•
Channel = TV/social media/etc.
•
Receiver = consumer.
•
Decoding = consumer meaning ගන්නවා.
•
Feedback = buy/ignore/comment වගේ response.
උදාහරණය: Brand Instagram ad → customer sees → understands → clicks/buys.
Exam මතකය: Sender → Encode → Message → Channel → Receiver → Decode → Feedback.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Goal: Understanding Communication

We need to learn the parts of communication and what can go wrong when a company talks to a customer.

KEY CONCEPT Communication Model (Figure 7.5) This shows how a message travels from a company to a customer. • Sender (Source) — the company that has something to say. • Encodes — turns the idea into words or pictures. • Message — the actual ad or words used. • Channel (Medium) — where the ad appears, like TV or Facebook. • Receiver (Consumer) — the person who sees the ad. • Decodes — the customer understands the message in their own way. • Feedback — the customer reacts, like buying or ignoring the product.
Example: SriLankan Airlines sends an ad (message) through Instagram (channel) to you (receiver).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide explains the basic goal of the chapter, setting up why we study humor and fear appeals next.
SLIDE 4 — Types of Communication
There are different styles of talking to customers — personal or not, formal or casual.
සිංහල idea
Communication personal ද mass ද, official ද casual ද කියලා different.
•
Impersonal = large audience.
•
Interpersonal = one-to-one.
•
Formal = official company communication.
•
Informal = friend/family advice.
උදාහරණය: TV ad = impersonal/formal; friend recommendation = interpersonal/informal.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Communications

There are different styles of talking to customers — personal or not, formal or casual.

DEFINITION Impersonal vs. Interpersonal Impersonal means talking to a big crowd. Interpersonal means talking one-on-one. Formal vs. Informal Formal is official, like a company ad. Informal is casual, like a friend's advice.
Example: A TV ad for Keells is impersonal; your friend telling you about a good tuk-tuk shop is interpersonal.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide moves from the general communication model (Slide 3) to the specific styles companies use to talk to us.
SLIDE 5 — Communication Interference
Sometimes people don't notice or believe ads. These are the reasons why, and how companies fix it.
සිංහල idea
Company message එක customerට යන flow එක.
•
Sender = company/source.
•
Encoding = idea words/images වලට හරවනවා.
•
Message = ad/content.
•
Channel = TV/social media/etc.
•
Receiver = consumer.
•
Decoding = consumer meaning ගන්නවා.
•
Feedback = buy/ignore/comment වගේ response.
උදාහරණය: Brand Instagram ad → customer sees → understands → clicks/buys.
Exam මතකය: Sender → Encode → Message → Channel → Receiver → Decode → Feedback.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Communication Interference

Sometimes people don't notice or believe ads. These are the reasons why, and how companies fix it.

KEY CONCEPT Barriers (Noise) Things that stop a customer from receiving the message properly. • Selective exposure — people choose which ads to pay attention to. • Time shift — people skip ads by recording shows or using apps. • Psychological noise — other thoughts distract the customer's mind.
EXAM FOCUS Overcoming Psychological Noise Ways companies fight distraction to get their message across. • Repetition — show the ad many times so people remember it. • Contrast — make the ad look very different from others around it. • Digital technologies — use apps and data to reach the right person. • Effective positioning and providing value — show real benefits clearly.
Example: Apple repeats its iPhone ad often so you don't forget the new features.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide explains problems that can block the communication process described in Slide 3's model.
SLIDE 6 — Discussion Questions — Informal Sources
Think about how friends and family (informal sources) affect what you buy.
සිංහල idea
Informal source කියන්නේ friend, family, coworker වගේ company-controlled නොවන source එක. Customerට trust වැඩි නම් ඒ advice එක formal ad එකකට වඩා strong influence එකක් වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Simple English idea: Think about how friends and family (informal sources) affect what you buy.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Discussion Questions

Think about how friends and family (informal sources) affect what you buy.

EXAM FOCUS • How have informal sources affected your decision as a consumer? • Which informal sources are the most powerful? Why? When?
Example: A friend's WhatsApp recommendation for a tea shop can be stronger than a TV ad.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This connects to Slide 4's 'informal communication' idea, asking you to think about it personally.
SLIDE 7 — Traditional Media vs. New Media
Old-style media (like TV) and new media (like social apps) work in different ways.
සිංහල idea
Old media සහ digital/new media work වෙන way වෙනස්.
•
Traditional = mostly one-way, broad audience, less personal, harder to measure.
•
New media = interactive, targeted, shareable, measurable, real-time.
උදාහරණය: TV ad vs Instagram/TikTok targeted ad.
Exam මතකය: Broad one-way / targeted two-way.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Comparison

Old-style media (like TV) and new media (like social apps) work in different ways.

Traditional MediaNew Media
Broadcast (to everyone)Narrowcast (to a chosen group)
One-way communicationTwo-way communication
Directed at groupsAddressable to one person
Not customized or interactiveCustomized and interactive
Less accurate, delayed feedbackResponse is measurable
Example: A Dialog TV ad reaches everyone; a Dialog app notification is sent just to you.

Simple English idea: Cable companies use customer data to target ads more closely.

Discussion Question

Cable companies use customer data to target ads more closely.

EXAM FOCUS • How are cable operators making advertisements more addressable? • How do you feel consumers will react to cable operators' use of data to make ads more targeted?
Example: Amazon shows you shoe ads after you searched for shoes online.

SLIDE 9 — Messages
A message is what the company wants to say, and there are steps to plan it.
සිංහල idea
Ad message එක හදද්දි marketer choices කිහිපයක් decide කරනවා.
•
Images + text.
•
Positive/negative framing.
•
One-sided/two-sided message.
•
Information order.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Messages

A message is what the company wants to say, and there are steps to plan it.

DEFINITION Type of Message A message can use words (verbal), pictures/actions (nonverbal), or both together.
KEY CONCEPT Steps for Sponsor: Establish Objectives Before making an ad, the sponsor decides exactly what the ad should achieve. • Create awareness — let people know the product exists. • Promote sales — push people to buy right now. • Encourage/discourage practices — change a habit, like quit smoking. • Attract patronage — get customers to keep coming back. • Reduce dissonance — make buyers feel good about their choice. • Create goodwill/favorable image — build a positive brand reputation. • Select medium — choose where the ad will appear. • Design (encode) message — create the actual words and images.
Example: A Disney ad's objective is to create awareness for a new movie release.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide returns to the 'sender encodes a message' step from the communication model in Slide 3.
SLIDE 10 — Message Decisions
Marketers must choose exactly how to build and order their message.
සිංහල idea
Ad message එක හදද්දි marketer choices කිහිපයක් decide කරනවා.
•
Images + text.
•
Positive/negative framing.
•
One-sided/two-sided message.
•
Information order.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Message Decisions

Marketers must choose exactly how to build and order their message.

KEY CONCEPT • Images and text — choosing pictures and words together. • Message framing — saying the same thing in a positive or negative way. • One-sided vs. two-sided messages — showing only good points, or good and bad. • Order of presentation — deciding what information comes first or last.
Example: A Keells ad shows a fresh fruit photo (image) with the words 'Farm Fresh Today' (text).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide lists the four decision areas that Slides 11-14 will each explain one by one.
SLIDE 11 — Images and Text
Pictures and words work together to make an ad clear and attractive.
සිංහල idea
Ad එකක image attention ගන්නවා; text meaning/detail explain කරනවා. දෙක එකට clear, consistent message එකක් දෙන්න ඕන.
Simple English idea: Pictures and words work together to make an ad clear and attractive.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Images and Text

Pictures and words work together to make an ad clear and attractive.

DEFINITION • A strong image grabs attention faster than words alone. • Text explains details that a picture cannot show, like price or features.
Example: An iPhone ad shows a sleek photo plus the text 'Now with a better camera.'
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide expands on the first bullet point ('Images and text') listed in Slide 10.
SLIDE 12 — Message Framing
The same fact can be said in a way that sounds good (positive) or bad (negative).
සිංහල idea
Same fact එක gain side එකෙන්ද loss side එකෙන්ද කියන විදිහ.
•
Positive frame = customer get/save කරන දේ highlight.
•
Negative frame = lose/miss කරන දේ highlight.
උදාහරණය: 'Save Rs.500' vs 'Don't lose Rs.500'.
Exam මතකය: Same fact, different frame.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Message Framing

The same fact can be said in a way that sounds good (positive) or bad (negative).

EXAM FOCUS • When should a marketer use positive message framing? • When should a marketer use negative message framing?
Example: Positive: 'Save Rs. 500 today.' Negative: 'Don't miss out on Rs. 500 savings.'
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide explains the second bullet ('Message framing') introduced in Slide 10.
SLIDE 13 — One-Sided vs. Two-Sided Messages
A message can show only the good side, or both the good and bad sides.
සිංහල idea
Brand message එක good side විතරක්ද, good + limitation/other sideද කියලා.
•
One-sided = only benefits.
•
Two-sided = benefits + some weakness/other side; knowledgeable audienceට credibility වැඩි වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Good only / Good + other side.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
One- vs. Two-Sided Messages

A message can show only the good side, or both the good and bad sides.

DEFINITION One-Sided Message Only the positive points of a product are shown. Two-Sided Message Both strong points and small weak points are shown, which can feel more honest.
Example: A car ad says 'Great mileage, but slightly smaller trunk' — that is two-sided.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide expands on the third bullet ('one-sided vs. two-sided') from Slide 10.
SLIDE 14 — Order Effects
Where you place information in a message (first or last) changes how well people remember it.
සිංහල idea
Message එකේ first සහ last information memoryට different effect දෙනවා.
•
Primacy effect = first part strong/remembered.
•
Recency effect = last part strong/remembered.
උදාහරණය: Key benefit එක මුලින් දානවා primacy use කරන්න.
Exam මතකය: First / Last.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Order Effects

Where you place information in a message (first or last) changes how well people remember it.

EXAM FOCUS • When should marketers rely on the primacy effect (information placed first)? • When should marketers rely on the recency effect (information placed last)?
Example: A Coca-Cola ad puts its best line at the very start so viewers remember it best.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide covers the last bullet ('order of presentation') from the list in Slide 10.
