SLIDE 16 — Comparative Ads
These are ads that directly compare one brand against another brand.
සිංහල idea
Own brand එක competitor එක්ක direct/indirect compare කරලා difference show කරන ad.
•
Clear evidence important.
•
Too aggressive comparison credibility damage කරන්න පුළුවන්.
උදාහරණය: Battery life competitorට වඩා hours 3ක් වැඩි කියලා compare කරනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Comparative Ads

These are ads that directly compare one brand against another brand.

KEY CONCEPT • Sophistication — how smart or aware the audience is. • Negativity — how much the ad criticizes the other brand. • Gender — men and women may react differently to comparisons. • Promotion vs. prevention-focused — chasing a gain, or avoiding a loss. • Reasonable factual evidence — real proof makes the comparison believable.
Example: A soap powder ad says 'Cleans better than Brand X' with a side-by-side test.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide introduces a new ad type, moving on from the message-decision topics of Slides 10-15.
SLIDE 17 — Fear Appeals
Fear appeals warn people about bad things that can happen if they don't act.
සිංහල idea
Bad result එක පෙන්වලා action කරන්න motivate කරන ad.
•
Fear too low = ignore.
•
Fear too high = reject/avoid/boomerang වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Clear solution/what to do කියලා show කරන්න.
උදාහරණය: Smoking damage + quit line/help number.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Fear Appeals

Fear appeals warn people about bad things that can happen if they don't act.

EXAM FOCUS • When do fear appeals work well? • When are fear appeals unlikely to work?
Example: An anti-smoking ad shows damaged lungs to scare people into quitting.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide returns to 'Fear' from Slide 2's title, now explaining it in much more depth.
SLIDE 18 — Guidelines for Fear Appeals
These are 8 rules marketers should follow when using a fear-based ad.
සිංහල idea
Bad result එක පෙන්වලා action කරන්න motivate කරන ad.
•
Fear too low = ignore.
•
Fear too high = reject/avoid/boomerang වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Clear solution/what to do කියලා show කරන්න.
උදාහරණය: Smoking damage + quit line/help number.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Guidelines for Fear Appeals

These are 8 rules marketers should follow when using a fear-based ad.

#Guideline
1Understand the audience's reaction and previous experiences.
2Beware the boomerang effect (people may ignore or reject the message).
3Remember behavior change is a long and complex process.
4Study the relationship between the action and anxiety felt.
5Determine whether the fear appeal should be rational or emotional.
6Repeat the advertising over the long term for it to work.
7Accept that some people (e.g. addicts) may not respond at all.
8Consider alternative appeals if fear does not fit the product.
Example: SriLankan Airlines safety videos repeat the same warning every flight (Guideline 6).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide gives the practical rules for using the fear appeal explained in Slide 17.

SLIDE 21 — Timeliness Appeals

Timeliness Appeals

Ads change their message depending on the timing, like during a tough economy.

ObjectiveCampaign Theme
Restore confidence in banksHumor: 'We love Chase, and not just because they have a million dollars.'
Make people feel good in down timesHappiness themes: Bank of America 'Bank of opportunity'; Coca-Cola 'Open happiness'.
Attract more sensible consumersShow products as affordable and sensible, not just fancy or status-driven.
Attract nervous real-estate buyersShow simple, friendly scenes like a child at a local market instead of luxury.
'Comfort marketing' — appeal to nostalgiaBring back old jingles and characters people fondly remember.
Encourage cash-short DIY renovatorsPaint ads focus on imagination and creativity, not on the hard work of painting.
Example: During tough times, a Dialog ad might promise 'value for your money' instead of luxury.

SLIDE 22 — Measuring Feedback
Companies must check if their ad actually worked, and feedback can mean different things.
සිංහල idea
Ad එක work උනාද කියලා different outcomes measure කරන්න ඕන.
•
Media exposure = people saw/heard.
•
Persuasion effect = attitude/intention changed.
•
Sales effect = actual purchase/revenue.
උදාහරණය: Click එක exposure/engagement; purchase එක sales effect.
Exam මතකය: Saw / Changed mind / Bought.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Measuring Feedback

Companies must check if their ad actually worked, and feedback can mean different things.

KEY CONCEPT • Interpersonal communications give immediate feedback, like a reply in person.
EXAM FOCUS • How are sales effects different from persuasion effects? • How are persuasion effects different from media exposure effects?
Example: Amazon can measure a 'click' (exposure) separately from an actual 'purchase' (sales effect).

09. Reference Groups & WOM

Other people/groups change what we buy.
family/friends/online/celebs.
Influence
Normative = fit into own group; Comparative = copy admired people.
belong vs admire.
Group type
Membership = can belong; Symbolic = admire but cannot really join.
real group vs dream group.
4 consumption groups

friends / shop together / online / rights.

Expert + trustworthy + believable source works better.

Person people ask before buying.
tech reviewer, beauty expert, friend who knows cars.

REFERENCE GROUPS AND WORD-OF-MOUTH

This is the cover page of the chapter. It tells us the topic of the whole handout.

KEY CONCEPT What this chapter is about This chapter explains how other people around us change what we buy. It talks about family, friends, and online groups. It talks about opinion leaders who give advice. It talks about word-of-mouth, meaning people talking about products.
📌 EXAMPLE: A tuk-tuk driver in Colombo and a manager at Apple both listen to people around them before buying something new.
SLIDE 2 — Family = Most Important Reference Group
This means your family teaches you the most about how to shop and spend money.
සිංහල idea
අපි compare කරන, follow කරන, advice ගන්න group/person එක buying behaviour එකට influence කරනවා.
•
Family most important reference group එකක්.
•
Friends, coworkers, online communities, celebrities වගේ sourcesත් influence කරනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
FAMILY = MOST IMPORTANT REFERENCE GROUP

This means your family teaches you the most about how to shop and spend money.

DEFINITION Consumer Socialization This is the process where family teaches children how to be smart shoppers. Family gives children shopping skills. Family gives children product knowledge. Family gives children attitudes about brands. Family gives children real shopping experience.
📌 EXAMPLE: A child in Sri Lanka learns to bargain at the local market by watching parents shop, just like a child abroad learns to trust Coca-Cola by watching family buy it.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide starts the whole topic. It introduces the first and biggest reference group: family.
SLIDE 3 — Source Credibility
This means how much we believe and trust the person giving us information.
සිංහල idea
Message source එක expert, honest, believable කියලා customerට feel වෙන තරම.
•
Expertise = subject knowledge.
•
Trustworthiness = honest.
•
Believability = convincing.
උදාහරණය: Tech expert phone review එක random strangerට වඩා credible.
Exam මතකය: Know + honest + believable.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
SOURCE CREDIBILITY

This means how much we believe and trust the person giving us information.

DEFINITION Source Credibility This is how strongly a source can convince us, based on its expertise, honesty, and believability. Expertise means the source knows the topic well. Trustworthiness means the source is honest. Believability means people find the source convincing.
📌 EXAMPLE: We trust a Dialog phone review from a tech expert more than from a stranger, just like people trust Apple's own specs sheet over a random blog.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 2 showed family as a trusted source. This slide explains WHY family (and other sources) feel trustworthy — it is called source credibility.
SLIDE 4 — Reference Group Influence
This means how groups push us to think or act in a certain way.
සිංහල idea
අපි compare කරන, follow කරන, advice ගන්න group/person එක buying behaviour එකට influence කරනවා.
•
Family most important reference group එකක්.
•
Friends, coworkers, online communities, celebrities වගේ sourcesත් influence කරනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
REFERENCE GROUP INFLUENCE

This means how groups push us to think or act in a certain way.

KEY CONCEPT Normative Influence This means we copy the norms, values, and behaviour of a group we truly belong to. It comes from groups we naturally belong to, like family. It also comes from peers and our local community. Comparative Influence This means we compare ourselves to people we admire and copy them. We pick people we respect as our benchmark. We copy their values or imitate their behaviour.
DEFINITION Membership Group vs Symbolic Group A membership group is one you belong to or could join. A symbolic group is one you admire but cannot really join. Membership group example: your co-workers at a Keells store. Symbolic group example: famous Hollywood actors.
📌 EXAMPLE: A student in Colombo dresses neatly for Avurudu because family expects it (normative), while a teenager buys Nike shoes because a famous footballer wears them (comparative).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 3 explained why we trust sources. This slide goes deeper and shows the two ways reference groups actually influence us: normative and comparative.
SLIDE 5 — Consumption-Related Reference Groups
These are the four types of groups that affect what we buy.
සිංහල idea
Buying decisionsට common group types.
•
Friendship groups.
•
Shopping groups.
•
Virtual communities.
•
Advocacy groups.
උදාහරණය: Facebook tech group එකෙන් laptop advice ගන්නවා = virtual community.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
CONSUMPTION-RELATED REFERENCE GROUPS

These are the four types of groups that affect what we buy.

KEY CONCEPT Friendship Groups Friends give us company, safety, and honest purchase advice. Friends discuss problems we may not tell family. Friends are a trusted source for buying tips. Shopping Groups People shop together for fun or to feel safer about their choice. Someone with expertise joins to reduce mistakes. A crowd buying something creates 'social proof'. Virtual Communities These are online groups where people share opinions with strangers. People chat with online friends they never met. Sharing hobbies online helps marketers reach fans. Advocacy Groups These groups protect consumer rights. Some form to fix one problem, then close down. Some work for a long time on bigger issues.
📌 EXAMPLE: Choosing a tuk-tuk mechanic because a friend recommended him (friendship group); queuing outside a new Apple Store because the crowd looks excited (shopping group); joining a SriLankan Airlines Facebook group (virtual community); a group pushing McDonald's for clearer food labels (advocacy group).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 4 explained normative and comparative influence in general. This slide lists the actual real-life groups where that influence happens.
SLIDE 6 — Factors Affecting Reference Group Influence — Conformity
This explains what a group must do to actually change our behaviour.
සිංහල idea
අපි compare කරන, follow කරන, advice ගන්න group/person එක buying behaviour එකට influence කරනවා.
•
Family most important reference group එකක්.
•
Friends, coworkers, online communities, celebrities වගේ sourcesත් influence කරනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
FACTORS AFFECTING REFERENCE GROUP INFLUENCE

This explains what a group must do to actually change our behaviour.

DEFINITION Conformity Conformity means changing yourself to match the group so you fit in. (1) Inform – the group must tell members the brand or product exists. (2) Compare – the group gives a chance to compare your thinking with the group's attitude. (3) Influence – the group pushes you to adopt attitudes that match its norms. (4) Legitimize – the group makes it feel normal and okay to use the same product as everyone else.
📌 EXAMPLE: A group of Sri Lankan university friends all decide to use the same mobile wallet app, just like a friend group in the US all switching to iPhones together.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 5 listed the different types of reference groups. This slide explains the exact steps (conformity) a group uses to influence its own members.
SLIDE 7 — Factors Affecting Reference Group Influence — Continued
These are three more reasons a group's influence can be strong or weak.
සිංහල idea
අපි compare කරන, follow කරන, advice ගන්න group/person එක buying behaviour එකට influence කරනවා.
•
Family most important reference group එකක්.
•
Friends, coworkers, online communities, celebrities වගේ sourcesත් influence කරනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
FACTORS AFFECTING REFERENCE GROUP INFLUENCE (CONTINUED)

These are three more reasons a group's influence can be strong or weak.

KEY CONCEPT Group Power and Expertise If a group has power or skill, we copy it to get approval or avoid pressure. Power-based groups change behaviour, but not always real attitudes. Relevant Information and Experience People with their own experience trust themselves more; people with none ask others. Little experience means more reliance on other people's advice. Product Conspicuousness This means how visible or noticeable a product is to other people. Very visible, status products are bought with others' opinions in mind.
📌 EXAMPLE: A new employee copies a senior manager's branded watch to gain respect (group power); a first-time rice cooker buyer asks the shop staff for help (experience); choosing a visible car like a Toyota Prius versus invisible items like toothpaste (conspicuousness).
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 6 gave the first factor (conformity). This slide continues the SAME list of factors with three more: power, experience, and visibility.
SLIDE 8 — Discussion Question — Personality
Which personality traits make a person more or less open to group influence?
සිංහල idea
Group influence හැමෝටම එක වගේ නෑ. Personality අනුව සමහර අය othersගේ opinion ඉක්මනින් follow කරනවා; සමහර අය independent නිසා influence අඩු.
Simple English idea: Which personality traits make a person more or less open to group influence?
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
DISCUSSION QUESTION

Which personality traits make a person more or less open to group influence?

EXAM FOCUS Personality and Group Influence Some personality types are easily influenced by groups, and some are not. Compliant, conforming, and other-directed people accept group influence more easily. People who want to be liked follow the group more. Competitive, controlling, inner-directed people resist group influence.
📌 EXAMPLE: A shy new employee copies coworkers' lunch choices to fit in, while a confident manager picks their own favourite kottu shop no matter what others choose.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slides 6 and 7 explained group-side factors. This slide flips the focus to the PERSON'S own personality as a factor.
SLIDE 9 — Conveying Company Credibility
These are the ways a company can prove it is trustworthy.
සිංහල idea
Company itself trustworthy කියලා show කරන්න ways.
•
Good history/reputation.
•
Expert claims/evidence.
•
Guarantees/warranties.
•
Reliable customer service.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
CONVEYING COMPANY CREDIBILITY

These are the ways a company can prove it is trustworthy.

KEY CONCEPT Past performance — a long, safe track record builds trust. Example: SriLankan Airlines' decades of safe flights make travellers trust it. Good reputation — people saying good things about the company. Example: Keells Super is known as a reliable supermarket chain. Product quality — the product consistently works well. Example: iPhones are trusted because they rarely break down. Good service — friendly, helpful staff increase trust. Example: A Dialog customer care agent solving your problem fast. Image of spokespersons — a likeable presenter makes the ad believable. Example: A popular actor advertising Anchor milk powder. Reputation of retailers — a trusted shop selling the item adds safety. Example: A product feels safer bought at Cargills Food City. Media where they advertise — respected media adds trust. Example: An ad on Ada Derana news feels more trustworthy than a random pop-up. Institutional advertising — promoting the company's image, not one product. Example: McDonald's ads about community programs instead of burgers.
📌 EXAMPLE: See the eight separate examples listed above inside each bullet point.

SLIDE 10 — Endorsers and Spokespersons
These are the rules for choosing a celebrity to advertise a product.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් other people/groups, advice, credibility සහ word-of-mouth consumer choice එකට බලපාන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: These are the rules for choosing a celebrity to advertise a product.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
ENDORSERS AND SPOKESPERSONS

These are the rules for choosing a celebrity to advertise a product.

DEFINITION (1) Synergy – the endorser and the product must match well. Example: a cricketer endorsing sports shoes, not perfume. (2) Similar demographics – endorsers similar to the audience feel more credible. Example: a young YouTuber advertising phone cases to teenagers. (3) Trust the marketer – liking the celebrity is not enough; people must trust the company too. Example: liking a singer in a Coca-Cola ad does not guarantee buying it. (4) Matching qualifications – the message must fit the endorser's real skill. Example: a doctor should endorse health products, not cars.
📌 EXAMPLE: See the four separate numbered examples above.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 9 was about company credibility in general. This slide narrows down to ONE part of that: choosing the right celebrity endorser.
SLIDE 11 — Celebrity Uses in Advertisements
These are the four different jobs a celebrity can do in an advert.
සිංහල idea
Celebrity ad එකක roles වෙනස් වෙන්න පුළුවන්: product එක ගැන testimony දෙන්න, brand endorse කරන්න, ad එකේ actor/character වගේ act කරන්න, හෝ long-term spokesperson වෙන්න. Main point = celebrity credibility/meaning brand එකට transfer කරනවා.
Simple English idea: These are the four different jobs a celebrity can do in an advert.

CELEBRITY USES IN ADVERTISEMENTS

These are the four different jobs a celebrity can do in an advert.

KEY CONCEPT Celebrity Testimonial The celebrity says the product is good, based on personally using it. Celebrity Endorsement The celebrity appears for the product for a long time, with or without direct use. Celebrity Actor The celebrity plays a character or role in the advert, not themselves. Celebrity Spokesperson The celebrity represents the brand for a long period of time.
📌 EXAMPLE: Testimonial: a singer says she personally loves a Sri Lankan tea brand. Endorsement: a footballer appears in Pepsi ads for years. Actor: an actor plays a 'doctor' role in a toothpaste ad. Spokesperson: a TV host becomes the long-term face of Dialog.

SLIDE 12 — Other Sources of Credibility
These are more people and places that affect how much we trust a message.
සිංහල idea
Message source එක expert, honest, believable කියලා customerට feel වෙන තරම.
•
Expertise = subject knowledge.
•
Trustworthiness = honest.
•
Believability = convincing.
උදාහරණය: Tech expert phone review එක random strangerට වඩා credible.
Exam මතකය: Know + honest + believable.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
OTHER SOURCES CREDIBILITY

These are more people and places that affect how much we trust a message.

KEY CONCEPT Salesperson credibility — honest, eye-contact-making staff seem more trustworthy. Example: a tuk-tuk driver who explains the fare honestly. Vendor credibility — the seller's reputation affects trust in the product. Example: buying electronics from a trusted shop like Softlogic. Message credibility — past experience shapes trust in future messages. Example: if your last Samsung phone worked well, you trust their new ad more. Medium credibility — the place carrying the ad affects trust. Example: an ad in a respected newspaper feels more credible than random spam SMS.
EXAM FOCUS How does time affect source credibility? This is called the sleeper effect. Over time, people forget WHO said something, but remember WHAT was said. A low-credibility source's negative effect fades after about six weeks.
📌 EXAMPLE: Even if you first heard bad news about a shop from an unreliable source, weeks later you may forget the source and only remember the bad news.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 9 talked about company credibility, and Slide 11 about celebrities. This slide adds three MORE sources of trust: salesperson, vendor, and medium.

උදාහරණය: Tech reviewer phone choice influence කරනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
OPINION LEADERS

These are the kinds of advice opinion leaders give to other people.

KEY CONCEPT Information provided — opinion leaders share useful buying advice. Example: a neighbour tells you which rice brand is best. Best brands — they recommend a specific brand. Example: your uncle recommends Anchor milk powder over other brands. Use of brands — they teach how to use a product properly. Example: a friend shows you a new washing machine setting. Where to buy — they know the best place to purchase. Example: a colleague says Majestic City has the cheapest electronics. Important for services — valuable for choosing doctors, lawyers, hairdressers. Example: asking a friend to recommend a good dentist in Colombo. Category specific — they are experts in one category only. Example: a phone expert friend may know nothing about cars. Motivations — reasons they like to give advice. Example: a friend enjoys sharing tech tips because it makes them feel like an expert.
📌 EXAMPLE: See the seven separate examples listed above inside each bullet point.

CHARACTERISTICS OF OPINION LEADERS

These are the four signs that show someone is an opinion leader.

DEFINITION (1) Knowledgeable in the product category — Example: a friend who deeply studies phone specs before every purchase. (2) Self-confident, outgoing, sociable — Example: the talkative neighbour who always starts conversations about new gadgets. (3) Reads special-interest publications and websites — Example: a fan who follows Apple rumour websites closely. (4) Usually the same socioeconomic and age group as receivers — Example: a university student trusts advice from another student, not a much older person.

SLIDE 15 — Measuring Opinion Leadership
These are the tools researchers use to find out who the opinion leaders are.
සිංහල idea
Researchers opinion leader හඳුනාගන්න different methods use කරනවා.
•
Self-designating method.
•
Sociometric method.
•
Key informants.
•
Online influence measures.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
MEASURING OPINION LEADERSHIP

These are the tools researchers use to find out who the opinion leaders are.

KEY CONCEPT Self-designating method — people rate themselves on a survey about how much they influence others. Example: filling a survey asking 'Do people ask you for phone advice?' Sociometric method — researchers track who really talks to whom in a small community. Example: asking everyone in a university hostel who they get restaurant advice from. Key informant — asking one well-known, respected community member. Example: asking a respected village elder who people go to for advice. Klout score — a score that measures a person's online social media influence. Example: a popular Sri Lankan Instagram food blogger with a high influence score.
📌 EXAMPLE: See the four separate examples listed above inside each bullet point.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 14 described the traits of opinion leaders. This slide shows HOW researchers actually measure and find those leaders.
SLIDE 16 — E-WOM (Electronic Word of Mouth)
This means people sharing opinions about products online instead of face-to-face.
සිංහල idea
Word-of-mouth online වෙලා reviews/posts/videos/comments හරහා spread වෙන එක.
•
Tie strength = source එක්ක closeness.
•
Similarity = source මට වගේද.
•
Source credibility = trust/expertise.
උදාහරණය: Verified tech reviewer YouTube video එක e-WOM.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
E-WOM

This means people sharing opinions about products online instead of face-to-face.

KEY CONCEPT Factors Affecting Engagement These three things decide how much we trust an online opinion. Tie strength — how close and frequent the contact is. Example: trusting advice from your best friend more than a stranger online. Similarity — sharing a similar lifestyle or age with the source. Example: trusting a review from someone your own age. Source credibility — how expert or trustworthy the source seems online. Example: trusting a verified tech reviewer's YouTube video.
DEFINITION Social networks — online places where people with similar interests share personal info. Example: a Facebook group for Sri Lankan foodies. Brand communities — a group formed around love for one brand, anywhere in the world. Example: an online group of SriLankan Airlines frequent flyers. Blogs and microblogs — blogs are longer online posts; microblogs are short, like Twitter. Example: a food blogger reviewing a Colombo restaurant.
📌 EXAMPLE: A celebrity tweeting a short opinion about a new McDonald's item is a microblog example of e-WOM.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 13 to 15 were about traditional opinion leaders talking face-to-face. This slide moves the same idea online, into e-WOM.
SLIDE 17 — Stimulating Word of Mouth
These are the ways marketers try to get people talking about their products.
සිංහල idea
Marketers people brand ගැන talk/share කරන්න encourage කරනවා.

Viral marketing.
•
Shareable useful/funny content.
•
Buzz agents/free samples.
•
Brand communities.
උදාහරණය: Funny Dialog clip එක WhatsApp/Facebook share වෙනවා.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
STIMULATING WORD OF MOUTH

These are the ways marketers try to get people talking about their products.

KEY CONCEPT Viral marketing — using social networks to spread a brand message quickly through sharing. Example: a funny Dialog ad shared thousands of times on Facebook. Motivations for forwarding emails — people forward content that is fun or helps others. Example: forwarding a funny WhatsApp video to friends because it is entertaining. Buzz agents — normal consumers who secretly promote products, usually for free samples. Example: a blogger getting free Dilmah tea samples to talk about, like influencers getting free Starbucks drinks to post about.
EXAM FOCUS Why do people share negative rumours about companies? People notice bad news more, and sharing it can protect other people. Negative info feels rarer, so people pay more attention to it. Some people want to warn others or vent their own frustration.
📌 EXAMPLE: A customer warns friends on Facebook after bad service at a shop, spreading the negative word-of-mouth fast.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 16 explained e-WOM in general. This slide shows the exact TOOLS marketers use to actively start word-of-mouth.

This shows the five groups of people, based on how fast they buy new products.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
ADOPTER CATEGORIES

This shows the five groups of people, based on how fast they buy new products.

DEFINITION Innovators (2.5%) — the very first to buy. Example: the first person in your office to buy the newest iPhone. Early Adopters (13.5%) — buy soon after innovators. Example: friends who try a new food delivery app in its first month. Early Majority (34%) — buy after early adopters, want proof it works. Example: people who start using PickMe only after many friends already use it. Late Majority (34%) — wait until most people already use it. Example: someone who finally switches to online banking after almost everyone else does. Laggards (15%) — the very last to adopt, very cautious. Example: a person who still uses a basic phone long after smartphones are common.
📌 EXAMPLE: See the five separate examples listed above inside each bullet point.

සිංහල idea

INNOVATORS

This slide zooms in on the very first group from the last slide: the innovators.

KEY CONCEPT Who are Innovators? They are the earliest buyers of brand-new products, and they accept the risk of things going wrong. They accept the product may not work well at first. They will pay higher prices to be first. They enjoy showing off new gadgets to others.
📌 EXAMPLE: A gadget lover who buys the newest folding phone the day it launches in Colombo, paying extra just to be first.

This slide explains the other groups of buyers, and people who never adopt at all.
සිංහල idea
New product adopt කරන speed එක අනුව people groups.

Early Majority.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
ADOPTER CATEGORIES – NON-INNOVATORS

This slide explains the other groups of buyers, and people who never adopt at all.

KEY CONCEPT Early adopters — buy soon after launch and help others decide. Example: a friend who tries a new food delivery app early and tells everyone. Early majority — buy after early adopters, somewhat cautious. Example: someone waits for good reviews before buying a new rice cooker brand. Late majority — risk-averse, wait until most people already use it. Example: a parent switches to a smartphone only after almost all relatives already have one. Laggards — last to adopt, highly risk-averse. Example: a person who still writes cheques instead of using online banking.
DEFINITION Non-Adopters Not every non-user is the same — some might still become customers, and some never will. Prospective adopters — non-users who could still become customers. Example: someone who hasn't tried online banking yet but is curious about it. Persistent non-adopters — non-users very unlikely to ever adopt. Example: someone who refuses to ever use internet banking no matter what.

10. Culture

Handout එකේ හැම topic එකම + Sinhala idea + full original details
Source: 10(2).docx
Culture
Invisible rules that shape what feels normal and desirable.
අපිට නොපෙනී buying behavior guide කරන එක.
Culture layers
Symbols / heroes / rituals are visible; values are deep inside.
onion එකේ පිටින් දකින දේ vs ඇතුළේ values.
Own vs new culture
Enculturation = own; Acculturation = new culture.
own learn / other adapt.
Ritual
Repeated symbolic action with meaning.
wedding, Avurudu, birthday etc.
Culture changes
Technology, population, wars, foreign customs, media, language etc. change culture.
culture fixed නෙමෙයි.
Hofstede 6
Power distance / individualism / uncertainty avoidance / masculinity / long-term / indulgence.
country cultures compare කරන six dimensions.

SLIDE 2 — Learning Outcomes
These are the four things you must understand after this lesson.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් shared values, beliefs, customs සහ cultural practices customer wants/choices shape කරන විදිහ explain කරනවා.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Learning Outcomes

These are the four things you must understand after this lesson.

KEY CONCEPT 1. What is culture and how it affects buying Culture is like invisible glasses. It changes how we see products. Understand the meaning of culture. See how culture changes what we buy. 📌 Example: Sri Lankans buy rice and curry for lunch because of culture.
KEY CONCEPT 2. Culture as an invisible hand Culture pushes us to like or dislike things, without us noticing it. Culture guides our attitudes. Culture guides our values. Culture guides our daily choices. 📌 Example: Apple fans feel loyal to Apple products because of a shared tech culture.
KEY CONCEPT 3. Culture is learned through language, symbols, rituals Nobody is born knowing culture. We learn it slowly, like learning to walk. Culture is taught by family. Culture is shown using symbols. Culture is passed on through rituals. 📌 Example: A Kandyan wedding ceremony teaches culture through rituals.
KEY CONCEPT 4. Subcultures and their impact A big culture has small groups inside it. Each small group is a subculture. Subcultures are smaller groups inside a big culture. Each subculture can buy differently. 📌 Example: SriLankan Airlines' Buddhist and Muslim passengers get different in-flight meals.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Title Slide. It lists the goals of the whole lesson we are about to study.
SLIDE 3 — Culture
Culture is the shared way of life of a group of people.
සිංහල idea
Culture කියන්නේ society/group එකක share වෙන values, beliefs, norms, customs සහ way of life. මේවා consumerට මොනවද normal/important කියලා quietly guide කරනවා.
•
Born with නෑ; learn කරනවා.
•
Shared by many people.
•
Consumer wants/choices shape කරනවා.
උදාහරණය: Sri Lankan lunch culture නිසා rice-based meals demand වැඩි.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Culture (Meaning of Culture)

Culture is the shared way of life of a group of people.

DEFINITION Definition 1 — Learned values and customs We are not born with culture. Family and school teach it to us slowly. Culture is learned values, beliefs, and habits. It comes from family and important institutions. 📌 Example: A child learns to remove shoes before entering a Sri Lankan home.
DEFINITION Definition 2 — Culture is about the whole society Culture is not about one person. It is about a big group acting the same way. Culture shows the personality of a whole society. It focuses on social behaviour, not one person. 📌 Example: Sri Lankan society values respecting elders in public.
DEFINITION Definition 3 — Culture regulates consumer behaviour Culture is like a rule book. It tells members of a society what to buy and how to act. Culture is a sum of shared beliefs, values, norms and customs. It regulates how members of a society behave as consumers. 📌 Example: Dialog Axiata designs local ad campaigns that match Sri Lankan family values.
DEFINITION Definition 4 — Fundamental determinant of wants Culture decides, at the deepest level, what a person wants to buy. Culture is the most basic cause of a person's wants. Culture shapes behaviour before anything else does. 📌 Example: McDonald's sells a Rice Bowl in Sri Lanka because of local food culture.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Learning Outcomes slide. It now explains outcome 1 in full detail: what culture actually means.
SLIDE 4 — Different Levels of Cultural Norms
Culture works at three different levels, like three layers of a cake.
සිංහල idea
Culture rules/values different depth levels වලින් තියෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Deep values = easily change වෙන්නේ නෑ.
•
Everyday customs/practices = visible and more changeable.
•
Marketer deep value එකට against message දුන්නොත් reject වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Different Levels of Cultural Norms

Culture works at three different levels, like three layers of a cake.

KEY CONCEPT The Supranational Level This level is bigger than one country. It crosses many national borders. Subcultures exist across national or cultural boundaries. It is shared by people from different countries. 📌 Example: Global Muslim consumers share Halal food culture across many countries.
KEY CONCEPT The National Level This is the culture of one whole country. It is called the 'National Character.' It is the core values and customs of one country. It forms the 'National Character.' 📌 Example: Sri Lanka's National Character includes hospitality to guests.
KEY CONCEPT The Group Level This is the smallest level. It is a small group inside a country. It covers subdivisions of a country, like subcultures. It includes influence from reference groups. 📌 Example: University students in Colombo form their own small group culture.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Culture definition slide. It breaks the big idea of culture into three smaller levels.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
The Culture Programs Us... (Geert Hofstede)

Culture works like a computer program inside our mind. It tells us right from wrong.

KEY CONCEPT Right vs Wrong Culture sets an inner compass. It points us toward 'the right thing to do.' Culture tells us what feels right. Culture tells us what feels wrong. 📌 Example: Sri Lankans feel it is wrong to point their feet at elders.
DEFINITION Institutions in a Culture Institutions are the official groups and rules that hold culture together. Traffic rules guide how we interact on the road. Values, norms, laws, and regulations shape daily life. 📌 Example: Traffic police in Colombo enforce road rules that reflect our culture of order.
📌 EXAMPLE A tuk-tuk driver stops for an elderly passenger first — 'it's the right thing to do.'
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Cultural Norms Levels slide. It shows HOW those levels are 'programmed' into a person's mind.

•

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Cultural Values Are Survival Values

Cultural values help a group survive together. They are placed deep in our hearts and minds.

DEFINITION What gets 'programmed' into us Many small shared things join together to build one strong culture. Common values — shared ideas of good and bad. Common motivation — shared reasons to act. Common symbols — shared signs with meaning. Common history — a shared past. Common geography — a shared homeland. Common philosophy — a shared way of thinking. Common language — a shared way of speaking. Common preferences — shared likes. Common priorities — shared list of what matters most. 📌 Example: Sri Lankans share Sinhala and Tamil New Year customs as common rituals.
📌 EXAMPLE The Kandyan dance is a common symbol that gives Sri Lankans a shared identity.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows 'The Culture Programs Us' slide. It lists exactly what gets programmed into our minds.

The Onion Perspective on Culture

Culture is drawn like layers of an onion, with values deep inside and practices on the outside.

KEY CONCEPT Outer Ring — Visible Parts of Culture These are things you can easily see or hear when you visit a new place. Language and communication. Courtesies (polite manners). Rituals and roles. Customs and relationships. Practices and expected behaviors. Thoughts and manners of interacting. 📌 Example: Bowing your head slightly when greeting elders in Sri Lanka.
KEY CONCEPT Inner Layers — Symbols, Heroes, Rituals, Values, Practices As you go deeper into the onion, the culture becomes harder to see but more powerful. Symbols and heroes sit near the outside. Rituals sit in the middle. Values sit at the very center — the hardest to see. 📌 Example: The Sri Lankan flag's lion is a symbol; a national hero like a past king inspires values.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Cultural Values slide. It organizes those shared values into a visual 'onion' picture.
SLIDE 8 — Symbols, Heroes, Rituals, Values
These four words are the building blocks of every culture.
සිංහල idea
Culture build කරන visible/invisible parts.
•
Symbols = words, colours, objects with meaning.
•
Heroes = admired people/models.
•
Rituals = repeated meaningful actions.
•
Values = deep ideas about what is good/important.
උදාහරණය: National flag = symbol; wedding ceremony = ritual.
Exam මතකය: Symbol / Person / Repeated action / Deep belief.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Symbols, Heroes, Rituals, Values (Definitions)

These four words are the building blocks of every culture.

DEFINITION Symbols A symbol is anything — a word, picture, or object — that means something special in a culture. Words, gestures, pictures, or objects. They carry special meaning inside a culture. 📌 Example: The Sri Lankan flag's lion symbol represents courage.
DEFINITION Heroes A hero is a person people admire. The hero shows the culture how to behave well. Can be alive or dead, real or imaginary. They have qualities the culture admires. They act as models for behaviour. 📌 Example: A famous Sri Lankan monk is seen as a hero of wisdom and kindness.
DEFINITION Rituals A ritual is an activity we repeat because it feels important, even if it is not strictly needed. Collective activities done together. Not technically necessary, but socially important. Done for their own sake. 📌 Example: Lighting an oil lamp before a Sri Lankan wedding ceremony.
DEFINITION Values Values are deep feelings about what is good, bad, beautiful, or ugly. We cannot see them directly, but we see them in behaviour. Feelings of good and evil. Feelings of beautiful and ugly. Hard to observe, but shown through behaviour. 📌 Example: Respect for parents is a deep value shown when children care for elderly parents.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Onion picture. It explains each onion layer word — Symbols, Heroes, Rituals, Values — one by one.

Persistence of Cultural Values

This slide shows five features that make cultural values strong and long-lasting.

KEY CONCEPT Five Features of Values These points explain why cultural values do not disappear easily. Values are relatively few in number. Values guide culturally appropriate behaviour. Values are enduring — hard to change. Values are not tied to one object or situation. Values are widely accepted by most of society. 📌 Example: Respect for elders is a value that has stayed strong in Sri Lanka for generations.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Symbols/Heroes/Rituals/Values slide. It focuses only on the 'Values' layer and shows why it lasts so long.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Persistence of Cultural Values — Core vs Secondary Beliefs

Not all beliefs are equally strong. Some are very fixed, some can change more easily.

DEFINITION Core Beliefs and Values Core beliefs are like the roots of a tree. They are passed down and reinforced again and again. Passed on from parents to children. Reinforced by schools, churches, businesses, government. 📌 Example: Honesty is a core value taught by both Sri Lankan families and schools.
DEFINITION Secondary Beliefs and Values Secondary beliefs are more flexible. They can shift as the world changes. More open to change than core beliefs. Include views of self, others, organizations, society, nature, and the universe. 📌 Example: Views on using social media have changed quickly among Sri Lankan youth.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the 'Five Features of Values' slide. It splits values into two types: fixed core beliefs and flexible secondary beliefs.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Persistence — Views of Self, Organizations, and Society

This slide shows three ways people can see themselves and their community.

KEY CONCEPT People's View of Themselves Every person has an inner picture of who they are. This shapes personal choices and self-image. 📌 Example: A young Sri Lankan professional may see themself as ambitious and modern.
KEY CONCEPT People's View of Organizations People also form opinions about companies, schools, and government bodies. Trust or distrust of organizations affects buying choices. 📌 Example: Customers trust Keells supermarkets because of the brand's long history.
KEY CONCEPT People's View of Society People react to their own society in three different ways. (1) Patriots – They defend their society and are proud of it. (2) Reformers – They want to change and improve their society. (3) Malcontents – They feel unhappy and want to leave their society. 📌 Example: A reformer pushes for better recycling rules in their city.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Core vs Secondary beliefs slide. It gives real examples of secondary beliefs: views of self, organizations, and society.
SLIDE 12 — Persistence of Cultural Values (4)
This slide shows how people relate to the natural world and to bigger spiritual questions.

Persistence — Views of Nature and the Universe

This slide shows how people relate to the natural world and to bigger spiritual questions.

KEY CONCEPT People's View of Nature Different cultures feel differently about the power of nature. Some feel ruled by nature. Some feel in harmony with nature. Some seek to master nature. 📌 Example: A Sri Lankan farmer feels 'in harmony' with the monsoon rains for farming.
KEY CONCEPT People's View of the Universe People also think about their place in the wider universe. There is a renewed interest in spirituality worldwide. 📌 Example: Yoga and meditation apps have become popular globally, showing new interest in spirituality.

Cultural Values & American Core Values

This slide lists common cultural values and gives a special list of American core values.

DEFINITION General Cultural Values These are values found across many cultures, including Sri Lanka. Achievement, success, efficiency. Freedom and independence. Material comfort, fitness, and health. Individualism — for example, buying local products instead of imported ones. Hospitality, charity, compassion, empathy. Respect, politeness, loyalty, teamwork. 📌 Example: Buying Sri Lankan Ceylon tea instead of imported tea shows the value of individualism.
KEY CONCEPT American Core Values This is a special named list of values common in American culture. Achievement and success. Activity and efficiency. Practicality and progress. Material comfort and individualism. Freedom and external conformity. Humanitarianism, youthfulness, fitness, and health. 📌 Example: Apple's marketing often highlights 'progress' and 'individualism,' matching American core values.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the 'Views of Nature and Universe' slide. It now lists actual named values, closing the topic of Persistence of Values.

SLIDE 15 — Theoretical Model of Culture's Influence
This model is a flow chart. It shows step by step how culture becomes an action.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
A Theoretical Model of Culture's Influence on Behaviour

This model is a flow chart. It shows step by step how culture becomes an action.

KEY CONCEPT The Flow of the Model Follow the arrows: culture and personality first shape beliefs, then attitudes, and finally real behaviour. Personality Traits and Subjective Culture (regional, ethnic, religious, linguistic, national, professional, organizational, group) start the process. These shape Cognitive Beliefs. Beliefs combine with Practices and Values to shape Attitude and Social Norms. Attitude and Social Norms shape Behavioral Intention. Behavioral Intention leads to actual Behavior. 📌 Example: A Muslim customer's religious subjective culture shapes a belief that pork is forbidden, leading to buying Halal chicken instead.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the intro title slide. It shows the full theory model in one diagram, explaining exactly how culture leads to buying behaviour.
SLIDE 16 — Culture Satisfies Needs

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Culture Satisfies Needs

Culture sets rules about eating: what, when, where, and how to eat.

DEFINITION Food Example Culture decides what food feels 'normal' for each meal of the day. Breakfast: juice and cereal, or rice and curry. Lunch: a sandwich, or rice and curry. Dinner: something hot and healthy, or rice and curry. Snacks: something with quick energy. 📌 Example: Most Sri Lankans eat rice and curry for both lunch and dinner, following local food culture.

The Invisible Hand of Culture

We do not see culture directly, but it silently controls how we view the world.

KEY CONCEPT Cultural Lens Imagine wearing glasses that color everything you see. Culture works the same way — quietly, but always present. Each person sees the world through their own cultural lens. 📌 Example: A tourist may see a temple as art, while a local sees it as sacred, because of different cultural lenses.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Food example slide. It gives a simple summary idea: culture is an invisible hand guiding perception.
SLIDE 18 — Culture Is Learned — Ways of Learning
Nobody is born with culture. We learn it in three different ways.

Culture Is Learned (Formal, Informal, Technical Learning)

Nobody is born with culture. We learn it in three different ways.

DEFINITION Formal Learning This is direct teaching by people older than us. Adults and older siblings teach how to behave. 📌 Example: A father teaches his son how to shave properly.
DEFINITION Informal Learning This happens by copying, without anyone giving direct lessons. A child learns by imitating others — family, friends, or TV heroes. 📌 Example: A young girl copies her mother's makeup routine by watching her.
DEFINITION Technical Learning This is structured teaching in a school or classroom. Teachers instruct about what, how, and why things should be done. 📌 Example: A school teacher explains ironing clothes correctly in a home economics class.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows 'The Invisible Hand of Culture.' It now explains HOW that invisible hand actually teaches us culture.
SLIDE 19 — Culture Is Learned — Enculturation & Acculturation
There are two special words for learning your own culture versus learning a new one.
සිංහල idea
Enculturation = තමන්ගේ own culture එක family/society හරහා learn කරන එක. Acculturation = වෙන culture එකකට ගිහින්/එකට contact වෙලා ඒ culture habits/values learn කරන එක. මතකය: OWN / OTHER.
Simple English idea: There are two special words for learning your own culture versus learning a new one.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Culture Is Learned — Enculturation and Acculturation

There are two special words for learning your own culture versus learning a new one.

DEFINITION Enculturation This is learning your OWN culture, starting from childhood. The learning of one's own culture. Starts from the time we are small children. 📌 Example: A Sri Lankan child learns Sinhala New Year customs from their family.
DEFINITION Acculturation This is learning a NEW or foreign culture, usually later in life. The learning of a new or foreign culture. 📌 Example: A Sri Lankan student studying in the UK learns British table manners.

Culture Is Learned — Language and Symbols

Without shared language and symbols, people cannot understand each other's culture.

KEY CONCEPT Why Language and Symbols Matter Marketers must choose the right symbols so customers understand the message clearly. Without a common language, shared meaning cannot exist. Marketers must choose appropriate symbols in advertising. Marketers can use 'known' symbols to build associations. 📌 Example: The McDonald's golden arches symbol is instantly recognized worldwide.

Culture Is Learned — Rituals

Rituals are repeated symbolic actions that carry cultural meaning through life.

DEFINITION What Is a Ritual? A ritual is not just one action — it is a full series of steps done in order. A ritual is a symbolic activity with a series of steps. Rituals extend across the whole human life cycle. Marketers know that rituals often involve buying products. 📌 Example: A funeral ritual in Sri Lanka often includes flowers and a decorated coffin, both of which are purchased products.

Culture Is Learned — Sharing of Culture

A belief only becomes 'culture' when many people in a society share it.

DEFINITION How Culture Spreads Culture cannot belong to just one person — it must be shared widely to count as culture. A belief, value, or practice must be shared by a large part of society. Culture is transferred through family, schools, houses of worship, mass media, and virtual communities. 📌 Example: TikTok trends spread new shared habits quickly among young Sri Lankans.

Culture Is Dynamic

Culture is always changing because it must keep meeting people's needs.

KEY CONCEPT Factors That Change Culture Big world events and new inventions constantly reshape what a culture values. AI and social media. Technology. Population shifts. Resource shortages. Wars. Changing values. Customs from other countries. Art, music, fashion, food. Language. Entertainment. 📌 Example: The rise of AI apps is changing how Sri Lankan students study today.

The Measurement of Culture

Researchers use three main methods to measure and study culture.

KEY CONCEPT Three Measurement Methods Each method looks at culture from a different angle: media content, real behaviour, or surveys. Content Analysis. Consumer Fieldwork. Value Measurement Survey Instruments. 📌 Example: A researcher studies Dialog TV ads to measure Sri Lankan cultural values.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows 'Culture Is Dynamic.' Since culture keeps changing, researchers need tools to measure it — this slide names those tools.
SLIDE 26 — Content Analysis
This method studies adverts and messages to find hidden cultural values.
සිංහල idea

Simple English idea: This method studies adverts and messages to find hidden cultural values.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Content Analysis

This method studies adverts and messages to find hidden cultural values.

DEFINITION Definition Researchers count and study words or pictures in ads to see what values a society cares about most. A method for systematically analyzing verbal or pictorial communication. Used to determine the prevailing social values of a society. 📌 Example: Counting how many McDonald's ads focus on 'family togetherness.'
EXAM FOCUS Class Activity Try this activity yourself using real TV ads. Collect TV advertisements during the next week. Count how many focus on: Ethnocentrism, Personality, Peace, Health, Achievement. 📌 Example: You may notice many Keells ads focus on 'health.'
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows 'The Measurement of Culture.' It explains the first method in detail: Content Analysis, plus gives you a class activity.
SLIDE 27 — Value Measurement Survey Instruments
These are three famous survey tools used to measure a person's values.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් shared values, beliefs, customs සහ cultural practices customer wants/choices shape කරන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: These are three famous survey tools used to measure a person's values.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Value Measurement Survey Instruments

These are three famous survey tools used to measure a person's values.

DEFINITION Rokeach Value Survey (RVS) This is a long self-answered survey with two sets of values. 18 'terminal' values — personal goals. 18 'instrumental' values — ways of reaching those goals. 📌 Example: A goal like 'a comfortable life' is a terminal value in RVS.
DEFINITION List of Values (LOV) This is a shorter survey than RVS, based on the same idea. Asks consumers to pick their two most important values. From a list of nine values, based on RVS terminal values. 📌 Example: A consumer picks 'self-respect' as one of their top two LOV values.
DEFINITION Values and Lifestyles (VALS) This tool groups people by how they see themselves and what resources they have. Based on two categories: self-definition and resources. 📌 Example: VALS might classify a wealthy young professional as an 'Innovator.'
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows Content Analysis. It explains the third measurement method in full detail: survey instruments like RVS, LOV, and VALS.

A subculture is a smaller group living inside a bigger culture.
සිංහල idea

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Subculture (Definition)

A subculture is a smaller group living inside a bigger culture.

DEFINITION Definition People in a subculture share special experiences that make them a bit different from the wider society. A group of people within a culture with shared value systems. Based on common life experiences and situations. A distinct group that exists as an identifiable segment within a larger society. 📌 Example: University students form a subculture within Sri Lankan society, with their own slang and habits.

SLIDE 31 — Hofstede's Cultural Dimensions — Overview
This is a famous framework used to compare cultures of different countries.
සිංහල idea
Culture rules/values different depth levels වලින් තියෙන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Deep values = easily change වෙන්නේ නෑ.
•
Everyday customs/practices = visible and more changeable.
•
Marketer deep value එකට against message දුන්නොත් reject වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Hofstede's Cultural Dimensions

This is a famous framework used to compare cultures of different countries.

DEFINITION Purpose of the Framework Businesses use this framework to understand how to do business correctly in another country. Used to understand differences in culture across countries. Helps discern how business is done differently across cultures.
KEY CONCEPT The Six Dimensions There are six separate measuring sticks used to compare any two cultures. Power Distance. Collectivism vs. Individualism. Uncertainty Avoidance Index. Femininity vs. Masculinity. Short-Term vs Long-Term Orientation. Restraint vs. Indulgence. 📌 Example: A company like Amazon studies these six dimensions before entering a new country's market.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Subcultural Categories table. It opens a brand-new major topic: Hofstede's Cultural Dimensions, listing all six areas.
SLIDE 32 — Power Distance
This dimension shows how much a society accepts unequal power between people.
සිංහල idea
Society unequal power/status differences accept කරන තරම.
•
High power distance = hierarchy accept වැඩි.
•
Low = equality/participation වැඩි.
Exam මතකය: Power gap accept කරන තරම.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Power Distance Index

This dimension shows how much a society accepts unequal power between people.

DEFINITION Meaning It measures whether people are comfortable with big gaps in power and rank. Considers how much inequality and power difference is tolerated. The extent to which less powerful members accept unequal power distribution.
KEY CONCEPT High Power Distance In these places, bosses are obeyed strongly and questioned rarely. Accepts inequity and power differences. Encourages bureaucracy and shows high respect for rank and authority. People may blindly obey superiors and are less likely to question authority. Companies use centralized decision-making and tall organization structures. 📌 Example: In many Sri Lankan offices, junior staff rarely question a senior manager's decision directly.
KEY CONCEPT Low Power Distance In these places, everyone's voice matters more equally, even junior staff. Encourages flat organizational structures and decentralized decision-making. Uses a participative style of management. Employees are more likely to question their bosses. 📌 Example: In many Scandinavian companies, junior employees openly debate ideas with their managers.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the 'Six Dimensions' overview slide. It explains the first dimension, Power Distance, in full detail.
SLIDE 33 — Individualism vs. Collectivism
This dimension shows whether people focus on themselves or on their group.
සිංහල idea
People self goalsද group/family goalsද first ද කියන cultural difference එක.
•
Individualism = 'me' focus.
•
Collectivism = 'we' focus.
උදාහරණය: Family approval strong market එක collectivist side වැඩි වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: ME / WE.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Individualism vs. Collectivism

This dimension shows whether people focus on themselves or on their group.

DEFINITION Meaning It measures how much a society values personal goals versus group goals. Considers integration into groups and dependence on groups.
KEY CONCEPT Individualism In individualist societies, people look after themselves first. Greater importance placed on personal goals. Self-image is defined as 'I'. Ties between people are loose; everyone looks out for themselves. 📌 Example: In the USA, many young adults move out and live independently early.
KEY CONCEPT Collectivism In collectivist societies, the group's wellbeing comes before personal wants. Greater importance placed on the group's wellbeing. Self-image is defined as 'We'. People are integrated into strong groups; protection is exchanged for loyalty. 📌 Example: In Sri Lanka, extended families often live together and support one another.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Power Distance slide. It moves to the second dimension: Individualism vs. Collectivism.
SLIDE 34 — Uncertainty Avoidance Index
This dimension shows how comfortable a society is with the unknown and with risk.
සිංහල idea
Unknown/risk/unclear situations societyට කොච්චර uncomfortable ද.
•
High = rules, certainty, guarantees කැමති.
•
Low = ambiguity/newness accept වැඩි.
උදාහරණය: High UA market එකේ warranty/reliability messages strong වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Exam මතකය: Unknown එකට බය කොච්චරද?
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Uncertainty Avoidance Index

This dimension shows how comfortable a society is with the unknown and with risk.

DEFINITION Meaning It measures how a society deals with unexpected events and ambiguity. Considers how unknown situations and unexpected events are handled.
KEY CONCEPT High Uncertainty Avoidance These societies feel unsafe with the unknown, so they build strict rules. Low tolerance for uncertainty, ambiguity, and risk-taking. The unknown is minimized through strict rules and regulations. People feel anxious and stressed; they need strict rules. 📌 Example: Many government offices require detailed paperwork to reduce uncertainty.
KEY CONCEPT Low Uncertainty Avoidance These societies feel calm about the unknown and accept more risk. High tolerance for uncertainty, ambiguity, and risk-taking. The unknown is more openly accepted; rules are more relaxed. People feel more peaceful and are ready to take risks. 📌 Example: Tech startups in Silicon Valley often launch products quickly despite unknown outcomes.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows Individualism vs. Collectivism. It explains the third dimension: Uncertainty Avoidance.
SLIDE 35 — Masculinity vs. Femininity
This dimension shows how a society treats achievement, gender roles, and quality of life.
සිංහල idea
Achievement/competition/status vs care/quality-of-life orientation compare කරන dimension එක.
•
Masculinity side = achievement, competition, success.
•
Femininity side = care, relationships, quality of life.
Exam මතකය: Win/status / Care/life quality.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Masculinity vs. Femininity

This dimension shows how a society treats achievement, gender roles, and quality of life.

DEFINITION Meaning It is also called 'tough vs tender' — how much a culture pushes for competition versus caring. Considers preference for achievement, gender-role attitudes, and behaviour.
KEY CONCEPT Masculinity These societies focus strongly on winning, wealth, and clear gender roles. Distinct gender roles. Assertive behaviour. Concentrated on material achievement and wealth-building. 📌 Example: In some corporate cultures, promotions are strongly tied to sales targets and competition.
KEY CONCEPT Femininity These societies focus more on caring, sharing, and quality of everyday life. Fluid, overlapping gender roles. Modest and nurturing behaviour. Concerned with quality of life. 📌 Example: In Nordic countries, both parents commonly share parental leave equally.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Uncertainty Avoidance slide. It explains the fourth dimension: Masculinity vs. Femininity.
SLIDE 36 — Long-Term vs. Short-Term Orientation
This dimension shows whether a society focuses more on the future or the present and past.
සිංහල idea
Future planning/persistence ද tradition/present results ද කියන orientation.
•
Long-term = future, saving, persistence.
•
Short-term = present, tradition, quick results.
Exam මතකය: Future / Now-tradition.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Long-Term Orientation vs. Short-Term Orientation

This dimension shows whether a society focuses more on the future or the present and past.

DEFINITION Meaning It measures a society's overall attitude toward time — patience for the future, or focus on today. Considers the extent to which society views its time horizon. This was the last dimension added to Hofstede's typology.
KEY CONCEPT Long-Term Orientation These societies are willing to wait patiently for a bigger reward later. Focus on the future. Delays short-term success for long-term success. Emphasizes persistence, perseverance, and long-term growth. 📌 Example: Japanese companies often invest heavily in long product-development cycles.
KEY CONCEPT Short-Term Orientation These societies want quick results now, and respect for tradition. Focus on the near future. Delivers short-term success or gratification. Emphasizes quick results and respect for tradition. 📌 Example: Fast-food chains launching limited-time offers appeal to short-term orientation.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows Masculinity vs. Femininity. It explains the fifth dimension: Long-Term vs. Short-Term Orientation.
SLIDE 37 — Indulgence vs. Restraint
This last dimension shows how freely a society allows people to enjoy life and have fun.
සිංහල idea
Enjoyment/fun freely allow කරන culture ද strong social control තියෙන culture ද.
•
Indulgence = enjoyment/free expression.
•
Restraint = control/social rules stronger.
Exam මතකය: Enjoy freely / Control.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Indulgence vs. Restraint

This last dimension shows how freely a society allows people to enjoy life and have fun.

DEFINITION Meaning It measures how much a culture controls or allows the fulfillment of personal desires. Considers how societies control impulses and desires.
KEY CONCEPT Indulgent Cultures These cultures let people enjoy life freely without strong restrictions. Free gratification of basic human drives related to enjoying life. Common in Central and South America and some African countries. 📌 Example: Brazilian carnivals openly celebrate fun, music, and enjoyment of life.
KEY CONCEPT Restraint Cultures These cultures control personal desires more strictly through social norms. Suppresses gratification of needs through strict social norms. Common in most Arabic countries, Slovakia, and Poland — often countries with strong religious emphasis. 📌 Example: In many Middle Eastern countries, public behaviour is guided by strict social and religious norms.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: This slide follows the Long-Term vs Short-Term Orientation slide. It completes all six Hofstede dimensions, ending the lesson.

11. Brand Positioning — Cross Subject

Why this handout matters
Lecturer said this Brand Management handout also connects to Consumer Behavior.
ඒ නිසා main Consumer Behavior notes එක්කම consider කරලා තියෙනවා.
STP
Segment → Target → Position.
split → choose → place in mind.
4 segmentation bases
Geographic / Demographic / Psychographic / Behavioral.
where / who / inner lifestyle / what they do.
Coverage patterns
One segment / selected segments / one product-many groups / many products-one group / full market.
coverage strategy 5.
Targeting levels
Mass → segment → niche → local → individual.
wide ඉඳන් one-person දක්වා.
Positioning core
Frame of reference + POP + POD.
compare set + expected basics + unique reason.
POP vs POD
POP = must-have similarity; POD = meaningful unique advantage.
fit in category / stand out.
Perceptual map
Draw where brands sit in customers’ minds on two factors.
mind map of competitors.
Statement vs slogan vs mantra
Statement = internal full logic; slogan = public short line; mantra = 3–5 word brand heart.

A brand needs a clear place in the customer’s mind.

•
Why choose us?
•

Brand Positioning

A brand needs a clear place in the customer’s mind.

DEFINITION Brand Positioning This topic explains how customers remember and compare brands. Course: MKT 31623 - Brand Management. Lecturer: A. C. Karunaratne. Lecture focus: brand positioning. STP connects segmentation, targeting, and positioning. Positioning guides the final marketing mix.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Dialog can position itself as a reliable digital-life brand.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The visual shows STP moving toward the marketing mix. Each step guides the next step.

SLIDE 2 — Strategic Brand Management Process
Brand management follows four connected stages.
සිංහල idea
Slogan customer-facing short line; Brand Mantra internal 3–5 word brand heart/soul.
•
Slogan = memorable public phrase.
•
Brand mantra = emotional + descriptive + functional essence.
•
Mantra employees/decisions guide කරන්න use වෙනවා.
උදාහරණය: Nike-style public tagline slogan; internal 3–5 word essence mantra.
Exam මතකය: Public line / 3–5 word heart.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Strategic Brand Management Process

Brand management follows four connected stages.

KEY CONCEPT Four Strategic Stages Each stage answers a different brand-management question.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Apple defines its position, markets it, measures loyalty, then expands products.
EXAM FOCUS Process Order Remember the four stages in the correct order. First, define the brand position. Second, create marketing programs. Third, measure brand performance. Fourth, grow and protect brand equity.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Keells first chooses a promise, then promotes, checks, and improves it.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 1 introduced positioning. This slide places positioning inside the full brand process.
SLIDE 3 — Introduction: Why Companies Segment Markets
One company cannot serve every buyer in the same way.
සිංහල idea
හැම buyer කෙනාටම එකම needs නෑ. Company resources limited නිසා similar needs තියෙන groups හඳුනාගෙන best groups serve කරනවා.
•
Wasted marketing effort අඩු වෙනවා.
•
Offer-message fit වැඩි වෙනවා.
•
Company strengthsට match වෙන customers choose කරන්න පුළුවන්.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Introduction: Why Companies Segment Markets

One company cannot serve every buyer in the same way.

KEY CONCEPT Limits of One Offer Customers differ, so one offer cannot satisfy everyone. Companies cannot appeal equally to all buyers. Buyers are numerous and widely scattered. Buyer needs and buying habits are different. Companies have different serving abilities. Each company must choose customers it can serve best.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Keells may serve urban convenience shoppers better than remote wholesale buyers.
EXAM FOCUS Main Reason for Segmentation Segmentation helps match company strengths with customer needs. It reduces wasted marketing effort. It improves product and message fit. It helps companies focus limited resources.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Netflix recommends different content to different viewer groups.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 2 showed positioning as a strategic stage. This slide explains why segmentation begins that stage.
SLIDE 4 — STP Framework
STP helps a company choose customers and create value.
සිංහල idea
Segmentation → Targeting → Positioning කියන්නේ customer choose කරලා mind එකේ clear value හදන strategic flow එක.
•
Segmentation = groups create.
•
Targeting = group choose.
•
Positioning = why choose us කියන meaning mind එකේ හදනවා.
Exam මතකය: Split → Pick → Place.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
STP Framework

STP helps a company choose customers and create value.

KEY CONCEPT Select Customers to Serve The company first divides the market, then chooses groups. Segmentation divides the total market into smaller groups. Targeting selects one or more groups to enter.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: SriLankan Airlines may target business travellers and holiday travellers separately.
KEY CONCEPT Decide a Value Proposition The company then creates and communicates better value. Differentiation creates superior customer value. Positioning places the offer in target customers’ minds. All four activities create value for targeted customers.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: McDonald’s positions fast meals as convenient and familiar.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The diagram connects segmentation and targeting with differentiation and positioning. The centre shows targeted customer value.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 3 explained why markets differ. This slide gives the STP solution.
SLIDE 5 — Market Segmentation: Core Definition
A large mixed market becomes smaller reachable markets.
සිංහල idea
හැම buyer කෙනාටම එකම needs නෑ. Company resources limited නිසා similar needs තියෙන groups හඳුනාගෙන best groups serve කරනවා.
•
Wasted marketing effort අඩු වෙනවා.
•
Offer-message fit වැඩි වෙනවා.
•
Company strengthsට match වෙන customers choose කරන්න පුළුවන්.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Market Segmentation: Core Definition

A large mixed market becomes smaller reachable markets.

DEFINITION Market Segmentation Companies divide a heterogeneous market into smaller groups. A heterogeneous market contains many different customer types. Smaller groups are easier to understand. Companies can reach groups more efficiently. Products can match each group’s unique needs. Services can also match each group’s needs.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Dialog separates prepaid, postpaid, home broadband, and business customers.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 4 introduced segmentation as the first STP step. This slide defines it clearly.
SLIDE 6 — Market Segmentation Explained
Different buyers are grouped by similar characteristics.
සිංහල idea
හැම buyer කෙනාටම එකම needs නෑ. Company resources limited නිසා similar needs තියෙන groups හඳුනාගෙන best groups serve කරනවා.
•
Wasted marketing effort අඩු වෙනවා.

Company strengthsට match වෙන customers choose කරන්න පුළුවන්.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Market Segmentation Explained

Different buyers are grouped by similar characteristics.

KEY CONCEPT From Mixed Buyers to Similar Groups Segmentation changes one mixed crowd into clear customer groups. The starting market contains varied buyers. Similar buyers are placed together. Each group shares useful needs or traits. Separate groups can receive separate offers.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A tea company groups buyers into plain-tea, milk-tea, and herbal-tea users.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The visual shows one mixed group becoming four smaller similar groups. This is the basic segmentation idea.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 5 gave the definition. This slide shows the same idea visually.
SLIDE 7 — Market Segmentation: Detailed Meaning
Segments contain buyers with broadly similar need patterns.
සිංහල idea
හැම buyer කෙනාටම එකම needs නෑ. Company resources limited නිසා similar needs තියෙන groups හඳුනාගෙන best groups serve කරනවා.

•
Company strengthsට match වෙන customers choose කරන්න පුළුවන්.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Market Segmentation: Detailed Meaning

Segments contain buyers with broadly similar need patterns.

DEFINITION Dividing Buyers A varied group becomes smaller groups with similar needs. The group may include current buyers. It may also include potential buyers. People inside one segment show similar need patterns.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A local shop groups regular customers and possible new customers separately.
KEY CONCEPT Distinct Market Groups Different segments may need separate marketing decisions. Segments can have different needs. Segments can have different characteristics. Segments can have different behaviour. Separate products may be required. Separate marketing mixes may be required.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Apple offers standard and Pro iPhones for different buyer needs.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 6 showed grouping visually. This slide explains exactly what makes each group different.
SLIDE 8 — Bases for Segmenting Consumer Markets
Consumer markets can be divided in four main ways.
සිංහල idea
Consumer market divide කරන main bases 4ක්.
•
Geographic = location.
•
Demographic = age, income, gender, occupation etc.
•
Psychographic = lifestyle, personality, values.
•
Behavioral = knowledge, usage, loyalty, response, benefits sought.
උදාහරණය: Toothpaste market එක 'whitening', 'fresh breath', 'sensitive teeth' benefit groups වලට divide කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Where / Who / Mind-life / Behaviour.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Bases for Segmenting Consumer Markets

Consumer markets can be divided in four main ways.

KEY CONCEPT Four Segmentation Bases Each base studies a different customer characteristic.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Amazon uses location, age, interests, and purchase behaviour together.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 7 explained distinct groups. This slide names four common ways to create them.

The market is divided by physical location.

Geographic Segmentation

The market is divided by physical location.

DEFINITION Geographic Segmentation Customers are grouped using geographical units. Units can be nations. Units can be regions or states. Units can be counties. Units can be cities. Location can change needs and buying behaviour.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Keells may promote different products in Colombo and hill-country towns.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 8 listed four bases. This slide explains the first base: geography.
SLIDE 10 — Demographic Segmentation
Customers are grouped by measurable personal characteristics.
සිංහල idea

Demographic Segmentation

Customers are grouped by measurable personal characteristics.

DEFINITION Demographic Variables Demographics describe who the customer is. Age and gender can define groups. Family size and life cycle can define groups. Income and occupation can define groups. Education and religion can define groups. Race, generation, and nationality can define groups.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A bank offers student accounts and senior-citizen accounts.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 9 used location. This slide changes the focus to personal characteristics.
SLIDE 11 — Psychographic Segmentation
Customers are grouped by their inner life and lifestyle.
සිංහල idea
හැම buyer කෙනාටම එකම needs නෑ. Company resources limited නිසා similar needs තියෙන groups හඳුනාගෙන best groups serve කරනවා.

Psychographic Segmentation

Customers are grouped by their inner life and lifestyle.

DEFINITION Psychographic Variables Psychographics explain how people think, live, and express themselves. Social class can create groups. Lifestyle can create groups. Personality traits can create groups. These factors explain deeper buying reasons.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Nike targets active people who value achievement and self-expression.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 10 used visible demographics. This slide studies deeper psychological differences.
SLIDE 12 — Lifestyle
Lifestyle shows how a person spends time, money, and energy.
සිංහල idea
Lifestyle කියන්නේ person time, money, energy spend කරන pattern එක. AIO = Activities, Interests, Opinions.

Psychographic segmentation එකට useful.

Same age/income දෙන්නෙක් lifestyle වෙනස් නම් buying patterns වෙනස් වෙන්න පුළුවන්.

Lifestyle

Lifestyle shows how a person spends time, money, and energy.

DEFINITION Lifestyle Pattern Lifestyle is expressed through activities, interests, opinions, and values. It includes consumption behaviour. It shows everyday living patterns. It reflects time use. It reflects money use. It reflects energy use.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A busy Colombo worker may buy quick meals and delivery services.
KEY CONCEPT Lifestyle Indicators Marketers observe many parts of daily life.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Disney studies family entertainment habits when designing content.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 11 introduced lifestyle. This slide explains its meaning and main indicators.

AIOs study activities, interests, and opinions.
සිංහල idea

Psychographic segmentation එකට useful.
