AIOs and Lifestyle Importance

AIOs study activities, interests, and opinions.

KEY CONCEPT Activities, Interests, and Opinions AIOs give a practical picture of customer lifestyle.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Keells studies shopping activities and food interests.
EXAM FOCUS Why Lifestyle Matters Lifestyle strongly changes buying and product use. It influences products customers buy. It influences brands customers prefer. It influences how customers use products.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: iPhone users may choose accessories that match their digital lifestyle.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 12 described lifestyle indicators. This slide organizes them through AIOs.
SLIDE 14 — VALS Framework
VALS groups consumers by motivation and available resources.
සිංහල idea
Consumers motivation + resources අනුව group කරන psychographic framework එක.
•
Inner motivation differences capture කරනවා.
•
Resources high/low influence choices.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
VALS Framework

VALS groups consumers by motivation and available resources.

KEY CONCEPT Primary Motivation Groups VALS uses ideals, achievement, and self-expression. Thinkers and believers are guided by ideals. Achievers and strivers seek achievement. Experiencers and makers value self-expression.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A car brand may target achievers with status-focused messages.
KEY CONCEPT Resource Levels Resources and innovation affect each consumer group. Innovators have high resources and innovation. Survivors have low resources and innovation. Other groups sit between these extremes.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Premium Apple products often attract resource-rich innovators.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The framework links primary motivation with resource level. Students should know all eight VALS groups.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 13 used AIOs to study lifestyle. This slide shows a formal lifestyle classification system.
SLIDE 15 — Personality
Personality creates stable responses to the consumer environment.

Personality

Personality creates stable responses to the consumer environment.

DEFINITION Consumer Personality Personality means unique and lasting psychological characteristics. It affects consistent responses. It affects long-term responses. It shapes reactions to products and messages.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A confident customer may choose a bold fashion brand.
KEY CONCEPT Personality Traits The slide lists six useful personality traits.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Jeep advertising often appeals to autonomy and confidence.

Behavioural Segmentation

Customers are grouped by knowledge, attitudes, use, or response.

DEFINITION Behavioural Basis This method studies what customers do around a product. It studies product knowledge. It studies customer attitudes. It studies product uses. It studies customer responses.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Netflix groups viewers by watching behaviour.
KEY CONCEPT Main Behavioural Variables Five variables describe different forms of customer behaviour.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Dialog offers loyalty rewards to regular heavy users.

SLIDE 18 — Behavioural Segmentation Process
Customer behaviour can be followed through awareness, trial, loyalty, and usage.
සිංහල idea
හැම buyer කෙනාටම එකම needs නෑ. Company resources limited නිසා similar needs තියෙන groups හඳුනාගෙන best groups serve කරනවා.

Behavioural Segmentation Process

Customer behaviour can be followed through awareness, trial, loyalty, and usage.

KEY CONCEPT Behaviour Stages The tree follows customers from awareness to repeated use. A target customer may be unaware or aware. Aware customers may try or not try. Non-triers may hold negative, neutral, or favourable opinions. Triers may reject, stop, or repeat. Repeaters may switch or become loyal. Loyal users may be light, regular, or heavy.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A customer learns about PickMe, tries it, repeats, then becomes loyal.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The tree shows how marketers classify customers at each behavioural stage. Each branch suggests a different action.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 17 showed one behavioural example. This slide expands behaviour into a full decision path.
SLIDE 19 — Multiple and Geo-demographic Segmentation
Companies can combine several variables for sharper groups.
සිංහල idea
හැම buyer කෙනාටම එකම needs නෑ. Company resources limited නිසා similar needs තියෙන groups හඳුනාගෙන best groups serve කරනවා.

•
Company strengthsට match වෙන customers choose කරන්න පුළුවන්.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Multiple and Geo-demographic Segmentation

Companies can combine several variables for sharper groups.

DEFINITION Multiple Segmentation Several segmentation bases identify smaller, clearer target groups. One variable may be too broad. Combined variables improve group definition. Smaller groups can receive better-matched offers.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A bank targets young, urban, digital, high-income professionals.
DEFINITION Geo-demographic Segmentation This approach combines location with lifestyle patterns. It is a multivariable method. It links neighbourhoods with consumer lifestyles. People in similar areas may share buying patterns.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Keells studies neighbourhood income and shopping habits before opening a store.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 18 followed one behavioural path. This slide combines several segmentation bases.
SLIDE 20 — Bases for Segmentation
Segmentation can use consumer facts, thoughts, and purchase behaviour.
සිංහල idea
Consumer market divide කරන main bases 4ක්.
•
Geographic = location.
•
Demographic = age, income, gender, occupation etc.
•
Psychographic = lifestyle, personality, values.
•
Behavioral = knowledge, usage, loyalty, response, benefits sought.
උදාහරණය: Toothpaste market එක 'whitening', 'fresh breath', 'sensitive teeth' benefit groups වලට divide කරනවා.
Exam මතකය: Where / Who / Mind-life / Behaviour.

Bases for Segmentation

Segmentation can use consumer facts, thoughts, and purchase behaviour.

KEY CONCEPT Four-Part Segmentation Matrix The matrix combines consumer-rooted and consumption-specific information.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Amazon combines age, location, browsing, benefits, and loyalty data.

Substantial/profitable.
•
Accessible/reachable.

Criteria for Effective Targeting

A segment must be useful, reachable, and suitable for the company.

KEY CONCEPT Core Targeting Criteria Five criteria test whether a segment is workable.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A tiny unreachable group is not a useful target for Keells.
EXAM FOCUS Additional Suitability Tests The slide adds four supporting tests. The segment should be identifiable. The segment should be unique. The segment should remain stable. It should fit company objectives and resources.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Apple chooses stable groups that fit its premium brand resources.

SLIDE 22 — Market Targeting
A company chooses the buyer group it will serve.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් market/segment/competitor/brand meaning analyze කරලා target customerගේ mind එකේ clear position එකක් හදන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: A company chooses the buyer group it will serve.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Market Targeting

A company chooses the buyer group it will serve.

DEFINITION Target Market A target market contains buyers with shared needs or characteristics. The buyers have something important in common. The company deliberately chooses them. The company designs value for this group.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: SriLankan Airlines may target travellers seeking regional connections.
KEY CONCEPT Selecting a Target Market Selection follows segmentation and evaluation. First, identify possible segments. Second, evaluate segment attractiveness. Third, choose the best segment or segments.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Netflix selects families, drama fans, and documentary fans as different targets.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 21 tested segment quality. This slide defines the selected target market.
SLIDE 23 — Target Marketing Tasks
Target marketing has three main actions.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් market/segment/competitor/brand meaning analyze කරලා target customerගේ mind එකේ clear position එකක් හදන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: Target marketing has three main actions.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Target Marketing Tasks

Target marketing has three main actions.

KEY CONCEPT Three Target-Marketing Actions The company studies, chooses, and communicates. Identify distinct buyer groups. Profile their needs and preferences. Select one or more segments. Establish distinctive offer benefits. Communicate those benefits clearly.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Dialog profiles youth users, selects them, then promotes affordable data benefits.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 22 defined a target market. This slide explains how target marketing is done.

Five Patterns of Market Coverage

A company can cover products and markets in five ways.

KEY CONCEPT Market-Coverage Patterns The pattern shows which products serve which market segments.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Amazon uses full coverage across many products and customer groups.

SLIDE 25 — Single Segment Concentration
The company focuses on one product-market combination.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් market/segment/competitor/brand meaning analyze කරලා target customerගේ mind එකේ clear position එකක් හදන විදිහ explain කරනවා.

Single Segment Concentration

The company focuses on one product-market combination.

DEFINITION One Focused Segment The company chooses one segment and serves it deeply. Resources are concentrated in one area. Customer knowledge can become very strong. Risk increases if the segment weakens.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A boutique selling only bridal sarees targets one narrow segment.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The highlighted cell shows one product offered to one market segment.

SLIDE 26 — Selective Specialization
The company selects several attractive product-market combinations.
සිංහල idea
Selective Specialization කියන්නේ company එක unrelated/limited segments කිහිපයක් choose කරන targeting pattern එක. Risk එක එක market එකකට depend වෙන එක අඩු කරන්න පුළුවන්.

Selective Specialization

The company selects several attractive product-market combinations.

DEFINITION Several Selected Cells The chosen combinations do not need a close relationship. Each selected segment can be attractive. Risk is spread across several areas. Management becomes more complex.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A company sells school software, hotel software, and hospital software.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The highlighted cells are separate opportunities chosen from the full grid.

SLIDE 27 — Product Specialization
One product is sold to several market segments.
සිංහල idea
Product Specialization කියන්නේ company එක එක product category එකක් focus කරලා different customer segments වලට ඒ product එක sell කරන approach එක.
Simple English idea: One product is sold to several market segments.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Product Specialization

One product is sold to several market segments.

DEFINITION One Product, Many Markets The company becomes an expert in one product category. The same product serves several groups. Product reputation can become strong. A product decline creates major risk.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Microsoft sells Office software to students, firms, and government offices.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The highlighted row shows one product offered across three market segments.

SLIDE 28 — Market Specialization
Many products are sold to one customer market.
සිංහල idea

Market Specialization

Many products are sold to one customer market.

DEFINITION One Market, Many Products The company becomes a specialist for one customer group. Customer knowledge becomes deep. Several needs of one group are served. Risk increases if that market declines.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A supplier sells many products only to hotels.

SLIDE 29 — Full Market Coverage
The company tries to serve all major groups with many products.
සිංහල idea
Full Market Coverage කියන්නේ possible customer segments ගොඩක්/whole market එක serve කරන්න try කරන strategy එක. සාමාන්‍යයෙන් resources වැඩි large firms වලට suitable.
Simple English idea: The company tries to serve all major groups with many products.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Full Market Coverage

The company tries to serve all major groups with many products.

DEFINITION Complete Coverage Large companies may cover the whole market. Many product-market cells are served. Resources and capabilities must be large. Coverage may use one offer or several offers.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Unilever serves many customer groups with many product brands.
KEY CONCEPT Two Full-Coverage Approaches Full coverage can be undifferentiated or differentiated. Undifferentiated marketing uses one broad offer. Differentiated marketing uses separate offers for segments.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Coca-Cola sells a core drink broadly and also offers many variants.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL All cells are highlighted because all major products and markets are covered.

SLIDE 31 — Undifferentiated, Differentiated, and Concentrated Marketing
Firms can use one mix, many mixes, or one focused mix.
සිංහල idea
Customerට greater value දෙන්න company offer එක වෙනස් කරන්න පුළුවන්.
•
Product differentiation.
•
Service differentiation.
•

•
People differentiation.
•
Image differentiation.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Undifferentiated, Differentiated, and Concentrated Marketing

Firms can use one mix, many mixes, or one focused mix.

KEY CONCEPT Three Coverage Approaches Each approach connects the firm to segments differently. Undifferentiated marketing uses one mix for the market. Differentiated marketing uses separate mixes for segments. Concentrated marketing focuses one mix on one segment.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: McDonald’s uses broad offers, local menus, and selected niche products.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The visual compares one broad offer, several segment offers, and one concentrated offer.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 30 listed five strategy levels. This slide compares three major coverage approaches.
SLIDE 32 — Mass Marketing
One offer targets the whole market.
සිංහල idea
Mass Marketing කියන්නේ market එක segments වලට වෙනම treat නොකර almost everyoneට same broad offer/message එකක් දෙන approach එක.
Simple English idea: One offer targets the whole market.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Mass Marketing

One offer targets the whole market.

DEFINITION Mass or Undifferentiated Marketing The firm focuses on common needs, not customer differences. One offer serves the whole market. Mass production supports high volume. Mass distribution makes the offer widely available. Mass promotion uses broad messages. Common needs receive the main attention.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A basic table-salt brand may use one national offer.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 31 compared three approaches. This slide explains undifferentiated marketing.
SLIDE 33 — Segment Marketing
Separate offers are designed for several segments.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් market/segment/competitor/brand meaning analyze කරලා target customerගේ mind එකේ clear position එකක් හදන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: Separate offers are designed for several segments.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Segment Marketing

Separate offers are designed for several segments.

DEFINITION Several Segment Offers Each chosen segment receives an offer matching its needs. A segment contains customers with similar needs. The company designs separate offers. Customer fit is usually stronger. Costs are higher than mass marketing.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Toyota offers small cars, family cars, SUVs, and luxury cars.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 32 used one broad offer. This slide uses different offers for different segments.
SLIDE 34 — Homogeneous Preferences
Most customers want nearly the same benefit combination.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් market/segment/competitor/brand meaning analyze කරලා target customerගේ mind එකේ clear position එකක් හදන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: Most customers want nearly the same benefit combination.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Homogeneous Preferences

Most customers want nearly the same benefit combination.

KEY CONCEPT One Central Preference Customer preferences gather around one central point. Sweetness preferences are similar. Creaminess preferences are similar. One broad offer may satisfy most buyers.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Most buyers may prefer medium sweetness and medium creaminess in yoghurt.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The points form one central cluster. This indicates very similar market preferences.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 33 explained segment marketing. This slide shows a market that may need little segmentation.
SLIDE 35 — Cluster Preferences
Customers form several natural preference groups.
සිංහල idea
Market customer preferences shape 3 main ways.
•
Homogeneous = most people similar wants.
•
Clustered = natural groups/clusters.
•
Diffused = preferences spread out.
Exam මතකය: Same / Groups / Scattered.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Cluster Preferences

Customers form several natural preference groups.

KEY CONCEPT Natural Market Segments Distinct clusters show groups with different benefit combinations. Each cluster has similar internal preferences. Different clusters prefer different combinations. Each cluster can become a target segment.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Ice-cream buyers may prefer creamy, sweet, or lighter choices.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL Three clusters show three natural market segments. Each cluster may need a different offer.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 34 showed one shared preference. This slide shows several clear preference clusters.

Customer preferences are scattered across the market.
සිංහල idea

Diffused Preferences

Customer preferences are scattered across the market.

KEY CONCEPT No Clear Natural Clusters Preferences do not gather into strong groups. Sweetness choices vary widely. Creaminess choices also vary widely. A new brand can choose a central position. Several brands may occupy different positions.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Fashion preferences can be widely scattered across many styles.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The scattered points show no strong natural groups. Positioning choices become more flexible.

SLIDE 37 — Niche Marketing
A company serves a small specialised group inside a large market.
සිංහල idea
Niche Marketing කියන්නේ small, clearly defined customer group එකක් deep focus කරලා එයාලගේ special need එකට strong offer එකක් දෙන strategy එක.

Niche Marketing

A company serves a small specialised group inside a large market.

DEFINITION Niche Market A niche seeks a distinctive combination of benefits. It is a small share of a large market. Customer needs are narrowly defined. Limited resources can be focused. Special market knowledge improves service. Marketing can become more effective and efficient.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A Sri Lankan shop sells only vegan and gluten-free foods.

Local Marketing

Brands and promotions are adapted for local customer groups.

DEFINITION Local Customer Fit The offer changes according to local needs and wants. Different places can have different tastes. Promotions can use local language and culture. Products can match local buying conditions.

සිංහල idea

Simple English idea: Products and marketing are tailored for one customer.

Individual Marketing

Products and marketing are tailored for one customer.

DEFINITION Segments of One Individual marketing is the smallest possible segmentation level. It is also called customized marketing. It is also called one-to-one marketing. Products can match individual needs. Marketing programs can match individual preferences.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Amazon recommends products using one customer’s previous behaviour.
KEY CONCEPT Customization Customization changes the offer for each customer. Customer data helps create the personal offer. Technology makes customization easier. Personal fit can improve satisfaction and loyalty.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Nike By You lets customers design personal shoes.

Differentiation and Positioning

Positioning is a battle for the customer’s mind.

DEFINITION Product Position Product position is the place a product holds in customers’ minds. Customers define the product using important attributes. They compare it with competing products. Position comes from perceptions. Position comes from impressions. Position also comes from feelings.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Volvo is often positioned around safety.
EXAM FOCUS Mind, Not Only Product Strong products still need a clear mental position. Customers choose using remembered meanings. Marketing shapes those meanings. Competitors fight for a preferred mental place.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Apple competes through innovation and premium feelings, not only hardware.

Brand Positioning

The brand must occupy the desired place in target customers’ minds.

DEFINITION Proper Mental Location Positioning guides how customers should think about the brand. The location exists in consumers’ minds. It applies to a chosen market segment. The brand should be understood in the desired way.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: PickMe may seek a position as easy and reliable local transport.
KEY CONCEPT Optimal Competitive Location The firm chooses its brand position relative to competitors. The brand and competitors are mentally compared. The best position maximizes customer value. The best position also supports firm benefit.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Samsung balances technology, choice, and price against Apple.

Clear positioning guides strategy and gives customers a buying reason.

Determining a Frame of Reference

The brand must know its market, competitors, similarities, and differences.

KEY CONCEPT Two Frame Questions A frame of reference defines the competitive setting. Who is the target market? What is the nature of competition? Which points of parity are ideal? Which points of difference are ideal? Associations must be judged against competitors.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: For Uber, the frame may include taxis, ride-hailing, and public transport.

Frame of Reference: Required Knowledge

Marketers need four clear answers before positioning a brand.

EXAM FOCUS Four Questions These answers define the brand’s competitive position. Who is the target consumer? Who are the main competitors? How is the brand similar to competitors? How is the brand different from competitors?
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Dialog studies mobile users, Mobitel, shared services, and unique digital benefits.

SLIDE 45 — Category Membership
Customers should first understand the brand’s category.

Category Membership

Customers should first understand the brand’s category.

DEFINITION Competitive Frame and Category The frame of reference determines category membership. Category membership tells what the brand is. It identifies the main comparison set. Customers need this information before evaluating differences.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Red Bull first belongs to energy drinks before claiming unique energy benefits.
KEY CONCEPT Preferred Communication Order State category membership before the point of difference. First, explain the category. Second, confirm expected category benefits. Third, communicate the unique difference.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Airbnb explains accommodation first, then stresses local-home experiences.

Brand Positioning Guidelines

Optimal positioning requires a frame and strong POPs and PODs.

EXAM FOCUS Two Key Issues Both issues are essential for a complete brand position. Define the competitive frame of reference. Communicate that frame clearly. Choose points of parity. Establish points of parity. Choose points of difference. Establish points of difference.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Apple identifies smartphones, matches basic functions, then stresses ecosystem benefits.

SLIDE 47 — Nature of Competition
Target choice affects which competitors matter.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් market/segment/competitor/brand meaning analyze කරලා target customerගේ mind එකේ clear position එකක් හදන විදිහ explain කරනවා.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Nature of Competition

Target choice affects which competitors matter.

KEY CONCEPT Target Defines Competition A chosen customer need reveals relevant competitors. Different targets compare different alternatives. Competition can include direct and indirect options. The frame should not be too narrow.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Milo competes with milk, soft drinks, water, and other energy choices.
EXAM FOCUS Avoid a Narrow View Hidden competitors can satisfy the same customer need. Direct brand rivals are only one level. Product-category rivals are another level. Need-based alternatives create wider competition.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Netflix competes with YouTube, gaming, cinemas, and social media time.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The visual widens competition from direct brands to every solution for the same need.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 46 required a frame of reference. This slide explains how broadly competition should be defined.
SLIDE 48 — Beverage Category Hierarchy
A broad category can be divided into many smaller subcategories.
සිංහල idea

Beverage Category Hierarchy

A broad category can be divided into many smaller subcategories.

KEY CONCEPT Beverage Category Levels Category levels help define direct and indirect competitors. Beverages divide into water and flavoured drinks. Flavoured drinks divide into non-alcoholic and alcoholic. Non-alcoholic drinks include milk, juices, hot drinks, and soft drinks. Alcoholic drinks include wine, beer, and distilled spirits.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Coca-Cola competes directly with soft drinks and indirectly with water.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The hierarchy shows how broad beverage competition becomes narrower at each lower level.

Soft Drink Category Hierarchy

The soft-drink category contains types, brands, and package forms.

KEY CONCEPT Soft Drink Levels A category can be divided by product type, brand, and pack. Soft drinks divide into cola and non-cola. Cola includes Pepsi and Coke. Non-cola includes orange and unflavoured drinks. Pepsi can appear in several package forms. Examples include 500 ml bottles and 1.5 litre family packs. Examples also include 300 ml cans and 12-can packs.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A Pepsi can competes with Coke cans and other convenient drinks.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The hierarchy narrows from soft drinks to type, brand, and package form.

SLIDE 50 — Points of Parity and Points of Difference
Brands need expected similarities and meaningful unique benefits.
සිංහල idea
Brand එක compare වෙන්නේ කාත් එක්කද, category එක මොකක්ද කියලා define කරන competitive setting එක.

Target customer.
•
Main competitors.
•
Points of parity.
•
Points of difference.
උදාහරණය: Uber frame එක taxis + ride-hailing + some transport alternatives වෙන්න පුළුවන්.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Points of Parity and Points of Difference

Brands need expected similarities and meaningful unique benefits.

DEFINITION Points of Difference (PODs) PODs are strong, positive, and relatively unique brand associations. Customers strongly connect the benefit with the brand. Customers evaluate the benefit positively. Competitors cannot provide it to the same extent.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Apple’s ecosystem can function as a point of difference.
DEFINITION Points of Parity (POPs) POPs are important associations shared with competing brands. They are not always unique. They may be expected category features. They help the brand appear credible in the category.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: All major banks need secure online banking as a point of parity.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 49 showed category and competitors. This slide explains similarity and difference within that frame.
SLIDE 51 — Using POPs and PODs in Positioning
POPs build legitimacy, while PODs build preference.
සිංහල idea
Target customerගේ mind එකේ brand එකට desired clear place/meaning එකක් හදන එක.
•
What are we?
•
Who are we for?
•
Why choose us?
•
How are we same/different from competitors?
උදාහරණය: Volvo historically safety meaning එක්ක strong mental position එකක් හදනවා.
Exam මතකය: Battle for the customer's mind.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Using POPs and PODs in Positioning

POPs build legitimacy, while PODs build preference.

KEY CONCEPT Brand Identity and Image Positioning establishes both POPs and PODs. POPs confirm category membership. PODs create a distinct brand identity. Together they shape the desired brand image.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A hotel needs clean rooms plus a unique service experience.
KEY CONCEPT Competitive Advantage PODs give customers a clear reason to buy. A strong POD creates competitive advantage. Some favourable associations may equal competitors. Equal associations function as POPs in customers’ minds.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Samsung matches smartphone basics and differentiates through model variety.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 50 defined POPs and PODs. This slide explains their strategic roles.
SLIDE 52 — POPs and PODs: Fast Food
Fast-food brands share basics but can differentiate selected benefits.
සිංහල idea
Strong positioning එකට category fit + unique reason දෙකම ඕන.
•
POP = category එකට belong වෙන expected similarities/benefits.
•
POD = competitor same extent දෙන එක අමාරු strong positive unique association.
උදාහරණය: Smartphone POP = calls/apps/camera; POD = unique ecosystem or special camera system.
Exam මතකය: Fit in / Stand out.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
POPs and PODs: Fast Food

Fast-food brands share basics but can differentiate selected benefits.

KEY CONCEPT Fast-Food Points of Parity Competing restaurants must meet common category expectations. Menus may offer similar basic choices. In-store facilities may be similar. Pricing may be broadly similar.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: McDonald’s and Burger King both offer quick meals and seating.
KEY CONCEPT Fast-Food Points of Difference A brand can own selected superior benefits. Higher-quality ingredients can differentiate. More menu flexibility can differentiate. The difference must matter to target customers.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Subway may stress menu flexibility and customisation.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The centre shows shared category features. The outer bubbles show possible unique benefits.

Education providers share core requirements but can add special value.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
POPs and PODs: Education

Education providers share core requirements but can add special value.

KEY CONCEPT Education Points of Parity Core education features are expected by students. Qualifications may be similar. Courses may be similar. Course content may be similar.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Universities must offer recognised programmes and core subject content.
KEY CONCEPT Education Points of Difference Institutions can differentiate the learning experience. Smaller classes can differentiate. Flexible timetables can differentiate. Practical teaching can differentiate.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A weekend degree programme can attract working students.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The centre contains shared education expectations. The outer bubbles contain differentiating benefits.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 52 used fast food. This slide applies the same POP-POD logic to education.
SLIDE 54 — Choosing POPs and PODs
Good associations must satisfy customers and be deliverable by the firm.
සිංහල idea
Strong positioning එකට category fit + unique reason දෙකම ඕන.
•
POP = category එකට belong වෙන expected similarities/benefits.
•
POD = competitor same extent දෙන එක අමාරු strong positive unique association.
උදාහරණය: Smartphone POP = calls/apps/camera; POD = unique ecosystem or special camera system.
Exam මතකය: Fit in / Stand out.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Choosing POPs and PODs

Good associations must satisfy customers and be deliverable by the firm.

KEY CONCEPT Desirability Criteria Customers must want and trust the chosen association.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A shampoo benefit must solve a real hair problem.
KEY CONCEPT Deliverability Criteria The company must be able to create and maintain the association.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Apple’s ecosystem is communicable, profitable, and difficult to copy fully.

SLIDE 56 — Competitive Advantage

Competitive Advantage

A firm wins by giving customers greater value.

DEFINITION Competitive Advantage The firm performs better than competitors on customer value. Greater value can come from lower prices. Greater value can come from more benefits. Extra benefits can justify higher prices. The advantage must matter to consumers.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: IKEA combines affordable prices with practical furniture design.
KEY CONCEPT Possible Value Differences Differences are useful only when customers value them. A difference should solve an important need. The company must deliver it consistently. The difference should support the desired position.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: PickMe can differentiate through easy local booking and payment.

SLIDE 58 — How Many Differences Should Be Promoted?
A brand must choose both the number and type of differences.
සිංහල idea
මේ topic එකෙන් market/segment/competitor/brand meaning analyze කරලා target customerගේ mind එකේ clear position එකක් හදන විදිහ explain කරනවා.
Simple English idea: A brand must choose both the number and type of differences.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
How Many Differences Should Be Promoted?

A brand must choose both the number and type of differences.

KEY CONCEPT One-Benefit Positioning Some marketers recommend one aggressively promoted benefit. One benefit is easy to remember. The message can become very clear. The chosen benefit must be highly important.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Volvo strongly promotes safety.
KEY CONCEPT Multiple-Difference Positioning Other marketers recommend more than one differentiator. Several benefits can create a richer position. The message may become harder to understand. Benefits must fit together without conflict.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Apple promotes design, privacy, performance, and ecosystem benefits.

Selecting an Overall Positioning Strategy

A value proposition combines the benefits used to position a brand.

DEFINITION Value Proposition It is the full mix of promised customer benefits. It explains what customers receive. It connects benefits with the price level. It gives the overall reason to choose the brand.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Keells may promise trusted quality, convenience, and fair value.
KEY CONCEPT Benefit-Price Choices Five strong value positions are shown in the matrix. More for more. More for the same. More for less. The same for less. Less for much less.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A budget airline may offer less service for much less money.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The matrix compares benefit level with price level. Green cells show defensible value propositions.

SLIDE 61 — VRIO Framework
VRIO tests whether resources can create lasting competitive advantage.
සිංහල idea
Difference එක real advantage වෙන්න companyට deliver/protect කරන්න පුළුවන් resource/ability එකක් ඕන.
•
VRIO = Valuable, Rare, Inimitable, Organized.
•
Temporary difference එකක් competitor copy කළාම advantage නැති වෙන්න පුළුවන්.

Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
VRIO Framework

VRIO tests whether resources can create lasting competitive advantage.

KEY CONCEPT Four VRIO Questions A resource is tested in a fixed order. Is the resource valuable? Is the resource rare? Is it difficult to imitate? Is the firm effectively organised?
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Amazon’s logistics network can be tested using all four questions.
KEY CONCEPT Competitive Implications Different answer patterns create different outcomes.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A common website gives parity, but a unique system may give advantage.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 60 selected a value proposition. This slide tests whether internal resources can support it.
SLIDE 62 — Attribute and Benefit Trade-offs
Customers may think two desirable benefits cannot exist together.
සිංහල idea
Positioning හදද්දි every benefit එකම equally strong කරන්න බෑ. Customerට really important attributes/benefits තෝරලා ඒවා strengthen කරද්දි වෙන features එක්ක trade-off එකක් වෙන්න පුළුවන්.
Simple English idea: Customers may think two desirable benefits cannot exist together.
Full handout details — topic/content එක අඩු කරලා නැහැ
Attribute and Benefit Trade-offs

Customers may think two desirable benefits cannot exist together.

KEY CONCEPT Common Trade-offs Brands may need to overcome opposing benefit beliefs.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A healthy snack must prove it can also taste good.
EXAM FOCUS Resolving Trade-offs Strong brands provide evidence that both benefits are possible. Product design can combine the benefits. Communication can explain the combination. Proof improves credibility.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Toyota can communicate power together with safety.

Positioning Strategies: Influencing Factors

Many business and customer signals shape brand positioning.

KEY CONCEPT Inputs to Brand Positioning Positioning is influenced by several connected factors. Media used shapes the message. Product range shapes category meaning. Performance shapes credibility. Prices shape value perception. Distribution networks shape availability. Advertising and promotion shape awareness. Customer profiles shape target fit. Word of mouth shapes trust. Customer experiences shape lasting impressions.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: McDonald’s position comes from price, outlets, advertising, and customer experience.

Perceptual Mapping

A map shows how customers mentally compare competing brands.

DEFINITION Perceptual Map Brands are plotted using two important customer perceptions. One axis shows price. The other axis shows quality. Each point represents a brand. Nearby brands are perceived as similar. Empty areas may show positioning opportunities.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A new phone may target high quality with a medium price.
KEY CONCEPT — RECREATED VISUAL The map places brands by perceived price and quality. The positions reflect customer beliefs, not company claims.

Fast-Food Perceptual Map

Fast-food brands can be compared by healthiness and menu choice.

KEY CONCEPT Map Axes The chart uses two customer-perception dimensions. Horizontal movement changes from junk food to healthy. Vertical movement changes from limited to wide choice. Each restaurant occupies a different perceived position.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Subway appears healthier with a relatively wide choice.
EXAM FOCUS Reading the Map Compare both direction and distance between brands. Close brands are strong perceptual competitors. Distant brands have more different positions. Open spaces may represent market opportunities.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: A healthy brand with limited choice could occupy an empty space.

Developing a Positioning Statement

A positioning statement summarises the target, brand, concept, and difference.

EXAM FOCUS Positioning Statement Formula Use the four-part formula in the correct order. To: state the target segment and need. Our: state the brand. Is: state the main concept or category. That: state the point of difference.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: To busy workers, PickMe is transport that offers easy local booking.
KEY CONCEPT Purpose of the Statement The statement guides internal marketing decisions. It keeps the target clear. It keeps the brand promise clear. It keeps the difference consistent. It supports communication and product decisions.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Apple teams use one clear promise across product and promotion decisions.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 66 showed perceived positions. This slide turns the desired position into a formal sentence.

Slogans

A slogan expresses a brand idea in a short memorable line.

KEY CONCEPT Slogan Examples The slide shows famous slogans connected with brand positions.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Nike’s slogan supports action, confidence, and performance.
EXAM FOCUS Role of a Slogan A slogan should support the brand position. It should be short. It should be easy to remember. It should communicate a meaningful brand idea. It should remain consistent with customer experience.

SLIDE 69 — Core Brand Values

Core Brand Values

Core values summarise the most important meanings in a brand’s mental map.

DEFINITION Core Brand Values They are abstract concepts or phrases describing important brand dimensions. They usually cover five to ten dimensions. They come from the brand mental map. They connect with points of parity. They connect with points of difference. Related associations are grouped under one value label.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Disney core values may include fun, family, imagination, and trust.

SLIDE 70 — Brand Mantra

සිංහල idea

Brand Mantra

A brand mantra captures the brand’s heart and soul in three to five words.

DEFINITION Meaning of a Brand Mantra It expresses the brand essence or core brand promise. It states the heart and soul of the brand. It is similar to brand essence. It is similar to the core brand promise. It uses a short three-to-five-word phrase. It captures brand positioning and brand values.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Disney can use a mantra around fun family entertainment.
EXAM FOCUS Three Mantra Considerations A good mantra must communicate, simplify, and inspire. Communicate the brand’s meaning. Simplify the main brand promise. Inspire employees and partners.
📌 EXAMPLE Example: Nike’s mantra can guide products, staff, and advertising decisions.
🔗 Link to Previous Slide: Slide 69 defined core values. This slide compresses those values into one short internal phrase.
SLIDE 71 — Designing the Brand Mantra
A mantra combines function, description, and emotion.
සිංහල idea
Slogan customer-facing short line; Brand Mantra internal 3–5 word brand heart/soul.
•
Slogan = memorable public phrase.
•
Brand mantra = emotional + descriptive + functional essence.
•
Mantra employees/decisions guide කරන්න use වෙනවා.
උදාහරණය: Nike-style public tagline slogan; internal 3–5 word essence mantra.
